# Appendix C: Answer Key with Full Reasoning

Answers and reasoning for every self-check question, practice question, mock question and case in Chapters 1 to 37, in chapter order. Try each question yourself first; use the key to compare your reasoning, not only your answer. Where a key says "checked by brute force", every possible arrangement or assignment was tried by code and exactly one fits. Where it says "hand-checked" or "judgement", the answer rests on reasoning. Names and numbers in the questions are invented.


## Chapter 1

### Self-check answers

1. **Conclusion:** "lunch will be late". The word "so" points to it; "the canteen is shut today" is the premise.
2. **False.** The conclusion can come first ("Rohan is late because the train was delayed"), in the middle, or last.
3. **Induction.** The premise lists past cases; the conclusion predicts the next one. A late payslip is still possible, so the premises do not guarantee it.

---

### Practice answers

**Q1** 🟢 Argument. Premises: (1) all sales team employees get a phone allowance; (2) Meera is in the sales team. Conclusion: Meera gets a phone allowance ("therefore" signals it). If both premises are true, the conclusion cannot be false: **deduction**.

**Q2** 🟢 Argument. Premise: every time the printer was refilled on a Friday it jammed by Monday; also it was refilled this Friday. Conclusion: it will probably jam by Monday ("so", "probably"). Past cases to a future case, with "probably": **induction**.

**Q3** 🟢 Argument. "Because" introduces the premises, so the conclusion is first. Conclusion: Rohan must be at the airport by 6 am. Premises: (1) the flight leaves at 8 am; (2) check-in closes two hours before departure. Two hours before 8 am is 6 am, so the conclusion is forced: **deduction**.

**Q4** 🟢 Argument. Premises: (1) no visitor may enter the lab without a badge; (2) Anil has no badge. Conclusion: Anil may not enter ("hence"). Forced by the rule: **deduction**.

**Q5** 🟢 **Not an argument.** It lists meal timings. Apply the test: no sentence is being defended and none needs a reason.

**Q6** 🟡 Argument. Conclusion (last, signalled by "It seems"): the calls work. Premises: (1) customers who got a call renewed; (2) most of those who did not get a call let their plans lapse. The word "seems" and the nature of the evidence (observed cases, not a rule) make it **induction**.

**Q7** 🟡 Argument. "Since" introduces premises: (1) the warehouse floods every monsoon; (2) this year's monsoon is forecast to be heavy. Conclusion: we should move the stock upstairs before June. Premises make the action sensible but do not guarantee a flood: **induction**.

**Q8** 🟡 Argument. Premise: 40 randomly chosen claims out of 500 all had receipts. Conclusion: nearly all 500 claims have receipts ("So"). Sample to whole group: **induction**. (The remaining 460 claims could differ.)

**Q9** 🟡 Argument. Premises: (1) either the payment failed or the bank is down; (2) the bank is not down. Conclusion: the payment failed ("So"). If both premises are true, the conclusion must be true (the only remaining option): **deduction**.

**Q10** 🟡 **Not an argument; an explanation.** "Because" is present, but the writer is not trying to convince you that the meeting moved (that is taken as known). The sentence explains why it happened. There is no claim being proved.

**Q11** 🔴 Argument. Stated premise: Priya has worked here eight years. Conclusion: she is eligible for the long-service award. **Unstated premise:** employees with eight years of service (or a threshold that eight years meets) are eligible for the long-service award. With that added, the conclusion is forced: **deduction** (any wording of the hidden rule that covers eight years is acceptable). Without it, the argument has a gap.

**Q12** 🔴 Argument with two steps.
- Premises: (1) a request is valid if filed within 30 days and the item is unopened; (2) every valid request is paid; (3) Sana filed within 30 days; (4) her item is unopened.
- **Intermediate conclusion:** Sana's request is valid. It follows from (1), (3), (4), and then acts as a premise for the next step.
- **Final conclusion:** Sana's request will be paid. It follows from the intermediate conclusion and (2).
- Each step is forced if the premises are true: **deduction**.

**Q13** 🔴 Argument. Conclusion: the vendor is probably not cheaper overall ("So I doubt"). Premise: the speaker tracked their own bills for two weeks and spent more than before. "Many people say the new vendor is cheaper" is **not a premise**: it is the opposing view that the writer pushes back against ("But"). Observations from one person's two weeks make the conclusion plausible, not certain: **induction**.

**Q14** 🔴 **Not an argument.** It is a short report of events in order: crash, restart, report. "So" here means "and then as a result", not "therefore this claim is proved". No statement is offered as a conclusion that needs support.

**Q15** 🔴 Argument. Conclusion: our next hire will probably accept the offer. Premises: (1) the last twelve candidates who got this offer all accepted; (2) the offer is now better than before. There is no clue word at the start; we recognise the conclusion because it is the only sentence that predicts something about the future (and says "probably"), while the others report facts that back it up. Past cases to a future case: **induction**.


---


## Chapter 2

### Self-check answers

1. **No.** It is a command. Commands are carried out or ignored; they are not true or false.
2. **Yes.** It is a claim about a fact in the past; the truth value exists, we just do not know it. Label: statement, unknown to us.
3. **No.** It is an open sentence. It is true when x = 6 and false for other values; without x it has no truth value.

---

### Practice answers

Step used for every item: (1) does it claim something about how things are? (2) if yes, what would settle it? (3) label.

#### Easy

| Q | Label | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| Q1 | **True** | A claim about numbers. 12 + 9 = 21. |
| Q2 | **Not a statement** (command) | "Please switch off" tells someone to act. |
| Q3 | **True** | Mumbai is a city in India. |
| Q4 | **Not a statement** (question) | It asks; yes or no is not applicable. |
| Q5 | **False** | A year has 12 months. Still a statement. |
| Q6 | **Not a statement** (exclamation) | It expresses feeling and claims nothing checkable. |
| Q7 | **True** | By definition, a triangle has three sides. |
| Q8 | **False** | 15 divided by 2 leaves remainder 1, so it is odd. |
| Q9 | **Not a statement** (greeting) | A greeting is not a claim. |
| Q10 | **False** | 7 is greater than 5. |

#### Medium

| Q | Label | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| Q11 | **Not a statement** (opinion) | "Better" depends on taste; no evidence settles it. |
| Q12 | **Not a statement** (open sentence) | True if n = 7, false otherwise; n is not given. |
| Q13 | **Not a statement** (wish or blessing) | It expresses a hope, not a claim. |
| Q14 | **False** | 8 + 7 = 15, not 14. |
| Q15 | **True** | A square has four right angles, so it is a rectangle (with extra equal sides). |
| Q16 | **Not a statement** (question) | It asks. (The answer, "29 is prime", would be a true statement, but that is another sentence.) |
| Q17 | **True** | A compound statement; every whole number is even or odd, and 6 is even. At least one part is true, so the whole "either...or" is true (exactly one is true here, so it is true even if "or" is read as "one but not both"; Chapter 3 settles that convention). |
| Q18 | **Not a statement** (command) | An instruction. |
| Q19 | **True** | January, March, May, July, August, October and December have 31 days. |
| Q20 | **Not a statement** (opinion) | "Best way" is a judgement; people can honestly disagree. |

#### Hard

| Q | Label | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| Q21 | **True** | Both parts must hold: Bilal is in Sales (table) and 55,000 > 50,000. |
| Q22 | **False** | Anita earns 42,000; Chitra earns 48,000. 42,000 is not more than 48,000. |
| Q23 | **Not a statement** (open sentence) | "The salary" does not say whose. True for Bilal and Dev, false for Anita and Chitra, so no single value. |
| Q24 | **True** | "Either...or" is true when at least one part is true; Dev is in IT and not in HR, so it is true under either reading of "or" (Chapter 3 settles that convention). |
| Q25 | **True** | The table has four rows: Anita, Bilal, Chitra, Dev. |
| Q26 | **False** | Sales employees are Bilal (55,000) and Chitra (48,000). 55,000 + 48,000 = 103,000, not 100,000. |
| Q27 | **Not a statement** (question) | It asks; the answer "Dev" would be a statement. |
| Q28 | **True** | Count: This (1) sentence (2) has (3) five (4) words (5). It talks about itself, but the claim is checkable and correct. |
| Q29 | **Statement but unknown to us** | A definite fact about a real event (that customer either had a refund pending or did not), but no information is given. |
| Q30 | **Not a statement** (self-contradicting loop) | If it were true, then what it says (it is false) would hold, so it is false. If it were false, then it would be telling the truth. It cannot be given a consistent value, so we do not count it as a statement. |

## Chapter 3: AND, OR, NOT and Truth Tables

### Self-check answers

1. **F (false).** NOT flips the value, and the office is open (true), so "the office is not open" is false.
2. **Only when both p and q are false.** One true part is enough for OR to be true (and both true also gives true).
3. **8 rows.** Each statement has 2 values, so 2 x 2 x 2 = 8.

### Practice answers

#### Q1 🟢

NOT flips each value. One statement means 2 rows.

| p | NOT p |
|---|---|
| T | F |
| F | T |

**Answer:** NOT p is F when the server is running and T when it is not.

#### Q2 🟢

AND is true only when both are true.

| p | q | p AND q |
|---|---|---|
| T | T | T |
| T | F | F |
| F | T | F |
| F | F | F |

**Answer:** only the first row (T, T) is true: the letter is issued only when probation is complete and HR has signed.

#### Q3 🟢

OR is false only when both are false.

| p | q | p OR q |
|---|---|---|
| T | T | T |
| T | F | T |
| F | T | T |
| F | F | F |

**Answer:** the fine is waived in **3** of the 4 rows (all except F, F). Nobody is both under 12 and over 65, so in real life the T, T row never happens, but the table still lists it because a table covers every combination.

#### Q4 🟢

Compute the helper column NOT q first, then AND with p.

| p | q | NOT q | p AND NOT q |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | F | F |
| T | F | T | T |
| F | T | F | F |
| F | F | T | F |

**Answer:** the order ships in only one row: paid (T) and not on hold (q is F).

#### Q5 🟢

First p OR q, then flip it with NOT.

| p | q | p OR q | NOT (p OR q) |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | F |
| T | F | T | F |
| F | T | T | F |
| F | F | F | T |

**Answer:** "Nothing to drink" shows only in the last row (F, F): no tea and no coffee.

#### Q6 🟢

Keep the first column T for the top half and F for the bottom half, then halve the block size for each next column.

| p | q | r |
|---|---|---|
| T | T | T |
| T | T | F |
| T | F | T |
| T | F | F |
| F | T | T |
| F | T | F |
| F | F | T |
| F | F | F |

**Answer:** the table above has 8 rows. 4 statements need 2 x 2 x 2 x 2 = **16** rows. 5 statements need **32** rows.

#### Q7 🟢


| p | q | p OR q |
|---|---|---|
| T | T | T |
| T | F | T |
| F | T | T |
| F | F | F |

**Answer:** in the row where it rains and the road is closed, "late" is **true**. Some people think it is false because in daily speech "or" often suggests only one of the two; logical OR is inclusive, so both happening still makes the rule true.

#### Q8 🟡


| p | q | p AND q | NOT (p AND q) | NOT p | NOT q | (NOT p) OR (NOT q) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | F | F | F | F |
| T | F | F | T | F | T | T |
| F | T | F | T | T | F | T |
| F | F | F | T | T | T | T |

**Answer:** the columns NOT (p AND q) and (NOT p) OR (NOT q) are identical (F, T, T, T). They are **equivalent** (De Morgan's first law).

#### Q9 🟡


| p | q | p OR q | NOT (p OR q) | NOT p | NOT q | (NOT p) AND (NOT q) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | F | F | F | F |
| T | F | T | F | F | T | F |
| F | T | T | F | T | F | F |
| F | F | F | T | T | T | T |

**Answer:** both result columns read F, F, F, T. They are **equivalent** (De Morgan's second law).

#### Q10 🟡


| p | q | NOT p | (NOT p) AND q |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | F | F |
| T | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | T |
| F | F | T | F |

**Answer:** an interview is booked only when p is F (not rejected) and q is T (shortlisted): the third row only.

#### Q11 🟡

Helper columns: p OR q, then NOT r, then AND.

| p | q | r | p OR q | NOT r | (p OR q) AND NOT r |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | F | F |
| T | T | F | T | T | T |
| T | F | T | T | F | F |
| T | F | F | T | T | T |
| F | T | T | T | F | F |
| F | T | F | T | T | T |
| F | F | T | F | F | F |
| F | F | F | F | T | F |

**Answer:** **3** rows give access (T,T,F; T,F,F; F,T,F). During a lockdown (r is T) nobody gets in.

#### Q12 🟡

Helper columns: NOT p, then q AND r, then OR.

| p | q | r | NOT p | q AND r | (NOT p) OR (q AND r) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | F | T | T |
| T | T | F | F | F | F |
| T | F | T | F | F | F |
| T | F | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | T | T | T |
| F | T | F | T | F | T |
| F | F | T | T | F | T |
| F | F | F | T | F | T |

**Answer:** **5** rows are true: the four rows where p is F, plus the row T, T, T.

#### Q13 🟡


| p | q | r | q OR r | p AND (q OR r) | p AND q | (p AND q) OR r |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | T | T | T |
| T | T | F | T | T | T | T |
| T | F | T | T | T | F | T |
| T | F | F | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | T | F | F | T |
| F | T | F | T | F | F | F |
| F | F | T | T | F | F | T |
| F | F | F | F | F | F | F |

**Answer:** **not equivalent.** They differ in rows 5 (F, T, T) and 7 (F, F, T): there r is true and p is false, so the second rule is true (r alone is enough) but the first is false (it needs p). The first rule gives 3 true rows, the second gives 5. Moving a bracket changes the meaning.

#### Q14 🟡


| p | q | p OR q | p AND q | (p OR q) AND NOT (p AND q) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | F |
| T | F | T | F | T |
| F | T | T | F | T |
| F | F | F | F | F |

**Answer:** true in the rows T, F and F, T (exactly one of the two). This is the **exclusive or**.

#### Q15 🟡


| p | q | NOT p | (NOT p) OR q |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | F | T |
| T | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | T |
| F | F | T | T |

**Answer:** **3** rows are true. The single false row is T, F: the claim is above the limit and no approval is attached.

#### Q16 🔴


| p | NOT p | p OR NOT p | p AND NOT p |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | F | T | F |
| F | T | T | F |

**Answer:** "p OR NOT p" is true in every row: a **tautology** (always true). "p AND NOT p" is false in every row: a **contradiction** (always false). In data work a filter that is a contradiction returns nothing, and a tautology returns everything.

#### Q17 🔴

Helper columns: i AND c, then NOT d, then g AND NOT d, then OR.

| i | c | g | d | i AND c | NOT d | g AND NOT d | approve |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | T | F | F | T |
| T | T | T | F | T | T | T | T |
| T | T | F | T | T | F | F | T |
| T | T | F | F | T | T | F | T |
| T | F | T | T | F | F | F | F |
| T | F | T | F | F | T | T | T |
| T | F | F | T | F | F | F | F |
| T | F | F | F | F | T | F | F |
| F | T | T | T | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | F | F | T | T | T |
| F | T | F | T | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | F | F | F | T | F | F |
| F | F | T | T | F | F | F | F |
| F | F | T | F | F | T | T | T |
| F | F | F | T | F | F | F | F |
| F | F | F | F | F | T | F | F |

**Answer:** **7** of the 16 rows are approved: 4 rows with i and c both true (any g, d), plus the rows with g true and d false that were not already counted (3 more).

#### Q18 🔴


| p | q | r | q OR r | p AND (q OR r) | NOT (p AND (q OR r)) | (NOT q) AND (NOT r) | (NOT p) OR ((NOT q) AND (NOT r)) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | T | F | F | F |
| T | T | F | T | T | F | F | F |
| T | F | T | T | T | F | F | F |
| T | F | F | F | F | T | T | T |
| F | T | T | T | F | T | F | T |
| F | T | F | T | F | T | F | T |
| F | F | T | T | F | T | F | T |
| F | F | F | F | F | T | T | T |

**Answer:** the two result columns are identical, so they are **equivalent** (De Morgan applied twice). **5** rows are true.

#### Q19 🔴

The wanted column is true when p and q are the same (both T or both F). Build it from two cases joined by OR.

| p | q | p AND q | (NOT p) AND (NOT q) | (p AND q) OR ((NOT p) AND (NOT q)) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | F | T |
| T | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | F | F | F |
| F | F | F | T | T |

**Answer:** (p AND q) OR ((NOT p) AND (NOT q)) gives T, F, F, T. In words: "p and q have the same truth value" (both true, or both false). Any rule with the same column is also correct.

#### Q20 🔴

At least two means (a AND b) OR (a AND c) OR (b AND c).

| a | b | c | a AND b | a AND c | b AND c | alarm |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | T | T | T |
| T | T | F | T | F | F | T |
| T | F | T | F | T | F | T |
| T | F | F | F | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | F | F | T | T |
| F | T | F | F | F | F | F |
| F | F | T | F | F | F | F |
| F | F | F | F | F | F | F |

**Answer:** the alarm rings in **4** rows (T,T,T; T,T,F; T,F,T; F,T,T). Exactly one sensor is triggered in **3** rows (T,F,F; F,T,F; F,F,T), and in those rows the alarm stays silent.

## Chapter 4: If-Then, Converse, Inverse, Contrapositive and If and Only If

### Self-check answers

1. **Only when p is true and q is false** (the condition happens but the result does not). In the other three combinations the rule is not broken.
2. **The contrapositive** (swap p and q and negate both: "If NOT q then NOT p"). The converse and inverse are not equivalent to the original.
3. **"If a refund is given, then the receipt was shown."** "Only if" points to what is required, so the requirement (receipt shown) is the result part.

### Practice answers

#### Q1 🟢
The condition is the part after "if" and the result is the part after "then".
- p (condition): a candidate accepts the offer.
- q (result): HR sends a joining kit.

#### Q2 🟢
Original: if p (parcel damaged) then q (courier files a report).
- **Converse** (swap): If the courier files a report, then the parcel is damaged.
- **Inverse** (negate both): If a parcel is not damaged, then the courier does not file a report.
- **Contrapositive** (swap and negate both): If the courier does not file a report, then the parcel is not damaged.

#### Q3 🟢
A promise is broken only when the condition is true and the result is false.
- Asha: finished and got a certificate: promise kept.
- **Bilal: finished and got none: promise broken** (condition true, result false).
- Chitra: did not finish, got one anyway: the condition is false, so the promise says nothing; not broken.
- Dev: did not finish, got none: condition false, nothing promised; not broken.

**Answer:** only Bilal's case breaks the promise.

#### Q4 🟢
p = "the lights are off", q = "the office is closed". Contrapositive (swap and negate both): **"If the office is not closed, then the lights are not off"**, that is, "If the office is open, the lights are on."

| p (lights off) | q (closed) | Original: if p then q | Contrapositive: if NOT q then NOT p |
|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T |
| T | F | F | F |
| F | T | T | T |
| F | F | T | T |

**Answer:** the two columns are identical (T, F, T, T), so they are equivalent.

#### Q5 🟢
"Only if" points to the requirement, so the rule is "If a refund is given, the receipt was shown."

**Answer: (a).** Option (b) is the converse (having the receipt does not force a refund). Option (c) says the opposite of the requirement.

#### Q6 🟢
**No.** The rule says what happens when the temperature is above 40; it does not say that postponement happens only then. The match may have been postponed for rain or another reason. Going from "result happened" back to "condition happened" is using the converse, which is not safe.

#### Q7 🟢
Let p = "it is a leap year", q = "February has 29 days". February did not have 29 days (NOT q), so by the contrapositive ("If NOT q then NOT p") **the year is not a leap year.** We used the contrapositive.

#### Q8 🟡
- If-then form: **"If a customer complains, then she gets a callback."**
- Converse: **"If a customer gets a callback, then she complained."**
- A customer who did not complain but got a callback is a case with the converse's condition true and result false, so the **converse is false** in that world.
- The **original rule is not broken**: that customer did not complain, so the promise says nothing about her.

#### Q9 🟡
A person who **works in Delhi but is not the manager of the Delhi team** (for example, an analyst in the Delhi office). She makes the converse's condition ("works in Delhi") true and its result ("is the Delhi team manager") false, so the converse is false. She does not break the original rule.

#### Q10 🟡
Read "unless" as "if not": **(a)** "If the maintenance flag is not set, then the server restarts."
- **(b)** The flag is set, so the condition ("flag not set") is false. The rule says nothing: the server may or may not have restarted.
- **(c)** Flag not set (condition true) and no restart (result false): **yes, the rule was broken.**

#### Q11 🟡
Rule broken only when days > 5 (condition true) and no HR approval (result false).

| Request | Days above 5? | HR approval? | Broken? |
|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | No | Yes | No |
| R2 | Yes | Yes | No |
| R3 | Yes | No | **Yes** |
| R4 | No | No | No |
| R5 | Yes | No | **Yes** |
| R6 | No (5 is not more than 5) | No | No |

**Answer:** **2** requests (R3 and R5) break the rule.

#### Q12 🟡
Let p = "the invoice is paid", q = "the order is shipped".
(a) is the converse (if q then p), (b) is the contrapositive (if NOT q then NOT p), (c) is the inverse (if NOT p then NOT q).

| p | q | Original (if p then q) | (a) if q then p | (b) if NOT q then NOT p | (c) if NOT p then NOT q |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T | T | T | T | T | T |
| T | F | F | T | F | T |
| F | T | T | F | T | F |
| F | F | T | T | T | T |

**Answer: only (b).** Its column (T, F, T, T) matches the original. (a) and (c) both read T, T, F, T, which differs in rows 2 and 3.

#### Q13 🟡
The rule holds only when "in group" and "can edit" have the same truth value.
- In group and can edit: **possible**.
- In group and cannot edit: **violation**; it breaks the half "if in the group, then can edit".
- Not in group and can edit: **violation**; it breaks the half "if can edit, then in the group".
- Not in group and cannot edit: **possible**.

#### Q14 🟡
p = "the badge is not scanned", q = "the door stays locked". Contrapositive: "If the door does not stay locked, then the badge was not NOT scanned."
Tidy: remove the double negatives. "Door does not stay locked" is "the door unlocks"; "not NOT scanned" is "scanned".

**Answer:** **"If the door unlocks, then the badge was scanned."**

#### Q15 🟡
p = "the shop is not open", q = "the till is locked".
- Converse (if q then p): **"If the till is locked, then the shop is not open."**
- Inverse (if NOT p then NOT q): **"If the shop is open, then the till is not locked."**
- The converse and the inverse are **equivalent to each other** (always true together; the inverse is the contrapositive of the converse).
- Neither is equivalent to the original. The equivalent form of the original is its contrapositive: "If the till is not locked, then the shop is open."

#### Q16 🔴
Let p = "has a degree AND has at least 2 years of experience", q = "is shortlisted".
**(a)** Contrapositive: "If she is not shortlisted, then she does NOT (have a degree and at least 2 years of experience)", which is "If she is not shortlisted, then she has no degree OR she has less than 2 years of experience." (NOT of an AND becomes an OR of NOTs; Chapter 3.)

**(b)** Isha was not shortlisted, so at least one of the two is missing. She has a degree, so the degree is not the missing piece. Therefore **she has less than 2 years of experience.**

#### Q17 🔴
Two parts: Part 1 "if score at least 40, then passes"; Part 2 "if passes, then attendance at least 75". A student breaks the rule if either part is broken.

| Student | Score at least 40? | Attendance at least 75? | Passed? | Part 1 broken? (score ok, not passed) | Part 2 broken? (passed, attendance low) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | Yes | Yes | Yes | No | No |
| S2 | Yes | No | No | **Yes** | No |
| S3 | No | Yes | Yes | No | No |
| S4 | Yes | No | Yes | No | **Yes** |
| S5 | No | No | No | No | No |
| S6 | Yes | Yes | No | **Yes** | No |

**Answer:** **S2, S4 and S6** break the rule. S3 does not: a score below 40 is not mentioned in either part, and attendance is fine. (The rule is not an "if and only if", so passing without 40 is allowed.) Note: the two parts together forbid any student who scores at least 40 but has attendance below 75 (Part 1 says she must pass, Part 2 says she must not), so S2 and S4 can never satisfy the rule. That is why a rule with two parts needs both parts checked.

#### Q18 🔴
Let p = "overloaded", q = "stopped at the check post". S1 is "if p then q".
- S2: "stopped only if overloaded" is "if q then p": the **converse**. Not equivalent.
- S3: "not stopped, so not overloaded" is "if NOT q then NOT p": the **contrapositive**. Equivalent.
- S4: "overloaded only if stopped" is "if p then q": the same as S1. Equivalent.

**Answer:** **S3 and S4** say the same as S1; S2 does not. (Four-row check on the earlier trucks table: S1, S3, S4 all give T, F, T, T; S2 gives T, T, F, T.)

#### Q19 🔴
Let p = "the test passes", q = "we release".
- **(a)** The release happened (q is true). Concluding "the test passed" goes from q back to p: that is the **converse**, which is **not valid**. They may have released despite a failed test, or for another reason. Priya's reasoning is invalid.
- **(b)** The release did not happen (NOT q). By the **contrapositive** ("If NOT q then NOT p"), **the test did not pass.** This is valid.

#### Q20 🔴
Name the facts: A, B, C, D mean "attends".
1. Dev does not attend (D false).
2. "At least one of Chen and Dev attends" and D is false, so **Chen attends**.
3. "If Bela attends, Chen does not attend". Chen does attend, so by the contrapositive **Bela does not attend**.
4. "If Anil attends, Bela attends". Bela does not attend, so by the contrapositive **Anil does not attend**.

**Answer:** **only Chen attends.** A check of all 16 attendance combinations (done by code) gives exactly one combination that satisfies all four facts, so the answer is unique.

## Chapter 5 answer key: Quantifiers and Negation

Every negation below was checked by computer against all small groups (0 to 3 items, and up to 5 for counting statements) with every combination of properties; in each group exactly one of the statement and its negation is true. Nested statements were checked against all small tables.

### Self-check answers

1. "Some stall in the market is not open." (At least one stall is closed.) One closed stall is enough to break "all open".
2. **False.** One customer who did not pay is a counterexample, and a counterexample defeats "every".
3. **Yes.** "Some" means "at least one", so it is still true when all products are on sale.

### Practice answers

**Q1** Negation: **Some intern did not attend the orientation.** Why: "all" is broken by one exception; the property flips to "did not attend". (Not "No intern attended", which is much stronger.)

**Q2** Negation: **Some parcel arrived damaged.** Why: "no" means zero damaged; it fails when there is at least one.

**Q3** Negation: **No staff member works night shifts.** Why: "some" (at least one) fails only when there are zero.

**Q4** Negation: **Some bus on route 12 did not run on time today** (at least one bus was late). Why: "every" fails with one exception.

**Q5** Negation: **Every candidate scored 90 or below** (no candidate scored above 90). Why: "at least one scored above 90" fails only when none did, so everyone is at 90 or below. Note: "below 90" would be wrong; a candidate with exactly 90 is not above 90.

**Q6** Answer: **(b)**. The original is "at least one driver has no licence". It fails only if every driver has a licence. (a) can be true together with the original (one licensed, one unlicensed). (c) is much stronger than needed. (d) is just a copy of the original with an extra condition. Checked: only (b) always disagrees with the original.

**Q7**
- (a) False: Bilal (Night) and Esha (Night) work nights. Negation: **Some employee does not work the day shift.**
- (b) True: Esha lives in Bengaluru.
- (c) True: the night-shift employees are Bilal (Pune) and Esha (Bengaluru); neither is in Hyderabad.
- (d) False: Bilal is in Pune and works nights. Negation: **Some Pune employee does not work the day shift.**

**Q8** Negation: **Every seller offers free delivery.** Why: "Not every" already is the negation of "every"; negating it again removes the "not" (double negation cancels).

**Q9** Negation: **At least one of the three test cases failed.** Why: "none failed" means zero failures; it fails when there is one or more.

**Q10** Negation: **Some new joiner does not have a laptop or does not have an ID card** (lacks at least one of them). Why: "every" becomes "some"; the "and" inside becomes "or" once negated, because one missing item breaks the "and".

**Q11** Negation: **No report is both accurate and on time**, which means every report is inaccurate or late (or both). Why: "some" becomes "none"; "none are accurate-and-on-time" means each report fails at least one of the two. Trap: "No report is accurate and no report is on time" is too strong; a report that is accurate but late is allowed.

**Q12** Negation: **Some customer neither paid in cash nor used a card.** Why: "every" becomes "some"; the inside "cash or card" becomes "not cash and not card" (both fail).

**Q13** Negation: **At most 2 of the 10 trainees passed** (fewer than 3). Why: 3 or more fails only when the number is 0, 1 or 2.

**Q14** Negation: **Either no machine is faulty, or two or more machines are faulty.** Why: "exactly one" is false for every count except 1, which means 0 or 2 or more. Trap: "No machine is faulty" alone ignores the "two or more" case.

**Q15**
- (a) Orders above 500: O2, O3, O4. O3 was not delivered. **False**; counterexample **O3**.
- (b) Orders above 1,000: O2 (paid), O4 (unpaid). **True**; **O4** proves it.
- (c) Orders below 500: O1 and O5, both delivered. **True** (no order below 500 is undelivered).
- (d) Delivered orders: O1, O2, O4, O5. O4 is unpaid. **False**; counterexample **O4**.

**Q16** Negation: **Some manager has no employee reporting to them.** Why: outer "every manager" becomes "some manager"; inner "at least one employee reports" becomes "no employee reports".

**Q17** Negation: **Every employee has missed at least one course on the list** (nobody has completed all of them). Why: "some employee completed every course" becomes "no employee completed every course"; "not every course completed" is "at least one course missed". Trap: "No employee has completed any course" is too strong.

**Q18** The statement is "For every order above 500, it gets free delivery". Negation: **Some order above 500 does not get free delivery.** Why: "every" becomes "some"; the property flips to "does not get free delivery". Orders at or below 500 do not matter here.

**Q19** The true negation pairs are **(ii) and (iii)**.
- (i) No: both are false if the menu has a mix of vegetarian and non-vegetarian dishes.
- (ii) Yes: "some vegetarian" and "none vegetarian" always disagree.
- (iii) Yes: "every dish is under 200" fails exactly when some dish is 200 or more.
- (iv) No: both are true on a mixed menu.

**Q20** Negation: **There is a product for which no supplier can deliver within 2 days.** Why: outer "for every product" becomes "for some product"; inner "there is a supplier who can" becomes "no supplier can".

**Q21** Negation: **On every day of the week, at least one shop in the market was closed.** Why: outer "there is a day" becomes "for every day"; inner "every shop was open" becomes "some shop was closed". Trap: "On every day, every shop was closed" is much too strong.

**Q22**
(a) Teams with a senior member that missed target: **T2 and T6**.
(b) Teams without a senior member (**T3 and T5**) can never refute the claim, whether they met target or not, because the claim says nothing about them. (T5 met target and T3 did not; neither matters.)
(c) The claim is **false**, since counterexamples exist. Its negation, "Some team with a senior member did not meet its target", is true.

**Q23**
(a) **Yes.** If the folder holds two files, one encrypted and one not, S1 is false (one is unencrypted) and S2 is false (one is encrypted).
(b) **Only if the folder is empty.** With no files, neither statement can be broken, so both are (vacuously) true. With at least one file they cannot both be true.
(c) **No**, because a negation can never leave both false, and here both can be false (mixed folder). The true negation of S1 is "Some file is not encrypted".

**Q24**
(a) **No.** 3 claims remain unchecked; any of them could be fraudulent. A "none" claim can only be confirmed by checking all 8.
(b) **A single fraudulent claim** among the remaining 3 would prove the manager wrong.
(c) Checking all remaining 3 and finding no fraud among them would confirm the manager is right (all 8 clean).

**Q25** The negation is **Bob's**: "Some employee who completed the training did not pass the test." Why: "every ... passed" becomes "some ... did not pass".
- Alice is wrong. Group: two trainees who completed the training, one passed and one failed. The original is false (one failed). Alice's "none passed" is also false (one passed). A negation can never leave both false.
- Carol is wrong. Same group: Carol's "every one failed" is false (one passed), and the original is false too.
- Bob in the same group: true (one failed) while the original is false: they disagree, as a negation must.

## Chapter 6 answer key: Valid, Invalid and Sound Arguments

Every verdict below was checked by computer: if-then shapes by full truth table; "all/some/no" arguments by testing every small group (up to 3 people, with every combination of properties), a counterexample being a group where all premises hold and the conclusion fails.

### Self-check answers

1. **No.** This is the invalid shape "If P then Q, Q, so P". Counterexample: the shop is closed but someone left the lights on. Both premises true, conclusion false.
2. **Yes.** If true premises and a true conclusion are connected by an invalid shape, the argument is still invalid (for example, "If 15 is divisible by 5 then 15 is odd; 15 is odd; so 15 is divisible by 5").
3. Valid means the conclusion must follow if the premises are true; sound means it is valid **and** the premises really are true.

### Practice answers

**Q1** **Valid** (modus ponens: If P then Q; P; so Q). Whenever both premises are true, the dashboard must not load.

**Q2** **Invalid.** Shape "If P then Q, Q, so P". Counterexample: today is the day before a festival, the branch is crowded, but it is not salary day. Both premises hold, conclusion false.

**Q3** **Invalid.** Shape "All A are B, x is B, so x is A". Counterexample: Sita is an office cashier who wears a helmet when riding her scooter to work; she is not warehouse staff. Premise 1 can still be true (all warehouse staff wear helmets) and premise 2 is true, yet the conclusion is false.

**Q4** **Valid.** "All A are B; x is A; so x is B". If every drawer pen is blue and this pen is from the drawer, it must be blue.

**Q5** **Yes.** Validity only says "if the premises are true then the conclusion is true". With a false premise the conclusion can be false. Example: "All fish can fly. A salmon is a fish. So a salmon can fly." Valid (structure is fine), conclusion false, because premise 1 is false.

**Q6** **Valid** (All A are B; x is A; so x is B). **Unsound**, because the first premise "All birds can fly" is false (penguins cannot). Its conclusion "penguins can fly" is also false, which shows a valid argument with a false premise can give a false conclusion.

**Q7** **Valid** (same shape) and **sound**: both premises are true (every prime greater than 2 is odd; 7 is a prime greater than 2), so the conclusion "7 is odd" is guaranteed true.

**Q8** **Valid** (modus tollens: If P then Q; not Q; so not P). If approval always brings a signature, no signature means it cannot have been approved.

**Q9** **Invalid** (denying the condition: If P then Q; not P; so not Q). Counterexample: Priya was given a long-service award at 3 years because of an exceptional contribution. The policy says 5 years leads to an award; it does not say nothing else can. Both premises true, conclusion false.

**Q10** **Valid** (the chain: If P then Q; if Q then R; so if P then R). Truth-table check: no combination makes both premises true while "if P then R" is false.

**Q11** **Invalid** ("P or Q, P, so not Q" wrongly rules out the other). Counterexample: both Arun and Bela approved the order. "Arun or Bela approved" is true (inclusive or), "Arun approved" is true, "Bela did not approve" is false.

**Q12** **Invalid.** Counterexample: Asha is an analyst and a manager but is never late; Basu is a manager and a late-comer but not an analyst. Premise 1: some analysts are managers (Asha). Premise 2: some managers are late-comers (Basu). Conclusion: some analysts are late-comers. Asha is the only analyst, and she is not late, so it is false.

**Q13** **Invalid.** Counterexample: Raj is an intern with no admin access, but he can delete records because his team lead granted him a delete permission directly. Take also one admin holder, Lata, who can delete. Premise 1: no intern has admin access (Raj has none). Premise 2: all admin holders can delete (Lata can). Conclusion "no intern can delete records" is false (Raj can). The second premise says what admin holders can do, not that only they can.

**Q14** Many answers work. One: A = dogs, B = pets, C = cats. Premise 1: all dogs are pets (true). Premise 2: some pets are cats (true). Conclusion: some dogs are cats (false). Another: A = cars, B = vehicles, C = bicycles. Computer check: this shape is invalid, so a counterexample always exists.

**Q15**
- (a) **(1) valid and sound.** All multiples of 4 are even (true), 12 is a multiple of 4 (true).
- (b) **(2) valid but unsound.** Structure "All A are B; x is A; so x is B" is fine, but premise 1 is false (10 is even but not a multiple of 4, so "all even numbers are multiples of 4" fails). The conclusion "10 is a multiple of 4" is false.
- (c) **(3) invalid.** Shape "All A are B; x is B; so x is A". Premises true, conclusion false (6 is not a multiple of 4).
- (d) **(3) invalid.** Shape "If P then Q; Q; so P". All three statements happen to be true, yet the shape does not guarantee it. The same shape with another number shows it can fail: "If 9 is divisible by 5 then 9 is odd" is true (its if-part is false, so nothing can break it); "9 is odd" is true; but "9 is divisible by 5" is false.

**Q16** **Valid** (All A are B; no B is C; so no A is C). Reason: a supervisor is a trained staff member; no trained staff member is on probation; so no supervisor can be on probation. Checked: no group makes both premises true and the conclusion false.

**Q17** **Invalid.** Both premises true and R true but P false occurs in exactly **2** combinations (P, Q, R):
- P false, Q false, R true: "if P then Q" is true (P false), "if Q then R" is true, R true, conclusion P false.
- P false, Q true, R true: both ifs true, R true, P false.

Other combinations fail either a premise or already make P true. A concrete reading: the candidate got feedback (R) without clearing aptitude (P false).

**Q18** **(a)**. With "Everyone who gets a raise has been promoted", "Dev got a raise" gives "Dev was promoted", a valid chain. (b), (c) and (d) say nothing linking raises to promotions; a computer search found a case for each where all premises are true and Dev was not promoted, so they leave the argument invalid.

**Q19** **Valid, but unsound.** The two premises contradict each other (all encrypted, yet one is not), so they can never be true together. Therefore there is no situation with true premises and a false conclusion, which is the definition of valid (sometimes called "vacuously valid"). It is **not sound**, because at least one premise is always false. It also shows that validity alone proves nothing: from contradictory premises any conclusion is "valid".

**Q20**
(a) Replace premise 2 with **"Only admin-access holders can delete records"** (that is, everyone who can delete records holds admin access). Then: no intern has admin access, so no intern can delete records. Checked valid.
(b) Keep the premises and write the conclusion **"No admin-access holder is an intern."** Checked valid: it is the original first premise turned around. Any conclusion that must hold in every case where the premises hold works.

## Chapter 7: Sets and Venn Diagrams

All numbers were recomputed by script; puzzles with an unknown were brute-forced over every possible region filling and give a unique answer (or the stated range).

### Self-check answers

1. The circles share some people. The people in the overlap are in both groups; the overlap is called the intersection.
2. At least one = 30 + 20 - 10 = 40.
3. No. Only 10 people use the app, so at most 10 can use both. A "both" of 15 is impossible.

### Practice answers

**Q1** (a) Collect every name once: {Asha, Bilal, Chen, Dara, Esha, Farid, Gita}, 7 people. (b) Names in both lists: {Chen, Esha}. (c) X without the overlap: {Asha, Bilal, Dara}. (d) Y without the overlap: {Farid, Gita}. Check: 3 + 2 + 2 = 7.

**Q2** At least one = 25 + 18 - 6 = 37 (the 6 were counted twice). Neither = 40 - 37 = 3.

**Q3** (a) True: every passer is in the batch. (b) False: 8 batch members did not pass. (c) True: A is inside S, so the overlap is all of A, which is 22. (d) True: the union is just S, 30 people.

**Q4** (a) 30 - 12 = 18. (b) Remote 12, of whom 2 are on leave: 12 - 2 = 10. (c) Remote or on leave = 12 + 5 - 2 = 15; neither = 30 - 15 = 15.

**Q5** (a) Only A = 60 - 25 = 35. (b) Only B = 45 - 25 = 20. (c) 60 + 45 - 25 = 80 (check 35 + 25 + 20 = 80).

**Q6** (a) 14 + 9 + 11 + 6 = 40. (b) A = only A + both = 14 + 9 = 23. (c) B = 11 + 9 = 20. (d) Exactly one = 14 + 11 = 25.

**Q7** (a) 8. (b) Not in A = only B + neither = 12 + 15 = 27. (c) At most one = everything except both = 8 + 12 + 15 = 35. (d) 12.

**Q8** The monthly circle sits entirely inside the weekly circle. (a) The union is the weekly set: 70. (b) Weekly only = 70 - 30 = 40. (c) Both = 30 (all monthly subscribers).

**Q9** The four regions must add to 120: only phone 35 + only laptop 28 + neither 20 = 83, so both = 120 - 83 = 37. Phone in total = only phone + both = 35 + 37 = 72 (the circle total includes the overlap). Check 35 + 37 + 28 + 20 = 120.

**Q10** Only yoga = 30 - 12 = 18; only swim = 28 - 12 = 16; exactly one = 34. At least one = 30 + 28 - 12 = 46; neither = 50 - 46 = 4. Check 34 + 12 + 4 = 50.

**Q11** Both = 55 + 50 - 85 = 20. Only B = 50 - 20 = 30. Neither = 100 - 85 = 15. Check: only A 35, both 20, only B 30, neither 15 = 100.

**Q12** Let neither = n, so both = 2n. At least one = 90 - n. Also at least one = 54 + 48 - 2n = 102 - 2n. So 90 - n = 102 - 2n, giving n = 12 and both = 24. Check: at least one = 78 = 54 + 48 - 24, and 78 + 12 = 90.

**Q13** At least one = 50 + 40 + 30 - 15 - 12 - 10 + 6 = 89. None = 100 - 89 = 11. Only E = 50 - 15 - 12 + 6 = 29; only S = 40 - 15 - 10 + 6 = 21; only P = 30 - 12 - 10 + 6 = 14; exactly one = 64. Exactly two = (15 - 6) + (12 - 6) + (10 - 6) = 9 + 6 + 4 = 19. Check 64 + 19 + 6 = 89.

**Q14** Total = 10 + 8 + 6 + 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 7 = 45. A = 10 + 5 + 4 + 2 = 21. B = 8 + 5 + 3 + 2 = 18. C = 6 + 4 + 3 + 2 = 15. At least two = 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 = 14.

**Q15** The pair totals include the centre, so remove it. A and B only = 14 - 5 = 9; B and C only = 11 - 5 = 6; A and C only = 9 - 5 = 4. Exactly two = 19. At least two = 19 + 5 = 24.

**Q16** (i) Can be correct: at least one = 25 + 22 - 9 = 38, which is at most 40; 2 people use neither. (ii) Cannot: at least one = 30 + 35 - 10 = 55, more than the 50 people. (iii) Cannot: the "both" group (25) cannot be larger than B (20), because everyone in both is in B.

**Q17** (a) If nobody is in neither, at least one = 100. Both = 70 + 60 - 100 = 30. (b) At least one = 130 - both. It cannot exceed 100, so both is at least 30. It cannot be less than 70 either (the A circle alone has 70), so both is at most 60. Neither = 100 - (130 - both) = both - 30. Smallest neither = 30 - 30 = 0 (both = 30); largest neither = 60 - 30 = 30 (everyone in B also in A, so only 70 people buy anything). Smallest 0, largest 30 (confirmed by enumeration).

**Q18** Exactly one = (120 - x) + (90 - x) = 210 - 2x, where x = both. So 210 - 2x = 130, giving x = 40. At least one = 210 - 40 = 170; neither = 200 - 170 = 30. Check: only bread 80, only milk 50, both 40, neither 30 = 200.

**Q19** At least one = 120 - 10 = 110. Let x = all three. 60 + 50 + 55 - 20 - 25 - 18 + x = 110, so 102 + x = 110 and x = 8. Only Excel = 55 - 25 - 18 + 8 = 20. Only Python = 60 - 20 - 25 + 8 = 23; only SQL = 50 - 20 - 18 + 8 = 20. Exactly one = 23 + 20 + 20 = 63. Check: exactly two = (25 - 8) + (20 - 8) + (18 - 8) = 17 + 12 + 10 = 39; 63 + 39 + 8 = 110. Brute force over all region fillings gives only x = 8.

**Q20** Discounted or out of stock = discounted (36) + out-of-stock-not-discounted (4) = 40. Out of stock and discounted = 24 - 4 = 20. Neither = 60 - 40 = 20. Check: discounted only 16, both 20, out of stock only 4, neither 20 = 60.

**Q21** Sum of circle totals = 70 + 60 + 50 = 180. 180 = (exactly one) + 2 x 40 + 3 x 10 = (exactly one) + 110. So exactly one = 70. Check: at least one = 70 + 40 + 10 = 120, and none = 150 - 120 = 30, as given. Brute force confirms 70 is the only possible value.

**Q22** 5 completed nothing, so 55 completed at least one module. Smallest: among those 55, 55 - 45 = 10 missed P, 55 - 40 = 15 missed Q and 55 - 35 = 20 missed R. Anyone who misses something is on at least one of these lists, so at most 10 + 15 + 20 = 45 people miss something, and at least 55 - 45 = 10 completed all three. Largest: the sum of the circle totals is 45 + 40 + 35 = 120 = (exactly one) + 2 x (exactly two) + 3 x (all three), and (exactly one) + (exactly two) + (all three) = 55. Subtract: (exactly two) + 2 x (all three) = 65. So all three = (65 - exactly two) / 2, which is at most 32.5, so at most 32 (a whole number); 32 is reachable with exactly two = 1 and exactly one = 22. Smallest 10, largest 32 (confirmed by enumerating every region filling).

**Q23** A ∪ B = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8}, so (A ∪ B)' = {9, 10, 11, 12}. A' = {7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12}; B' = {1, 2, 3, 9, 10, 11, 12}; A' ∩ B' = {9, 10, 11, 12}. A ∩ B = {4, 5, 6}, so (A ∩ B)' = {1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12}. A' ∪ B' = {1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12}. Equal pairs: (A ∪ B)' = A' ∩ B', and (A ∩ B)' = A' ∪ B' (De Morgan's rules).

**Q24** Only pool = 40 - 14 - 12 + 5 = 19. Only floor = 36 - 14 - 10 + 5 = 17. Only classes = 30 - 10 - 12 + 5 = 13. Exactly two = (14 - 5) + (10 - 5) + (12 - 5) = 9 + 5 + 7 = 21. At least one = 19 + 17 + 13 + 21 + 5 = 75 (formula: 40 + 36 + 30 - 14 - 10 - 12 + 5 = 75). None = 90 - 75 = 15.

**Q25** Sum of club sizes = 40 + 35 + 25 = 100. Let a = exactly one club, c = all three. a + 14 + c = 80, so a + c = 66. Also a + 2 x 14 + 3c = 100, so a + 3c = 72. Subtract: 2c = 6, so c = 3 and a = 63. Brute force over all region fillings confirms c = 3 and a = 63 for every valid filling.

## Chapter 8: Classification, Odd One Out and Analogies

Letter, number, count and band items were recomputed by script. Each odd-one-out was checked against the listed features of every item: exactly one item breaks the stated rule (except Q24, which is deliberately ambiguous).

### Self-check answers

1. **Electricity bill per unit used.** Rent, insurance and the annual licence fee do not change with how much you use; the electricity bill grows with usage. Rule: fixed cost versus variable cost.
2. **Sew.** A broom is used to sweep, a needle is used to sew (tool and its job). Thread and cloth are things used in sewing; sharp only describes a needle.
3. The groups overlap: a client lunch on a train trip fits both Food and Travel. A good scheme needs each item to fit exactly one group, or a written tie-break rule (for example "group by main purpose").

### Practice answers

**Q1** Rent, insurance premium, annual licence and permanent salaries stay the same however much is produced (fixed). Raw material purchases grow with output (variable). Answer: **Raw material purchases.**

**Q2** Laptop, tablet, smartphone and smartwatch are electronic devices; an exercise book is paper. Answer: **Exercise book.**

**Q3** Triangle, square, pentagon and hexagon are shapes made only of straight edges (polygons). A circle has one curved edge. Answer: **Circle.**

**Q4** Department, city, grade and job family are labels (categories). Salary is an amount you can add and average. Answer: **Salary.**

**Q5** Pen, pencil, marker and crayon all make marks. An eraser removes marks. Answer: **Eraser.**

**Q6** Positions: B=2, D=4 (+2); F=6, H=8 (+2); J=10, L=12 (+2); N=14, Q=17 (+3); R=18, T=20 (+2). Answer: **NQ.** (The first letters B, F, J, N, R also step by 4 each time, so nothing else breaks a rule.)

**Q7** Sparrow, eagle, parrot and crow are birds; a bat is a mammal. Answer: **Bat.**

**Q8** Payroll, recruitment, training and onboarding are HR functions (activities a department does). An invoice is a document. Answer: **Invoice.**

**Q9** Sentence: "An author makes (writes) a book." So "A painter makes (paints) a ___." Answer: **Painting.** Canvas is the surface, a brush is the tool and a gallery is a place; none is the finished product.

**Q10** Sentence: "A kilogram measures weight." So "A litre measures ___." Answer: **Volume.** (Water and bottle are things, length is another quantity.)

**Q11** Generous and stingy are opposites. Opposite of brave: **Cowardly.** Bold means almost the same as brave; strong and angry are different ideas.

**Q12** A wheel is a part of a car. A row is a part of a **Table.** A column is another part of the same table, not a whole; a cell is a smaller part inside the row; a number is a value, not a whole.

**Q13** Rule: each letter moves 2 forward (C to E, A to C, T to V). D to F, O to Q, G to I gives **FQI.** (Script check: CAT gives ECV with this rule.)

**Q14** 4 x 4 x 4 = 64, so the rule is "cube". 5 x 5 x 5 = **125.** (The cube rule is the simplest rule that fits the first pair.)

**Q15** Regular exercise builds fitness, as study builds **Knowledge** (cause and effect). An exam, a book and a teacher are related things, but none is the result that study produces.

**Q16** A drizzle is a weak form of rain, a downpour a strong one. A breeze is a weak wind, a **Gale** is a strong wind (degree, weak to strong).

**Q17** Thermometer measures temperature (tool and the quantity it measures). Ruler measures length: **(a).** A clock on a wall, ink for a pen and paper in a book are other kinds of links.

**Q18** Fixed (amount does not depend on the number of sales): Office rent, Insurance, Manager salary, Annual software subscription (4 items). Variable: Sales commissions, Courier charges, Packaging material, Card payment fees (a percentage of each sale) (4 items). 4 + 4 = 8, none left out.

**Q19** Dozen (12), pair (2), score (20) and gross (144) are words for a fixed count of things. A metre is a unit of length. Answer: **Metre.**

**Q20** By the stated rule: EmployeeID = text label; Name = text label; Department = category; JoinDate = date; Salary = number; Grade = category; Age = number; City = category. Totals: text label 2, category 3, date 1, number 2. Check 2 + 3 + 1 + 2 = 8 columns.

**Q21** Gaps between letters: ADG (A=1, D=4, G=7: +3, +3), FIL (6, 9, 12: +3, +3), KNQ (11, 14, 17: +3, +3), PSV (16, 19, 22: +3, +3), UYB (U=21, Y=25, B=2, which is 28 after wrap-around: +4, +3). Answer: **UYB.**

**Q22** Position sums: C(3) + X(24) = 27; D(4) + W(23) = 27; F(6) + U(21) = 27; G(7) + V(22) = 29; H(8) + S(19) = 27. Answer: **GV.**

**Q23** Rule: a x b = c. 3 x 4 = 12, 5 x 2 = 10, 6 x 7 = 42, 8 x 3 = 24 (not 25), 9 x 2 = 18. Answer: **(8, 3, 25).**

**Q24** No single answer. S differs from the others in billing (yearly instead of monthly); T differs in hosting (on-premise instead of cloud). Each is "the odd one" under a different feature. The honest answer is that the question is ambiguous; it would be settled by naming the feature being tested ("odd one out by billing" gives S, "by hosting" gives T).

**Q25** Doctor : Hospital, Teacher : School, Chef : Kitchen and Judge : Court are person and workplace. Writer : Pen is person and tool. Answer: **Writer : Pen.**

**Q26** Sentence: "An employee receives a payslip (a record of the period's work and pay)." For a student, the matching record is a **Marksheet** (a record of the term's results). School and teacher are places and people; a fee is money paid.

**Q27** Cheap (below 200): 150, 199, 45 = 3. Mid (200 to 999): 899, 200, 999, 600, 350 = 5. Premium (1000 and above): 1200, 1000, 2500, 1999 = 4. Total 3 + 5 + 4 = 12. Yes, every item fits exactly one band because the bands do not overlap (199 is cheap, 200 is mid, 999 is mid, 1000 is premium) and together cover all prices.

**Q28** (a) Electronic: Laptop, Phone, Torch, Desktop, Router, Printer, Smart speaker = 7. (b) Portable: Laptop, Phone, Torch, Notebook, Backpack, Umbrella = 6. (c) Electronic or portable = 7 + 6 - 3 (the three in both) = 10. (d) Neither: Desk, Chair = 2. Check 10 + 2 = 12.

**Q29** (a) A flight to a skills workshop is Travel (moving a person) and Training (learning a skill): fits two groups. (b) Lunch with a client: Client entertainment only. (c) Online course fee: Training only. (d) Cab to a client meeting: Travel only. (e) Train ticket to head office: Travel only. Only (a) overlaps. Fix: a tie-break rule such as "file each expense under its main purpose; a trip taken only to attend training goes under Training" (or the reverse, as long as it is written down and used by everyone).

**Q30** Digit sums: 4+7+1 = 12; 3+6+3 = 12; 1+9+2 = 12; 2+6+4 = 12; 1+7+5 = 13. Rule: the three digits add up to 12. Answer: **BC-175.**

## Chapter 9 answer key: Number, Letter and Pattern Series

Letter positions used throughout: A=1, B=2, C=3, D=4, E=5, F=6, G=7, H=8, I=9, J=10, K=11, L=12, M=13, N=14, O=15, P=16, Q=17, R=18, S=19, T=20, U=21, V=22, W=23, X=24, Y=25, Z=26. After 26 the count wraps to 1.

### Self-check answers

1. **26.** Gaps: 5, 5, 5 (11-6, 16-11, 21-16). Constant gap, so add 5: 21 + 5 = 26.
2. **1215.** Multipliers: 15/5 = 3, 45/15 = 3, 135/45 = 3, 405/135 = 3. Constant multiplier 3: 405 x 3 = 1215.
3. **S.** G=7, J=10, M=13, P=16: gap +3 each time. 16 + 3 = 19 = S.

### Practice answers

**Q1. 44.** Gaps: 7, 7, 7, 7. Constant gap of +7. 37 + 7 = 44.

**Q2. 84.** Gaps: -9, -9, -9 (111-120, 102-111, 93-102). Subtract 9: 93 - 9 = 84.

**Q3. 768.** Gaps differ (9, 36, 144), so test multipliers: 12/3 = 4, 48/12 = 4, 192/48 = 4. Multiply by 4: 192 x 4 = 768.

**Q4. 9.** Multipliers: 243/729 = 1/3, 81/243 = 1/3, 27/81 = 1/3. Divide by 3: 27 / 3 = 9.

**Q5. 24.** Gaps: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 (4-3, 6-4, 9-6, 13-9, 18-13; they grow by 1). Next gap 6: 18 + 6 = 24.

**Q6. 35.** Gaps: 49-50 = -1, 47-49 = -2, 44-47 = -3, 40-44 = -4. The amount subtracted grows by 1 each step. Next gap -5: 40 - 5 = 35.

**Q7. A.** Positions: K=11, I=9, G=7, E=5, C=3. Gap -2 each time. 3 - 2 = 1 = A.

**Q8. D.** Positions: T=20, P=16, L=12, H=8. Gap -4. 8 - 4 = 4 = D.

**Q9. 28.** Gaps: 21 - 14 = 7 and 42 - 35 = 7. The known gaps are both 7, so ? = 21 + 7 = 28 and then 35 - 28 = 7 fits.

**Q10. 24.** Multipliers: 48/96 = 1/2; 12 to 6 is 1/2. Halve each time: 48 / 2 = 24, and 24 / 2 = 12 fits.

**Q11. K.** Positions: B=2, E=5, H=8, ?, N=14, Q=17. Gap +3. 8 + 3 = 11 = K, and 11 + 3 = 14 = N fits.

**Q12. 160.** Gap is +5 each visitor. The 12th term needs 11 steps from the first: 105 + 11 x 5 = 105 + 55 = 160. (Step count is position minus 1.)

**Q13. 17.** Counts 1, 3, 5, 7, 9: gap +2. Row 9 is 8 steps after row 1: 1 + 8 x 2 = 17.

**Q14. Square.** The repeating block is circle, square, square (length 3). 14 divided by 3 is 4 remainder 2, so the 14th shape is the 2nd shape in the block: square. (Check: positions 13 = circle, 14 = square, 15 = square.)

**Q15. 40.** Gaps: 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 (odd numbers). Next gap 11: 29 + 11 = 40. (Check: the terms are 0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36 plus 4, so the 7th is 36 + 4 = 40.)

**Q16. 730.** Gaps and multipliers are not constant (gaps 6, 18, 54, 162; multipliers 2.5, 2.8, about 2.93, about 2.98). Try "multiply then adjust": 4 x 3 = 12, minus 2 = 10; 10 x 3 = 30, minus 2 = 28; 28 x 3 = 84, minus 2 = 82; 82 x 3 = 246, minus 2 = 244. Rule: x 3, then - 2. Next: 244 x 3 = 732, minus 2 = 730.

**Q17. 25.** Split into two tracks. Odd places: 5, 8, 11, 14 (+3). Even places: 40, 35, 30 (-5). The 8th term is on the even track, which continues 30 - 5 = 25.

**Q18. Q.** Positions: D=4, T=20, G=7, S=19, J=10, R=18, M=13, ?. Odd places: 4, 7, 10, 13 (+3: D, G, J, M). Even places: 20, 19, 18 (-1: T, S, R). The 8th term is on the even track: 18 - 1 = 17 = Q.

**Q19. 47.** Gaps are 3, 4, 7, 11, which are not constant and do not grow by a fixed amount. Test "each term is the sum of the two before": 4 + 7 = 11, 7 + 11 = 18, 11 + 18 = 29. Next: 18 + 29 = 47.

**Q20. 38.** Gaps: 3, 5, 7, 9 (odd numbers, growing by 2). Next gap 11: 27 + 11 = 38.

**Q21. 38.** Divide each term by 2: 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17. These are the prime numbers in order (skipping 2). The next prime after 17 is 19, and 19 x 2 = 38.

**Q22. 219.** Subtract 3 from each term: 1, 8, 27, 64, 125. These are cubes (1, 2, 3, 4, 5 each cubed). Next cube: 6 x 6 x 6 = 216. Add 3 back: 219.

**Q23. M.** Positions: N=14, Q=17, U=21, Z=26, then 26 + 6 = 32, wrap: 32 - 26 = 6 = F. Gaps: 3, 4, 5, 6 (grow by 1). Next gap 7: 6 + 7 = 13 = M.

**Q24. 28 should be 27.** Most terms go up by 7: 6, 13, 20, ?, 34, 41, 48. After 20 we expect 27, then 34 (27 + 7), 41, 48 all fit. The term 28 breaks the rule; it should be 27.

**Q25. 43 should be 42.** Gaps between the first five terms: 2, 5, 8, 11 (2 to 4, 4 to 9, 9 to 17, 17 to 28). The gaps grow by 3, so the next gap is 14: 28 + 14 = 42, and then 42 + 17 = 59 fits (the last term). So 43 is wrong and should be 42.

**Q26. 60.** Gaps: 5, 6, 7, 8 (grow by 1). Next gap 9: 51 + 9 = 60.

**Q27. 41.** Sticks: 5, 9, 13, 17: gap +4 per added house. For 10 houses we add 9 more houses to the first: 5 + 9 x 4 = 41. (Check with a rule: sticks = 4 x houses + 1; 4 x 10 + 1 = 41.)

**Q28. 54.** Look at pairs of steps: 16 to 8 (divide by 2), 8 to 24 (multiply by 3), 24 to 12 (divide by 2), 12 to 36 (multiply by 3), 36 to 18 (divide by 2). The operations take turns: divide by 2, multiply by 3. The next one is multiply by 3: 18 x 3 = 54.

**Q29. 91.** Gaps: 4, 9, 16, 25 (squares of 2, 3, 4, 5). Next gap is 6 squared = 36: 55 + 36 = 91.

**Q30. 122.** Gaps: 1, 3, 9, 27 (each gap is 3 times the one before). Next gap 81: 41 + 81 = 122.

**Q31. 2520.** Multipliers: 3/1 = 3, 12/3 = 4, 60/12 = 5, 360/60 = 6 (the multiplier grows by 1). Next multiplier 7: 360 x 7 = 2520.

**Q32. 114.** Operations take turns: 6 x 3 = 18, 18 - 4 = 14, 14 x 3 = 42, 42 - 4 = 38. Rule: multiply by 3, then subtract 4. Next is multiply by 3: 38 x 3 = 114.

**Q33. 70.** Two tracks. Odd places: 3, 6, 12, 24 (doubles). Even places: 100, 90, 80 (-10). The 8th term is on the even track: 80 - 10 = 70.

**Q34. FPS.** Treat each position inside the group as its own series. First letters: B, C, D, E (positions 2, 3, 4, 5; +1) so next is 6 = F. Second letters: D, G, J, M (4, 7, 10, 13; +3) so next is 16 = P. Third letters: K, M, O, Q (11, 13, 15, 17; +2) so next is 19 = S. Answer: FPS.

**Q35. 40 should be 39.** Test "each term is the sum of the two before": 6 + 9 = 15, 9 + 15 = 24, 15 + 24 = 39. The series shows 40, so 40 is the odd one. Check with 39: 24 + 39 = 63 (matches), 39 + 63 = 102 (matches).

**Q36. 49.** Two rules by position (place). Odd places: 1, 9, 25, which are 1 x 1, 3 x 3, 5 x 5 (squares of odd numbers). Even places: 8, 64, 216, which are 2 cubed, 4 cubed, 6 cubed. The 7th term is on an odd place: the squares were of 1, 3, 5, so the next is 7 x 7 = 49. (Equivalent: place n odd gives n squared; place n even gives n cubed.)

**Q37. 1944.** Gaps and multipliers are not simple (multipliers 1.5, 2, 3, 6). Test "multiply the two before": 2 x 3 = 6, 3 x 6 = 18, 6 x 18 = 108. Next: 18 x 108 = 1944 (18 x 100 = 1800, 18 x 8 = 144, total 1944).

**Q38. 61.** Test "add the three before": 2 + 3 + 5 = 10, 3 + 5 + 10 = 18, 5 + 10 + 18 = 33. Next: 10 + 18 + 33 = 61.

**Q39. Z.** Positions: Y=25, X=24, U=21, P=16, I=9. Gaps: -1, -3, -5, -7 (odd numbers growing by 2). Next gap -9: 9 - 9 = 0, and position 0 wraps to 26 = Z.

**Q40. Rs 185.** Daily savings go up by Rs 3: 5, 8, 11, ... Day 10 saving is 5 + 9 x 3 = 32. List: 5, 8, 11, 14, 17, 20, 23, 26, 29, 32. Total: pair first and last (5 + 32 = 37), second and second last (8 + 29 = 37), 11 + 26 = 37, 14 + 23 = 37, 17 + 20 = 37. That is 5 pairs of 37: 5 x 37 = 185. Check by adding in order: 5+8=13, +11=24, +14=38, +17=55, +20=75, +23=98, +26=124, +29=153, +32=185.

---

### Verification note

Every question in this chapter was checked by computer against a library of rule types (constant gap, constant multiplier, changing gaps, changing multipliers, two interleaved tracks, squares, cubes, primes, add-the-two-before, add-the-three-before, product of the two before, alternating operations, repeating cycles, and wrap-around arithmetic for letters). In every case only one next value fitted. The three "wrong term" questions (24, 25, 35) were also checked: exactly one single-term correction repairs each list. The letter groups (34) were checked one position at a time. Q9 to Q11 (missing middle term) were checked by trying every candidate value. Q12 to Q14, Q27 and Q40 are word problems built on the same number series; their series were checked in the same way. Q36 (squares on odd places, cubes on even places) relies on a rule by place number, so it is unique only within the families listed above.

## Chapter 10 answer key

Every grid puzzle (Q1 to Q3, Q5 to Q7, Q9 to Q11, Q13 to Q15) was checked by brute force over all possible arrangements: each has exactly one solution, and removing any single clue makes it ambiguous, so every clue is needed. The table problems (Q4, Q8, Q12) were checked by searching all whole-number fillings: each has exactly one. Worked examples and scenarios were checked the same way.

### Self-check answers

1. A cell is the box where one row and one column cross. The Payroll row and Contract column cell shows how many contract staff Payroll has.
2. The row totals add to 10 + 20 + 30 = 60, which is the grand total. The column totals must also add to 60, so the missing one is 60 - 25 - 15 = 20.
3. Pooja must be in Pune (last one standing: the Pune column has crosses for Neha and Om, so only Pooja can fill it). Then the rest of Pooja's row becomes crosses for the other places.

| Meera | Payroll |
| Naveen | Recruitment |
| Omar | Training |

## Chapter 11 answer key

Flowcharts are written as text outlines (see Chapter 11 for how to read them). Every chart below was run by code against its rule on every combination of test values listed in the notes, with no mismatches, and checked so that every box is reachable and every path ends at an End.

### Self-check answers

1. A **diamond** is the shape for a question. A yes/no question has **two exits**, one labelled Yes and one labelled No.
2. 800 is not over 1000, so the answer to the question is No. The order follows the No arrow to 'Add delivery fee of 50'. The result is a fee of 50.
3. The person following the chart cannot tell which answer sends them along that arrow, so two readers could choose different paths. Every exit of a question must be labelled.

### Practice answers

#### Q1: Free delivery

**How to build it.** One question with two exits is all this needs. The test is 'at least 999', so exactly 999 goes to the Yes arrow: this is the boundary case that people get wrong.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is the order total 999 or more?
    Yes: E1 (End) Delivery fee 0
    No: E2 (End) Delivery fee 49
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| order total 998 | `S > D1(No) > E2` | Delivery fee 49 |
| order total 999 | `S > D1(Yes) > E1` | Delivery fee 0 |
| order total 2500 | `S > D1(Yes) > E1` | Delivery fee 0 |

#### Q2: Attendance marking

**How to build it.** Two questions in a row. The 'is there a record' question must come first because you cannot compare a time that does not exist. Then 09:30 exactly belongs to On time because the rule says '09:30 or earlier'.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is there a swipe-in record?
    Yes: D2 <Question> Is the swipe-in time 9:30 or earlier?
        Yes: E2 (End) On time
        No: E3 (End) Late
    No: E1 (End) Absent
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| no swipe record | `S > D1(No) > E1` | Absent |
| swipe at 09:30 | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > E2` | On time |
| swipe at 09:31 | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(No) > E3` | Late |

#### Q3: Refund approval

**How to build it.** Both conditions must hold for approval, so approval sits at the end of a chain: a No at either question exits to a rejection. The order of the questions matters here because the text asks for the 'too late' reason first.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is the request within 7 days of purchase?
    Yes: D2 <Question> Is the item unused?
        Yes: E3 (End) Approve refund
        No: E2 (End) Reject: item used
    No: E1 (End) Reject: too late
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 7 days, item unused | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > E3` | Approve refund |
| 3 days, item used | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(No) > E2` | Reject: item used |
| 9 days, item unused | `S > D1(No) > E1` | Reject: too late |

#### Q4: Leave approval

**How to build it.** The balance check comes first because it can end the process before any approval is needed. Then the days split into three bands with two chained questions. The manager question appears twice (once on the 3 to 5 band, once on the 6 or more band); drawing it twice keeps the chart a simple tree. HR is asked only after the manager says yes.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Are the days asked more than the leave balance?
    Yes: E1 (End) Reject: balance too low
    No: D2 <Question> Are the days asked 2 or fewer?
        Yes: E2 (End) Auto-approve
        No: D3 <Question> Are the days asked 5 or fewer?
            Yes: D4 <Question> Does the manager approve?
                Yes: E3 (End) Approve
                No: E4 (End) Reject by manager
            No: D5 <Question> Does the manager approve?
                Yes: D6 <Question> Does HR approve?
                    Yes: continue at E3 (shown above)
                    No: E5 (End) Reject by HR
                No: continue at E4 (shown above)
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 4 days asked, balance 3 | `S > D1(Yes) > E1` | Reject: balance too low |
| 2 days asked, balance 10 | `S > D1(No) > D2(Yes) > E2` | Auto-approve |
| 4 days asked, balance 10, manager says yes | `S > D1(No) > D2(No) > D3(Yes) > D4(Yes) > E3` | Approve |
| 8 days asked, balance 10, manager yes, HR no | `S > D1(No) > D2(No) > D3(No) > D5(Yes) > D6(No) > E5` | Reject by HR |
| 8 days asked, balance 10, manager no | `S > D1(No) > D2(No) > D3(No) > D5(No) > E4` | Reject by manager |

#### Q5: Hiring flow

**How to build it.** Each stage is a gate: a No exits to its own rejection, a Yes moves to the next gate. Four gates in a row make a straight line of questions with one exit per gate.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is the resume shortlisted?
    Yes: D2 <Question> Is the test score 60 or more?
        Yes: D3 <Question> Did the candidate pass the interview?
            Yes: D4 <Question> Is the background check clear?
                Yes: E5 (End) Make offer
                No: E4 (End) Offer withdrawn
            No: E3 (End) Reject after interview
        No: E2 (End) Reject after test
    No: E1 (End) Reject at screening
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| shortlisted, test 59 | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(No) > E2` | Reject after test |
| shortlisted, test 60, interview passed, background clear | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > D3(Yes) > D4(Yes) > E5` | Make offer |
| shortlisted, test 85, interview passed, background not clear | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > D3(Yes) > D4(No) > E4` | Offer withdrawn |
| not shortlisted | `S > D1(No) > E1` | Reject at screening |

#### Q6: Login with 3 tries

**How to build it.** This chart needs a loop: an action 'read the password and add 1 to tries' that can be visited up to 3 times. The counter is set to 0 once, before the loop, and the 'have 3 tries been used' question sits on the No branch of the password check, so a correct password never reaches it.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> P0 [Action] Set tries = 0
-> P1 [Action] Read the password entered; add 1 to tries
-> D1 <Question> Is the password correct?
    Yes: E1 (End) Logged in
    No: D2 <Question> Have 3 tries been used?
        Yes: E2 (End) Account locked
        No: continue at P1 (shown above)
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| wrong, wrong, right | `S > P0 > P1 > D1(No) > D2(No) > P1 > D1(No) > D2(No) > P1 > D1(Yes) > E1` | Logged in |
| wrong, wrong, wrong | `S > P0 > P1 > D1(No) > D2(No) > P1 > D1(No) > D2(No) > P1 > D1(No) > D2(Yes) > E2` | Account locked |
| right on the first try | `S > P0 > P1 > D1(Yes) > E1` | Logged in |

#### Q7: Expense claim

**How to build it.** The receipt rule applies only above 500, so the first question splits on the amount. The second split (5000) happens for every claim that survives the receipt rule, including small ones, so both branches of the first question join at the same node. Joining branches is allowed: many arrows may enter one node.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is the amount more than 500?
    Yes: D2 <Question> Is a receipt attached?
        Yes: D3 <Question> Is the amount 5000 or less?
            Yes: E2 (End) Approve by manager
            No: D4 <Question> Does the finance head approve?
                Yes: E3 (End) Approve by finance head
                No: E4 (End) Reject by finance head
        No: E1 (End) Reject: receipt missing
    No: continue at D3 (shown above)
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| amount 500, no receipt | `S > D1(No) > D3(Yes) > E2` | Approve by manager |
| amount 501, no receipt | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(No) > E1` | Reject: receipt missing |
| amount 5000, receipt attached | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > D3(Yes) > E2` | Approve by manager |
| amount 5001, receipt attached, finance head says no | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > D3(No) > D4(No) > E4` | Reject by finance head |
| amount 9000, receipt attached, finance head says yes | `S > D1(Yes) > D2(Yes) > D3(No) > D4(Yes) > E3` | Approve by finance head |

#### Q8: Complaint ticket

**How to build it.** Urgency chooses the target (two actions), then both branches join at the same action 'agent works, read reply, add 1 to rounds'. The loop returns to that join action. Escalation happens on the third No, which is why the count is checked after a No only.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Is the ticket urgent?
    Yes: P1 [Action] Assign to a senior agent, target 4 hours
    -> P3 [Action] Agent works on the ticket; read the customer reply; add 1 to rounds
    -> D2 <Question> Did the customer confirm it is solved?
        Yes: E1 (End) Closed
        No: D3 <Question> Have 3 rounds been used?
            Yes: E2 (End) Escalated to manager
            No: continue at P3 (shown above)
    No: P2 [Action] Assign to a normal agent, target 24 hours
    -> continue at P3 (shown above)
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| urgent; customer replies: not solved, solved | `S > D1(Yes) > P1 > P3 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P3 > D2(Yes) > E1` | Closed |
| not urgent; replies: not solved, not solved, not solved | `S > D1(No) > P2 > P3 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P3 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P3 > D2(No) > D3(Yes) > E2` | Escalated to manager |
| not urgent; replies: solved | `S > D1(No) > P2 > P3 > D2(Yes) > E1` | Closed |

#### Q9: Bonus decision

**How to build it.** This is a decision tree: no loops, six outcomes drawn as three end boxes reused by several arrows. Check warnings first because it overrides everything else. Then split on rating (4 or 5, exactly 3, other) and then on tenure inside each branch.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Does the employee have any warnings?
    Yes: E3 (End) No bonus
    No: D2 <Question> Is the rating 4 or 5?
        Yes: D3 <Question> Is tenure 2 years or more?
            Yes: E1 (End) Full bonus
            No: E2 (End) Half bonus
        No: D4 <Question> Is the rating exactly 3?
            Yes: D5 <Question> Is tenure 5 years or more?
                Yes: continue at E2 (shown above)
                No: continue at E3 (shown above)
            No: continue at E3 (shown above)
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| rating 4, tenure 2, no warnings | `S > D1(No) > D2(Yes) > D3(Yes) > E1` | Full bonus |
| rating 4, tenure 1, no warnings | `S > D1(No) > D2(Yes) > D3(No) > E2` | Half bonus |
| rating 3, tenure 5, no warnings | `S > D1(No) > D2(No) > D4(Yes) > D5(Yes) > E2` | Half bonus |
| rating 5, tenure 10, one warning | `S > D1(Yes) > E3` | No bonus |
| rating 3, tenure 4, no warnings | `S > D1(No) > D2(No) > D4(Yes) > D5(No) > E3` | No bonus |

#### Q10: Order fulfilment

**How to build it.** Two gates before the payment loop (stock) and one question after it (express). The loop retries the payment action; the count is checked only after a failure. Note that the end boxes for 'express' and 'standard' sit after the payment succeeded, so they can only be reached through the Yes arrow of 'did the payment go through'.

**Flowchart (text outline):**

```text
S (Start) Start
-> D1 <Question> Are all items in stock?
    Yes: P0 [Action] Set attempts = 0
    -> P1 [Action] Take the payment; add 1 to attempts
    -> D2 <Question> Did the payment go through?
        Yes: D4 <Question> Did the customer choose express delivery?
            Yes: E3 (End) Ship express
            No: E4 (End) Ship standard
        No: D3 <Question> Have 3 attempts been used?
            Yes: E2 (End) Order cancelled
            No: continue at P1 (shown above)
    No: E1 (End) Backorder
```

**Trace of the test cases:**

| Input | Path through the chart | Result |
|---|---|---|
| an item is out of stock | `S > D1(No) > E1` | Backorder |
| in stock; payments: fail, fail, fail | `S > D1(Yes) > P0 > P1 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P1 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P1 > D2(No) > D3(Yes) > E2` | Order cancelled |
| in stock; payments: fail, success; express chosen | `S > D1(Yes) > P0 > P1 > D2(No) > D3(No) > P1 > D2(Yes) > D4(Yes) > E3` | Ship express |
| in stock; payment succeeds first time; express not chosen | `S > D1(Yes) > P0 > P1 > D2(Yes) > D4(No) > E4` | Ship standard |

### Notes on verification

Each flowchart was coded as a small graph and run on many input combinations, comparing the outcome with a direct if/else version of the written rule: Q1 5, Q2 5, Q3 24, Q4 180, Q5 32, Q6 8, Q7 24, Q8 16, Q9 60, Q10 32 (no mismatches). Every traced path above was reproduced by running the graph. Worked examples 3 to 5 and both scenarios were checked the same way.
