## Chapter 18 answer key: Puzzles with Floors, Boxes, Days, Months and Schedules

Every puzzle (Q1 to Q3, Q7 to Q13, Q16 to Q20, Q23 to Q25 and worked examples 1, 2 and 5) was solved by brute force: all arrangements were generated and filtered by every clue, and in each case exactly one arrangement survived (Q4 to Q6, Q14, Q15, Q21 and Q22 were recomputed from calendars, clocks or a schedule search). The schedule answers (Q15, Q22, example 4, scenario 1) were checked by trying every task order and every assignment of tasks to workers. The calendar answers (Q4, Q14, Q21, example 3, scenario 2) were recomputed with a date library. In the reasoning below, "floor 1" is the lowest floor.

### Self-check answers

1. "A is directly above B" means A is exactly one floor higher than B. That is a special case of "A is above B", so **yes**, directly above always implies above. It does **not** work the other way: "A is above B" allows any gap (A could be 3 floors higher), so it does not mean directly above.
2. 10 days is 1 full week (7 days) plus 3 extra days. Wednesday plus 3 is Thursday, Friday, Saturday. **Saturday**.
3. The tasks are independent, so with two people one takes the 3-hour task and the other takes the 5-hour task at the same time. The job ends when the slower one ends: **5 hours** (one person alone would need 8 hours).

### Practice answers

**Q1** 🟢 Clue 1 puts Admin on floor 1. Floors 2, 3 and 4 are left for Payroll, Hiring and Training. Clue 3 ties Payroll and Hiring into a block (Payroll directly below Hiring), which fits floors 2 and 3, or floors 3 and 4. If the block is on floors 2 and 3, Training would be on floor 4, above Hiring, which breaks clue 2 (Hiring must be higher than Training). So the block is on floors 3 and 4 (Payroll 3, Hiring 4) and Training is on floor 2. Check: Hiring (4) is higher than Training (2); Payroll (3) is directly below Hiring (4). **Training is on floor 2.** (Full order: Admin 1, Training 2, Payroll 3, Hiring 4.)

**Q2** 🟢 Contracts are on shelf 5 (clue 1). Leave forms are on shelf 2 (clue 2). Offer letters are directly above Leave forms, so on shelf 3 (clue 3). Shelves 1 and 4 are left for Payslips and Exit papers. Clue 4 says Payslips are higher than Exit papers, so Payslips take shelf 4 and Exit papers take shelf 1. **Exit papers are on shelf 1.**

**Q3** 🟢 Grid of items against boxes. The diary cannot be in green (clue 2) or blue (clue 4), so the diary is in the **red** box. The ribbon cannot be red (clue 1), and red is now taken anyway. The stapler cannot be in blue (clue 3) and cannot be in red (taken), so the stapler is in green. The ribbon takes the last box, blue. Check: ribbon not red, diary not green, stapler not blue, diary not blue. All hold. **The blue box holds the ribbon.**

**Q4** 🟢 Counting 10 days forward from Tuesday: 10 is 1 week plus 3 days, so move 3 weekdays: Wednesday, Thursday, Friday. The due day is Friday. The holiday is the day before the due day, which is **Thursday**.

**Q5** 🟢 "7 months after April" means counting on from May: May 1, June 2, July 3, August 4, September 5, October 6, November 7. Priya joined in November. Three months after November: December, January, February. Her probation ends in **February** (the year changes after December).

**Q6** 🟢 Add the durations step by step. 9:20 plus 25 minutes is 9:45 (end of packing). Plus 15 minutes is 10:00 (end of labelling). Plus 10 minutes is 10:10 (end of the break). Plus 40 minutes is 10:50. Total duration is 25 + 15 + 10 + 40 = 90 minutes, and 9:20 plus 90 minutes is 10:50. **10:50.**

**Q7** 🟢 Kabir is not on Wednesday (clue 3). Lata is not on Monday (clue 2). Meera is before Kabir (clue 1), so Kabir cannot be on Monday, which leaves Tuesday for Kabir (Wednesday is barred). Meera, being before Kabir, is on Monday. Lata takes Wednesday. Check: Meera (Mon) is before Kabir (Tue); Lata not Monday; Kabir not Wednesday. **Lata is interviewed on Wednesday.**

**Q8** 🟢 Chain the clues: S is heavier than P (clue 3), P is heavier than Q (clue 1), Q is heavier than R (clue 2). That gives one unbroken chain S > P > Q > R. **S, P, Q, R** (heaviest to lightest).

**Q9** 🟡 Stores is directly above Accounts (clue 3), so Accounts is directly below Stores. IT must be adjacent to Stores (clue 1): that is either the floor above Stores or the floor below Stores. The floor below Stores is Accounts, so IT is directly above Stores. Legal is adjacent to IT (clue 4): the floor below IT is Stores, so Legal is directly above IT. This builds a block of four from the bottom: Accounts, Stores, IT, Legal. Sales is higher than IT (clue 2), so Sales cannot be below the block; the block occupies floors 1 to 4 and Sales takes floor 5. Order: Accounts 1, Stores 2, IT 3, Legal 4, Sales 5. All clues check. **Floor 1: Accounts. Floor 4: Legal.**

**Q10** 🟡 Sales is directly above HR (clue 4). Stores is adjacent to Sales (clue 1); the floor below Sales is HR, so Stores is directly above Sales. This gives a block going up: HR, Sales, Stores on floors k, k+1, k+2. Accounts is below HR (clue 5), so k is at least 2. Try each k.
- k = 4 would need floors 4 to 6 for the block, leaving no floor above HR for Legal (clue 2). Impossible.
- k = 3: block on floors 3 to 5. Legal must be above HR, so Legal is on floor 6, which is adjacent to Stores on floor 5. That breaks clue 3. Impossible.
- k = 2: block on floors 2 to 4, Accounts on floor 1. Floors 5 and 6 are left for Legal and IT. Legal cannot be on floor 5 (adjacent to Stores on floor 4, clue 3), so Legal is on 6 and IT on 5.
Check: Accounts 1, HR 2, Sales 3, Stores 4, IT 5, Legal 6. All five clues hold. **IT is on floor 5.**

**Q11** 🟡 Clue 5: blue holds the cables. Clue 4: the cables box is immediately left of the red box. Clue 1: the red box is immediately left of the mugs box. So three boxes in a row, left to right: blue (cables), red, mugs-box. Blue is at position 1 or 2.
- If blue is at 2: red at 3, mugs-box at 4. The mugs box must be next to the green box (clue 2), so green would be at 3, but red is there. Impossible.
- So blue is at 1, red at 2, and the mugs box is at position 3. The badges box is next to blue (clue 3), so badges are at position 2 (the red box). The files are at position 4. The mugs box (position 3) must be next to green (clue 2), so green is at position 4 (position 2 is red). The mugs box at position 3 is therefore the remaining colour, yellow.
Final: 1 blue cables, 2 red badges, 3 yellow mugs, 4 green files. **The yellow box holds the mugs, and the box at position 4 is green.**

**Q12** 🟡 Try each box as the key box and count how many labels are true (exactly one must be).
- Key in box 1: label 1 false (key is not in 2 or 3), label 2 false, label 3 true, label 4 true. Two true. No.
- Key in box 2: label 1 true, label 2 true, label 3 true, label 4 false. Three true. No.
- Key in box 3: label 1 true, label 2 false, label 3 false, label 4 true. Two true. No.
- Key in box 4: label 1 false, label 2 false, label 3 true ("not in this box" is true because the key is elsewhere), label 4 false. Exactly one true. Yes.
Assumption: exactly one box holds the key, as the question says. **The key is in box 4.**

**Q13** 🟡 Dosa is the day right after Upma (clue 2). Upma and Paratha are on consecutive days (clue 1); the day after Upma is taken by Dosa, so Paratha must be the day **before** Upma. This gives Paratha, Upma, Dosa in three consecutive days. Idli is next to Paratha (clue 3); the day after Paratha is Upma, so Idli is the day before Paratha. Poha is next to Idli (clue 4); the day after Idli is Paratha, so Poha is the day before Idli. The full chain: Poha, Idli, Paratha, Upma, Dosa, which exactly fills Monday to Friday. Monday Poha, Tuesday Idli, Wednesday Paratha, Thursday Upma, Friday Dosa. **Wednesday: Paratha.**

**Q14** 🟡 Months in July to December with 31 days: July, August, October, December. Check the months on either side.
- July: before it is June (30 days, good), after it is August (31 days, fails).
- August: before it is July (31 days), fails.
- October: before it is September (30), after it is November (30). Passes.
- December: before it is November (30), after it is January (31). Fails.
The month is **October**. If 1 October is a Wednesday, 31 October is 30 days later. 30 is 4 weeks plus 2 days, so move 2 weekdays: Thursday, Friday. The last day is a **Friday**.

**Q15** 🟡 One person: 2 + 3 + 2 + 4 + 3 = **14 hours**. For two people, find the waiting chains. A then C then E takes 2 + 2 + 3 = 7 hours; A then D takes 6; B then E takes 6. The longest chain (critical path) is 7, so nobody can do better than 7 hours. Is 7 reachable with two people? Person 1 does A (0 to 2), C (2 to 4), E (4 to 7). Person 2 does B (0 to 3), then D (3 to 7), because D needs A, which finished at hour 2. E starts at hour 4 and needs B (done at 3) and C (done at 4), so it can. Everything finishes at hour 7. Also total work 14 divided by 2 workers is 7, which agrees. **One person: 14 hours. Two people: 7 hours.**

**Q16** 🟡 Budget is later than Audit (clue 2) and later than Launch (clue 4). Hiring is before Audit (clue 1: Audit starts one hour after Hiring), so Hiring is also before Budget. Budget is later than the other three, so Budget is in the last slot, 12. Launch and Budget are in consecutive slots (clue 3), so Launch is at 11. Hiring and Audit take 9 and 10 with Audit one hour after Hiring: Hiring 9, Audit 10. All clues check. **Launch starts at 11.**

**Q17** 🟡 Ravi is not on Afternoon or Night (clues 1, 2), so Ravi is on Morning or Evening. Tej is also not on Afternoon or Night (clues 3, 4), so Tej is on Morning or Evening. Ravi and Tej therefore fill Morning and Evening between them, and Sana and Uma fill Afternoon and Night between them. Now use the "if" clues.
- Suppose Ravi is on Morning. Clue 5 says Sana is on Night, then clue 6 says Uma is on Evening. But Evening belongs to Tej in this case (Ravi has Morning). Contradiction. So Ravi is not on Morning.
- Ravi is on Evening and Tej is on Morning.
- Sana and Uma share Afternoon and Night. If Sana were on Night, clue 6 would put Uma on Evening, which is Ravi's. Contradiction. So Sana is on Afternoon and Uma is on Night.
Check: clue 5 is "if Ravi is on Morning": Ravi is not, so it asks for nothing. Clue 6 is "if Sana is on Night": Sana is not, so it asks for nothing. **Tej Morning, Sana Afternoon, Ravi Evening, Uma Night.**

**Q18** 🟡 Bhanu can do only Invoice, so Bhanu does Invoice. Remove Invoice from everyone else's options: Asha is left with only Filing, so Asha does Filing. Remove Filing: Chitra has Calls or Email, Dev has only Calls (Invoice is taken), so Dev does Calls. That leaves Email for Chitra. Check: each person has a task they can do and all four tasks are used. **Chitra does Email. Asha: Filing. Bhanu: Invoice. Dev: Calls.**

**Q19** 🔴 Work with HR at floor h. Stores is exactly two floors above HR (clue 1), so Stores is on h+2. Sales is adjacent to HR (clue 4), so Sales is on h+1 or h-1. Accounts is higher than Sales (clue 5). Legal is adjacent to Accounts (clue 2). IT is higher than Legal but not adjacent (clues 3 and 6), so IT is at least two floors above Legal.
- Case Sales on h+1: HR, Sales, Stores are floors h, h+1, h+2.
  - h = 1: floors 4, 5, 6 are left for Accounts, Legal, IT. IT is at least two above Legal, so Legal 4, IT 6, Accounts 5. Accounts (5) is above Sales (2), and Legal (4) is adjacent to Accounts (5). Works.
  - h = 2: block on floors 2 to 4, floors 1, 5, 6 left. Accounts must be above Sales (3), so 5 or 6, and Legal must be adjacent. Accounts 5 gives Legal 4 or 6 (4 is taken, 6 leaves IT on floor 1, not above Legal). Accounts 6 gives Legal 5, and IT has only floor 1. Fails.
  - h = 3: block on 3 to 5, floors 1, 2, 6 left. Accounts above Sales (4) must be floor 6, but then Legal must be on floor 5, which is taken. Fails.
  - h = 4 would need Stores on floor 6 and Sales on floor 5, leaving floors 1 to 3 for Accounts, Legal and IT with Accounts above Sales (5): impossible.
- Case Sales on h-1 (floors h-1, h, and h+2 used): h = 2, 3 or 4.
  - h = 2: Sales 1, HR 2, Stores 4; floors 3, 5, 6 left. Legal and Accounts must be adjacent: 5 and 6 only, leaving IT on floor 3, below them. Fails (IT must be above Legal).
  - h = 3: Sales 2, HR 3, Stores 5; floors 1, 4, 6 left. No two are adjacent, so Legal and Accounts cannot be adjacent. Fails.
  - h = 4: Sales 3, HR 4, Stores 6; floors 1, 2, 5 left. Accounts must be above Sales (floor 3), so floor 5, but then Legal would need floor 4 or 6, both taken. Fails.
Only one case works: HR 1, Sales 2, Stores 3, Legal 4, Accounts 5, IT 6. **Floor 1: HR. Floor 4: Legal.**

**Q20** 🔴 The blue box holds the mugs (clue 6). The yellow box is next to the mugs box, so next to blue (clue 4). The badges box is next to yellow (clue 5) and cannot be the blue box (blue holds mugs), so the badges box is on the other side of yellow. So three in a row: either blue, yellow, badges-box, or badges-box, yellow, blue.
- Order badges-box, yellow, blue: blue is somewhere left of red (clue 7), so red is right of blue, which means blue is at 3 and red at 4. Red is immediately left of the Delhi box (clue 2), but nothing is right of position 4. Also blue at 2 is not possible here. Fails.
- Order blue, yellow, badges-box: blue at 1 gives yellow 2 and badges-box 3; blue at 2 gives yellow 3 and badges-box 4. In the second case red can only be at position 4 or 1, and blue must be left of red, so red is at 4, which again cannot be left of Delhi. Fails. So blue 1, yellow 2, badges-box 3.
- Red is right of blue and must have a box to its right, so red is at 3 (the badges box) and green is at 4. Items: mugs 1, badges 3, so files and cables go to positions 2 and 4. Clue 3: the files box is immediately left of the Pune box, so files cannot be at 4; files are at 2 and cables at 4. Pune is position 3. Files go to Kochi (clue 1), so position 2 is Kochi. Red (3) is immediately left of Delhi, so Delhi is position 4. Goa is position 1.
Final: 1 blue mugs Goa, 2 yellow files Kochi, 3 red badges Pune, 4 green cables Delhi. **The green box goes to Delhi, and it holds the cables.**

**Q21** 🔴 The year is not a leap year, so February has 28 days. A 31-day month moves the same date forward 3 weekdays, a 30-day month 2, and February (28 days) 0. Moving the 13th from month to month, with February's 13th as offset 0 (offset = how many weekdays after February's 13th):
- February 0. March 0 (February has 28 days, shift 0). April 3 (March has 31 days). May 5 (April has 30 days, shift 2). June 1 (May has 31 days: 5 + 3 = 8, which is 1 after removing a full week). July 3 (June, shift 2). August 6 (July, shift 3). September 2 (August: 6 + 3 = 9, minus 7). October 4 (September, shift 2). November 0 (October: 4 + 3 = 7, which is 0). December 2 (November, shift 2).
- January is the month before February and has 31 days, so January's 13th is 3 weekdays before February's: offset -3, the same as +4.
Offsets by month: Jan 4, Feb 0, Mar 0, Apr 3, May 5, Jun 1, Jul 3, Aug 6, Sep 2, Oct 4, Nov 0, Dec 2. Months with the same offset share a weekday for their 13th. The offset 6 belongs to August alone. So "August is the only month with a Friday 13th" means August's 13th is a Friday and no other offset class is Friday, which fits: offset 6 is the only class with a single month.
- February's 13th is 6 weekdays before August's Friday, which is Saturday. January's 13th (offset 4) is 4 weekdays after that Saturday: Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday. So 13 January is a Wednesday. 1 January is 12 days earlier; 12 days is 1 week plus 5, so go back 5 weekdays from Wednesday: Tuesday, Monday, Sunday, Saturday, Friday. **1 January is a Friday.**
- December's 13th has offset 2, so 2 weekdays after Saturday: Monday. 25 December is 12 days later, which is 1 week plus 5, so Monday plus 5: Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday. **25 December is a Saturday.**
Cross-check: a date library confirms that every non-leap year whose only Friday 13th is in August starts on a Friday and has 25 December on a Saturday (and that such a year exists, for example 1909).

**Q22** 🔴 Total work is 2 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 5 + 3 = 19 hours. Waiting chains: A then C then F is 2 + 3 + 3 = 8; A then D then F is 2 + 2 + 3 = 7; B then E is 4 + 5 = 9. The critical path is 9 hours, so no number of people can beat 9.
- **Three people: 9 hours.** Person 1: B (0 to 4), E (4 to 9). Person 2: A (0 to 2), C (2 to 5), F (5 to 8) (F needs C at hour 5 and D at hour 4). Person 3: D (2 to 4), after A has finished at hour 2. Everything is done by hour 9. This matches the critical path, so it is the best possible.
- **Two people: 10 hours.** Lower bound: 19 hours of work shared by 2 people is 9.5, so at least 10 hours. 10 is reachable: Person 1 does B (0 to 4), E (4 to 9). Person 2 does A (0 to 2), C (2 to 5), D (5 to 7), F (7 to 10); F needs C and D, both done by hour 7. Finish at hour 10. Brute force over all task orders and assignments confirms 9 is impossible with two people.
**Two people: 10 hours. Three people: 9 hours.** (A fourth person would not help: still 9.)

**Q23** 🔴 Clue 3 says Bala does not do Payroll, Training, Onboarding or Reports. Of the five tasks only Hiring is left, so **Bala does Hiring**. Clue 2 says if Bala does Hiring then Asha does Payroll, so Asha does Payroll. Clue 1 says if Asha does Payroll then Esha does Training, so Esha does Training. That leaves Onboarding and Reports for Chitra and Dev. Clue 4 says Dev does not do Onboarding, so Dev does Reports and Chitra does Onboarding. Check all four clues. They hold. **Dev does Reports. Chitra does Onboarding.**

**Q24** 🔴 Short names: A = Anil, B = Bina, C = Charu, D = Dilip, E = Esha. Days Monday to Saturday are 1 to 6, with one empty day (gap, G).
- Clue 6: Dilip is right after Anil, so A then D form a block X. Clue 3: Bina is right after Esha, so E then B form a block Y.
- Clue 1 (Anil and Bina not consecutive): in the order Y then X, Bina would be directly next to Anil, which is forbidden. Clue 2 (Esha and Dilip not consecutive): in the order X then Y, Dilip would be directly next to Esha, which is forbidden. So X and Y can never touch; a gap or Charu must separate them.
- Clue 4: Charu is earlier than Dilip, so Charu is before block X. Clue 5 forbids Charu directly before Anil, so Charu is not immediately before X. Clue 7 forbids Charu directly after Bina, so Charu is not immediately after Y.
- If Charu sat between Y and X, she would be right after Bina or right before Anil. Both forbidden. So the separator between Y and X, or X and Y, must be the gap G, and it cannot be Charu.
- If Y comes before X, the order is Y, G, X with Charu somewhere before X. Charu cannot go between Y and X, cannot go after X (she must be before Dilip), and cannot go directly after Y. So she goes first: C, Y, G, X.
- If X comes before Y, Charu is before X, and G must separate X and Y: C, X, G, Y puts Charu right before Anil (forbidden); G, C, X, Y has no separator between X and Y. Neither works.
So the only schedule is C, E, B, G, A, D: Monday Charu, Tuesday Esha, Wednesday Bina, Thursday no delivery, Friday Anil, Saturday Dilip. Check all seven clues: Anil (5) and Bina (3) not consecutive; Esha (2) and Dilip (6) not consecutive; Bina (3) is right after Esha (2); Dilip (6) is later than Charu (1); Anil (5) and Charu (1) not consecutive; Dilip (6) is right after Anil (5); Charu (1) and Bina (3) not consecutive. **No delivery on Thursday; Bina is served on Wednesday.**

**Q25** 🔴 Clue 3: A + C = E. The two smallest different weights add to 1 + 2 = 3, so E is at least 3. Try each value of E.
- E = 3: A and C are 1 and 2, so B and D are 4 and 5. Clue 2 needs A + E > B + C. A + E is at most 2 + 3 = 5, but B + C is at least 4 + 1 = 5. Not strictly greater. Fails.
- E = 5: A and C are {1, 4} or {2, 3}, so B and D are the other two. Clue 1 needs B + D > C + E. Either way B + D is 5 (when {A, C} = {1, 4}, B and D are 2 and 3) or 5 (when {A, C} = {2, 3}, B and D are 1 and 4), but C + E is at least 6. Fails.
- E = 4: A and C are 1 and 3, so B and D are 2 and 5. Clue 1: B + D = 7 must beat C + 4, so C is 1 (not 3), so A is 3. Clue 4: B + 4 > 3 + D. If B = 2 and D = 5: 6 > 8 is false. If B = 5 and D = 2: 9 > 5 is true. So B = 5, D = 2.
Result: A = 3, B = 5, C = 1, D = 2, E = 4. Check: B + D = 7 > C + E = 5; A + E = 7 > B + C = 6; A + C = 4 = E; B + E = 9 > A + D = 5. All hold, and no other assignment survives. **Box E weighs 4 kg.**

## Chapter 19 answer key: Truth-Teller and Liar Puzzles

Every puzzle in the chapter (all 5 solved examples, both scenarios and all 20 practice questions) was checked by script that tried every possible labelling of truth-tellers and liars (together with every possible version of the facts, such as culprit, day or number) and confirmed that exactly one combination fits. Q19 was checked over all 3 x 3 x 3 = 27 assignments of the three kinds, and Q13 and Q20 over all 7 days. Short notation below: T = truth-teller, L = liar.

### Self-check answers

1. A liar's statement is false. "It rained yesterday" is false, so it **did not rain** yesterday.
2. Tomas tells the truth, so "Uma is a liar" is true and Uma is a liar. A liar's statement is false, so "The shop opens at 8" is false: the shop **does not open at 8**.
3. It is a **contradiction**, so the assumption "Ravi is a truth-teller" was wrong. (If nothing else could be wrong, Ravi must be a liar, but you still check that.)

### Easy

**Q1** 🟢 Omar is a liar, so his statement is false. "The refund was paid twice" is false. **No, it was not paid twice.**

**Q2** 🟢 Chen says Divya and he are both T.
- If Chen is T, then Divya is T too. But Divya says "Chen is a liar", which would then be false, and a T cannot say a false statement. Contradiction.
- So Chen is L. His statement "we are both truth-tellers" is then false, which fits (Chen is an L). Divya says "Chen is a liar", and Chen is an L, so this is true, so Divya is T.
**Chen is a liar and Divya is a truth-teller.**

**Q3** 🟢 Kiran says "Lena is a liar". Lena says "Exactly one of the two of us is a liar."
- Assume Kiran is T. Then Lena is L. Lena's statement "exactly one of us is a liar" would be true (Lena only), but an L cannot say something true. Contradiction.
- So Kiran is L. His statement is false, so Lena is T. Check Lena: Kiran is L and Lena is T, so exactly one is a liar: true. Fits a T.
**Lena is the truth-teller (Kiran is the liar).**

**Q4** 🟢 Priya is T, so the shortfall is in Sita's or Tom's ledger (so not Raj's). Quinn is L, so "in Tom's ledger" is false, so not Tom's. The only one left is Sita. **Sita's ledger.**

**Q5** 🟢 Hugo is L. Gita says "Hugo is a truth-teller", which is false, so Gita is L. Farah says "Gita is a liar", which is true, so Farah is T. Emeka says "Farah is a truth-teller", which is true, so Emeka is T. **Emeka and Farah are truth-tellers (Gita and Hugo are liars).**

**Q6** 🟢 Test each possible copier and count the false statements (the liars).

| Copier | Emeka ("Farah") | Farah ("Gita") | Gita ("Farah is lying") | Liars |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Emeka | false | false | Farah's statement is false, so Farah is lying: true | 2 |
| Farah | true | false | Farah is lying: true | 1 |
| Gita | false | true | Farah is truthful, so "Farah is lying" is false | 2 |

Only the Farah row has exactly one liar (Farah). **Farah copied the file.**

**Q7** 🟢 Exactly two statements are true. The statements are: more than 6; multiple of 3; exactly 12.
- If Chen (exactly 12) is true, then the number is 12. Then "more than 6" is true and "multiple of 3" is true, so all three are true: no liar. Breaks the clue.
- So Chen is the liar and Alma and Bruno are truthful: more than 6 and a multiple of 3, but not 12. The multiples of 3 up to 12 that exceed 6 are 9 and 12, and without 12 we have 9.
- Check: 9 is more than 6 (true), a multiple of 3 (true), not 12 (so Chen's statement is false). Exactly one liar.
**9 invoices.**

### Medium

**Q8** 🟡 Test each possible entrant (the number of false statements must be exactly 1). Lena says "neither Mona nor Nikhil", Mona says "Lena or Nikhil", Nikhil says "not Lena", Ola says "me".

| Entrant | Lena | Mona | Nikhil | Ola | False statements |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lena | true | true | false | false | 2 |
| Mona | false | false | true | false | 3 |
| Nikhil | false | true | true | false | 2 |
| Ola | true | false | true | true | 1 |

Only the Ola row has exactly one false statement, and it is Mona's. **Ola entered; Mona is the liar.**

**Q9** 🟡 Test each possible person (exactly two liars, so exactly two false statements). Hugo says "Iris", Iris says "Hugo", Jan says "not me", Kiran says "Hugo is telling the truth" (true exactly when Hugo's statement is true).

| Culprit | Hugo | Iris | Jan | Kiran | False |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hugo | false | true | true | false | 2 |
| Iris | true | false | true | true | 1 |
| Jan | false | false | false | false | 4 |
| Kiran | false | false | true | false | 3 |

Only the Hugo row has exactly 2 false statements, and they are Hugo's and Kiran's. **Hugo altered the payment; Hugo and Kiran are the liars.**

**Q10** 🟡 Remember that a false "X and Y" only needs one false part. Priya and Quinn say "Hugo and Monday" and "Hugo and Tuesday". These cannot both be true (the day cannot be both). So at least one of Priya and Quinn is a liar, and since only one liar exists, Raj is truthful.
- Raj is truthful: "Iris or Monday" is true.
- Case A: Priya is the liar and Quinn is truthful. Then Hugo and Tuesday are true. Raj: "Iris or Monday": Iris is not the forger (it was Hugo), and Monday is not the day (it was Tuesday): false. But Raj must be truthful. Contradiction.
- Case B: Quinn is the liar and Priya is truthful. Then Hugo and Monday are true. Raj: "Iris or Monday": Monday is true, so true. Quinn: "Hugo and Tuesday" is false (Tuesday is wrong), which fits a liar. Consistent.
**Hugo forged it, on Monday; Quinn is the liar.** (Note that Quinn's false statement still had a true part, "Hugo".)

**Q11** 🟡 The statements are "at least k of us are liars" for k = 1, 2, 3, 4. Let L be the number of liars. Statement k is true exactly when k is at most L. So the truth-tellers are the ones with k = 1 up to k = L, which makes L truth-tellers. But there are 4 people, so L liars plus L truth-tellers gives 2L = 4, so L = 2. Then the statements k = 1 and k = 2 are true, and k = 3 and k = 4 are false. Check: Quinn (3) and Raj (4) are liars (2 liars), and Ola and Priya are truth-tellers. Other values of L fail: for L = 1, 1 liar needs 3 truth-tellers but only 1 statement is true; for L = 3, 3 liars need 1 truth-teller but 3 statements are true; L = 0 or 4 fail similarly. **Quinn and Raj are the liars.**

**Q12** 🟡 Whatever anyone is, the honest answer to "Are you a truth-teller?" is yes, and a liar lies about it and also says yes. So nobody could have told the investigator "I am a liar", and Nikhil did not tell Mona that. Mona's statement is false, so Mona is a liar. Nikhil says "Mona is a truth-teller", which is false, so Nikhil is a liar as well. Check: both are liars and both statements are false. **Neither is a truth-teller (both are liars).**

**Q13** 🟡 Gita lies on Mon, Wed, Fri. Her statement "Tomorrow I will tell the truth" is true when tomorrow is a truthful day (Tue, Thu, Sat, Sun) and false when tomorrow is a lying day (Mon, Wed, Fri).

| Day | Gita that day | Tomorrow | Statement is | Needed | Fits? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mon | liar | Tue (truth) | true | false | no |
| Tue | truthful | Wed (lie) | false | true | no |
| Wed | liar | Thu (truth) | true | false | no |
| Thu | truthful | Fri (lie) | false | true | no |
| Fri | liar | Sat (truth) | true | false | no |
| Sat | truthful | Sun (truth) | true | true | yes |
| Sun | truthful | Mon (lie) | false | true | no |

**Saturday.**

**Q14** 🟡 Sana says "exactly 2 of Tej, Uma, Vik are liars". Tej says "Sana, Uma and Vik are all truthful". Uma says "Sana, Tej and Vik are all liars". Vik says "exactly 2 of Sana, Tej, Uma are liars".
- Assume Tej is T. Then Uma is T too, but Uma says Tej is a liar. Contradiction. So Tej is L.
- Assume Uma is T. Then Sana, Tej and Vik are all L. Sana is L, so her statement must be false. But among Tej, Uma, Vik the liars are Tej and Vik, exactly 2, so her statement is true. Contradiction. So Uma is L.
- Now Tej and Uma are liars. Sana's statement is true exactly when Vik is T (then the liars among Tej, Uma, Vik are exactly 2). Vik's statement is true exactly when Sana is T. So Sana and Vik are the same kind.
- If Sana and Vik were both L, then Sana, Tej and Vik would all be liars, and Uma's statement would be true. But Uma is L. Contradiction. So Sana and Vik are both T.
- Check: Sana T (Tej and Uma are the 2 liars among Tej, Uma, Vik: true), Tej L (false), Uma L (false), Vik T (true).
**Tej and Uma are the liars (Sana and Vik are truth-tellers).**

### Hard

**Q15** 🔴 Write the statements: Alma (Divya T), Bruno (Chen and Emeka both L), Chen (Bruno T), Divya (Emeka T), Emeka (Alma or Chen is a liar).
- Chen and Bruno vouch for each other, so they are the same kind (Bruno T if and only if Chen T, from both statements). If both were T, Bruno's statement "Chen and Emeka are both liars" would require Chen to be a liar. Contradiction. So Bruno and Chen are both L.
- Bruno is L, so "Chen and Emeka are both liars" is false. Chen is L, so that means Emeka is not an L: Emeka is T.
- Emeka is T and says "at least one of Alma and Chen is a liar": true, because Chen is L. Fine, but it does not decide Alma.
- Divya says "Emeka is a truth-teller", which is true, so Divya is T. Alma says "Divya is a truth-teller", which is true, so Alma is T.
- Check all: Alma T (true), Bruno L (false: Emeka is T), Chen L (false: Bruno is L, not T), Divya T, Emeka T. All fit.
**Alma, Divya and Emeka are truth-tellers (Bruno and Chen are liars).**

**Q16** 🔴 Test each possible person (exactly 2 false statements). Chen says "neither Alma nor Bruno". Divya's statement is true exactly when Chen's is. Emeka's is true exactly when Divya's is.

| Approver | Alma ("Emeka") | Bruno ("Divya") | Chen | Divya (= Chen) | Emeka (= Divya) | False |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Alma | false | false | false | false | false | 5 |
| Bruno | false | false | false | false | false | 5 |
| Chen | false | false | true | true | true | 2 |
| Divya | false | true | true | true | true | 1 |
| Emeka | true | false | true | true | true | 1 |

Only the Chen row has exactly 2 false statements. **Chen approved the refund; Alma and Bruno are the liars.**

**Q17** 🔴 Let L be the number of liars. A statement "exactly k are liars" is true only if k = L. So the number of truth-tellers equals the number of people who said k = L, and it must equal 6 minus L.

| L | Who says k = L | Truth-tellers | Needed (6 minus L) | Fits? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0, 1, 2 | nobody | 0 | 6, 5, 4 | no |
| 3 | Alma, Chen, Farah | 3 | 3 | yes |
| 4 | nobody | 0 | 2 | no |
| 5 | Bruno, Emeka | 2 | 1 | no |
| 6 | Divya | 1 | 0 | no |

**3 liars; the truth-tellers are Alma, Chen and Farah** (Bruno, Divya and Emeka are the liars).

**Q18** 🔴 Each pair has one T and one L. Test each possible thief. Statements: Alma "not Iris", Bruno "Hugo", Chen "not Hugo", Emeka "Jan", Divya's statement is true exactly when Farah is T, Farah's is true exactly when Alma is L.
- Billing pair: Alma and Bruno must differ in truth. Alma ("not Iris") and Bruno ("Hugo") are both true if Hugo did it, so Hugo cannot be the thief. If Iris did it, Alma false, Bruno false: both liars, breaks the pair rule. So the thief is Jan: Alma true (so T), Bruno false (so L). The pair is fine.
- Payroll: Chen says "not Hugo", true since Jan did it, so Chen is T and then Divya is L.
- Stores: Emeka says "Jan" which is true, so Emeka is T and Farah is L.
- Check the remaining statements: Divya (L) says "Farah is a truth-teller", which is false (Farah is L). Farah (L) says "Alma is a liar", which is false (Alma is T). All fit.
**Jan committed the theft. Alma T, Bruno L, Chen T, Divya L, Emeka T, Farah L.**

**Q19** 🔴 Exactly one person is each kind. Try each kind for Alma and read Bruno's statements: (1) "Alma is a switcher", (2) "Alma is not a truth-teller".
- Alma is a truth-teller: Bruno's (1) is false and (2) is false. Two false statements means Bruno is a liar, so Chen is the switcher. But Alma, a truth-teller, says "Bruno is a truth-teller", which is false. Contradiction.
- Alma is a liar: Bruno's (1) is false and (2) is true, a mixed pattern, so Bruno is a switcher and Chen is the truth-teller. But Chen says "Alma is a truth-teller", which is false. Contradiction.
- Alma is a switcher: Bruno's (1) and (2) are both true, so Bruno is a truth-teller and Chen is the liar.
- Check Chen (liar): "Alma is a truth-teller" is false and "Bruno is not a truth-teller" is false. Both false, fits. Check Alma (switcher): (1) "Bruno is a truth-teller" is true and (2) "Chen is a switcher" is false (true then false: opposite values, fits). Check Bruno: both true, fits.
**Alma is the switcher, Bruno the truth-teller, Chen the liar. Alma's first statement is true (her second is false).**

**Q20** 🔴 A day fits if both statements match the kind of day: on a truthful day both statements are true, on a lying day both are false. The lying days are Mon, Tue and Fri.

| Day | Kind of day | Yesterday was a lying day? | Tomorrow is a lying day? | Both statements | Needed | Fits? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mon | lying | Sun: no | Tue: yes | false, true | both false | no |
| Tue | lying | Mon: yes | Wed: no | true, false | both false | no |
| Wed | truthful | Tue: yes | Thu: no | true, false | both true | no |
| Thu | truthful | Wed: no | Fri: yes | false, true | both true | no |
| Fri | lying | Thu: no | Sat: no | false, false | both false | yes |
| Sat | truthful | Fri: yes | Sun: no | true, false | both true | no |
| Sun | truthful | Sat: no | Mon: yes | false, true | both true | no |

**Friday.**

## Chapter 20 answer key: Clocks and Calendars

All results were recomputed by script (exact fractions for clock times, Python's calendar for dates).

### Self-check answers
1. At 9:00 the hour hand is at 270° and the minute hand at 0° (the 12). The gap is 270°, which is more than 180°, so the angle is 360 - 270 = **90°**. Formula: |30 × 9 - 0| = 270, then 360 - 270 = 90.
2. **No.** 2100 ends in 00, so it is a century year. Century years are leap years only if divisible by 400. 2100 divided by 400 is 5.25, so 2100 is not a leap year.
3. 15 days = 2 full weeks (14 days) + 1 day. Only the remainder 1 matters: Tuesday + 1 = **Wednesday**.

### Practice answers

**Q1.** Hour hand at 8 × 30 = 240°, minute hand at 0°. The gap is 240°, above 180°, so 360 - 240 = **120°**.

**Q2.** The minute hand does a full 360° in 60 minutes: 360 / 60 = **6° per minute**. The hour hand does 360° in 12 hours = 720 minutes: 360 / 720 = **0.5° per minute**.

**Q3.** Hour hand: 30 × 5 + 0.5 × 30 = 165°. Minute hand: 6 × 30 = 180°. Gap = 180 - 165 = **15°**. Formula check: |150 - 165| = 15.

**Q4.** At 12:20, H = 12 counts as 0 on the dial. Hour hand: 0 + 0.5 × 20 = 10°. Minute hand: 6 × 20 = 120°. Gap = 120 - 10 = **110°**. Formula check with H = 0: |0 - 5.5 × 20| = 110.

**Q5.** 150 days: 150 / 7 = 21 weeks (147 days) with remainder 3. Going back 3 days from Tuesday: Monday (1), Sunday (2), **Saturday** (3). Python check: Saturday.

**Q6.** Rule: divisible by 4, but centuries must be divisible by 400.
- 1800: century, 1800 / 400 = 4.5, **not** a leap year.
- 2024: 2024 / 4 = 506, not a century, **leap year**.
- 2400: century, 2400 / 400 = 6, **leap year**.
- 2500: century, 2500 / 400 = 6.25, **not** a leap year.
Answer: **2024 and 2400**.

**Q7.** Days left in March after the 10th: 31 - 10 = 21 (this reaches 31 March). Then 25 more days in April. Total = 21 + 25 = **46 days**.

**Q8.** True time passed: 6 hours. The clock gains 2 minutes each hour, so it gains 6 × 2 = 12 minutes. It shows 6:00 pm + 12 minutes = **6:12 pm**.

**Q9.** 4 January + 30 days: January has 31 days, so 4 January + 27 days = 31 January, and 3 more days reach **3 February 2027**. Weekday: 30 = 4 weeks (28 days) + 2. Monday + 2 = **Wednesday**. So the answer is Wednesday 3 February 2027.

**Q10.** Hour hand: 30 × 9 + 0.5 × 12 = 276°. Minute hand: 6 × 12 = 72°. Gap = 276 - 72 = 204°, above 180°, so 360 - 204 = **156°**. Formula check: |270 - 66| = 204, then 156.

**Q11.** Hour hand: 30 × 1 + 0.5 × 48 = 54°. Minute hand: 6 × 48 = 288°. Gap = 288 - 54 = 234°, so 360 - 234 = **126°**. Formula check: |30 - 264| = 234, then 126.

**Q12.** At 2:00 the hour hand is 60° ahead. The minute hand gains 5.5° per minute. Time = 60 / 5.5 = 120/11 = 10 and 10/11 minutes. Answer: **2:10 and 10/11 minutes (about 2:10:55)**.

**Q13.** At 7:00 the hour hand is 210° ahead of the minute hand. The hands are opposite when the gap is 180°, so the gap must shrink from 210° to 180°. The minute hand must gain 210 - 180 = 30°. Time = 30 / 5.5 = 60/11 = 5 and 5/11 minutes. Answer: **7:05 and 5/11 minutes (about 7:05:27)**. Check: hour hand at 210 + 0.5 × 5.4545 = 212.73°; minute hand at 6 × 5.4545 = 32.73°; difference 180°.

**Q14.** True time passed: 6 hours. The clock loses 5 minutes each hour, so it loses 6 × 5 = 30 minutes. It shows 3:00 pm - 30 minutes = **2:30 pm**.

**Q15.** 26 November to 31 December: 4 days left in November (30 - 26) plus 31 days of December = 35 days. 35 = 5 weeks exactly, remainder 0. The weekday is the same: **Thursday**. (Python calendar: 31 Dec 2026 is a Thursday.)

**Q16.** 30 days = 4 full weeks (28 days) + 2 extra days. Every full week contains one Sunday, so 4 Sundays. The month starts on Saturday, so the 2 extra days (the 29th and 30th) are Saturday and Sunday. That gives one more Sunday. Total = **5 Sundays**.

**Q17.** From 20 January: 11 days remain in January (31 - 20), reaching 31 January. February (2027, 28 days): total 39. Remaining 60 - 39 = 21 days, so the date is **21 March 2027**. Weekday: 60 = 8 weeks (56 days) + 4. 20 January 2027 is a Wednesday (1 January 2027 is a Friday, 19 days later: 19 = 2 weeks + 5, Friday + 5 = Wednesday). Wednesday + 4 = **Sunday**. Python check: Sunday 21 March 2027. (A weekend due date: a finance team would need a rule for it.)

**Q18.** At 5:00 the hour hand is 150° ahead. The hands are 40° apart in two situations:
- Before they meet: the gap has shrunk from 150° to 40°. The minute hand gains 110°. Time = 110 / 5.5 = **20 minutes**, so 5:20.
- After they pass each other: the minute hand is 40° ahead. It must gain 150 + 40 = 190°. Time = 190 / 5.5 = 380/11 = **34 and 6/11 minutes**, so 5:34 and 6/11 minutes.
Check 5:20: hour hand 160°, minute hand 120°, gap 40°. Check 5:34 6/11: hour hand 150 + 17.27 = 167.27°, minute hand 6 × 34.545 = 207.27°, gap 40°.

**Q19.** The clock shows correct time on a 12-hour dial when its total gain is a whole multiple of 12 hours = 720 minutes (the first time is exactly 720). At 4 minutes per hour: 720 / 4 = 180 true hours. 180 hours = 7 days + 12 hours (7 × 24 = 168, remainder 12). Starting from midnight at the start of Sunday, that is **12:00 noon on the following Sunday, after 7 and a half days**. At that moment the clock has run 180 + 12 = 192 hours (exactly 8 days), so it shows 12:00 on its dial; the true time is 12:00 noon, which looks the same on a 12-hour dial.

**Q20.** The hands form a right angle when the gap is 90° or 270° (equivalent on the circle). The gap changes by 5.5° per minute, starting at 0° at midnight. The gap equals 90 + 360k or 270 + 360k at times t = (90 + 360k) / 5.5 or (270 + 360k) / 5.5 minutes. Counting those times inside 0 to 1440 minutes gives 44 (the gap goes through 1440 × 5.5 = 7920° in a day, which is 22 full turns, and each turn passes the 90° and 270° marks once). Answer: **44 times** (22 in each 12 hours). Script check by exact fractions: 44.

**Q21.** 1 January to 29 February 2028 (a leap year, so February has 29 days): January has 31 days, so 1 January + 31 days = 1 February, and 28 more days reach 29 February. Total 59 days. 59 = 8 weeks (56 days) + 3. Saturday + 3 = Sunday (1), Monday (2), **Tuesday** (3).

**Q22.** From 14 February 2025, 400 days later: 400 days = 365 + 35. One year later is 14 February 2026 (no 29 February falls between these two dates, so that year has 365 days). Then 35 more days: February 2026 has 28 days, so 14 Feb + 14 days = 28 Feb; 21 more days reach **21 March 2026**. Weekday: 400 = 57 weeks (399 days) + 1, so one day after the weekday of 14 February 2025. 14 February 2025 was a Friday, so the maturity day is **Saturday**. Python check: 21 March 2026, Saturday. Financial year (April to March): 21 March 2026 is before 1 April 2026, so it belongs to **FY 2025-26**.

**Q23.** Days from 1 January 2000 to 1 January 2026: 26 years. Leap years inside (2000, 2004, 2008, 2012, 2016, 2020, 2024) are 7. Days = 26 × 365 + 7 = 9490 + 7 = 9497. 9497 / 7 = 1356 weeks (9492 days) with remainder 5. Going back 5 days from Thursday: Wednesday (1), Tuesday (2), Monday (3), Sunday (4), **Saturday** (5). So 1 January 2000 was a **Saturday**.

**Q24.** Weekday of the 13th of each month, 2027. 13 January: 1 January is Friday, 12 days later is Friday + 5 (12 = 7 + 5) = Wednesday. Each next month's 13th shifts forward by the previous month's length mod 7 (31 gives +3, 30 gives +2, 28 gives +0):
- Jan 13: Wednesday. Feb 13: +3 = Saturday. Mar 13: +0 = Saturday. Apr 13: +3 = Tuesday. May 13: +2 = Thursday. Jun 13: +3 = Sunday. Jul 13: +2 = Tuesday. Aug 13: +3 = **Friday**. Sep 13: +3 = Monday. Oct 13: +2 = Wednesday. Nov 13: +3 = Saturday. Dec 13: +2 = Monday.
Only **August 2027** has a Friday the 13th. Script check: [8].

**Q25.** A mirror reflects each hand's position left to right. The mirror reading and true time add up to 12:00 on a 12-hour dial (12:00 minus the shown time). 12:00 - 8:20: borrow an hour: 11:60 - 8:20 = **3:40**. Check: true 3:40 has the hour hand at 110° and the minute hand at 240°. In a mirror these become 360 - 110 = 250° and 360 - 240 = 120°. 250° on the dial is between 8 and 9 (8:20 position, 250 / 30 = 8.33) and 120° is the 20-minute mark. So the mirror shows 8:20, which matches.

## Chapter 21 answer key: Statement-Assumption, Conclusion and Course of Action

Options for Q1 to Q34: (a) Only I, (b) Only II, (c) Both, (d) Neither. Every numeric result was recomputed by script.

### Self-check answers
1. **Yes.** Flip it: "Employees have no working email addresses." Then sending slips by email would be pointless. So it is a hidden but needed assumption.
2. **Yes.** 10 - 6 = 4, so 4 interns are not from commerce. It follows from the numbers alone.
3. **No.** Shutting the site for ever is extreme and does not fit the problem (slowness). A sensible step would be to find and fix the cause of the slowness.

### Practice answers

**Q1. Answer: (b).**
I: "All customers will order above Rs 999" is extreme; the offer only says orders above Rs 999 qualify. Not implicit.
II: Flip it: "Free delivery cannot attract any customer." Then the banner is pointless. Implicit.

**Q2. Answer: (c).**
I: Flip it: "Employees can never foresee leave 3 days ahead." Then the request is pointless. Implicit.
II: The reason for asking for early notice is to help planning. Flip: "Early notice does not help planning." The request loses its purpose. Implicit.

**Q3. Answer: (a).**
I: Flip it: "Nobody reads signs or takes care." The sign would be pointless. Implicit.
II: "Permanently" is extreme. The floor is slippery now, and the sign is a short-term step. Not implicit.

**Q4. Answer: (d).**
I: "All people who walk past love coffee" is extreme and not needed; a cafe only needs some customers.
II: A fixed daily income of Rs 5,000 is an invented result, not something the sign needs. Neither is implicit.

**Q5. Answer: (c).**
I: 12 - 8 = 4 employees. Follows.
II: Half of 12 is 6, and 8 is more than 6. Follows. Both follow.

**Q6. Answer: (a).**
I: Invoice 417 (Rs 62,000) is above Rs 50,000, so the rule applies. Follows.
II: The rule covers only invoices above Rs 50,000; smaller ones are not mentioned. Does not follow.

**Q7. Answer: (b).**
I: The statement says nothing about how well anyone performs. Does not follow.
II: 20 - 3 = 17. Follows.

**Q8. Answer: (b).**
I: Closing a whole floor over an empty cooler is extreme and disproportionate. Fails.
II: Relevant (the cooler runs empty), practicable, mild. Should be followed.

**Q9. Answer: (c).**
I: More evening agents directly reduce waiting. Relevant and practicable.
II: A callback option also removes the waiting without cutting service. Relevant and practicable. Both should be followed.

**Q10. Answer: (d).**
Both ideas are extreme. The problem is unsold bread at closing, so the fix is about quantities or timing, not ending the business. Neither fits.

**Q11. Answer: (a).**
I: Flip it: "Nobody is free in the evenings." Then evening classes would attract no one. Implicit.
II: "Everyone who joins will become an analyst" is a hoped result and an extreme claim. Not implicit.

**Q12. Answer: (a).**
I: 130 is less than 150. Follows.
II: The statement gives numbers only. A special menu is an invented reason. Does not follow.

**Q13. Answer: (c).**
I: Checking by hand protects people from wrong pay while the fault is fixed; reporting to the vendor tackles the cause. Relevant and practicable.
II: Telling affected employees keeps them informed and harms nothing. Both should be followed.

**Q14. Answer: (a).**
I: Flip it: "Team members cannot open the sheet." Then the request is pointless. Implicit.
II: Higher sales are a hoped result. The request makes sense even if sales do not change. Not implicit.

**Q15. Answer: (c).**
I: Flip it: "Employees do not value being paid before a long weekend." Then the reason given ("so that employees receive salary before a long weekend") would be pointless. Implicit.
II: Flip it: "The payroll team cannot finish 5 days earlier." Then the plan could not be carried out. Implicit. Both.

**Q16. Answer: (b).**
I: "No other hiring method exists" is extreme and not needed; the HR head simply chose this one. Not implicit.
II: Flip it: "A campus drive cannot again bring good applicants." Then holding another drive is pointless. Implicit.

**Q17. Answer: (a).**
I: Flip it: "Nobody looks for lower fares." Then advertising lower fares is pointless. Implicit.
II: "Everyone" is extreme; the offer only needs some people to have smartphones. Not implicit.

**Q18. Answer: (d).**
I: "Every employee loves outings" is extreme. The head only needs enough interest to go ahead.
II: "Unlimited money" is extreme. Neither is implicit.

**Q19. Answer: (a).**
I: 25 percent of 480 = 480 × 25 / 100 = 120. Follows.
II: 480 - 120 = 360 calls were about other topics. The statement does not say they were all about delivery. Does not follow.

**Q20. Answer: (b).**
I: Other transport = 60 - 45 = 15, not 20. False; does not follow.
II: 15 follows.

**Q21. Answer: (a).**
I: Only shortlisted candidates received an offer letter, so Kabir was shortlisted. Every shortlisted candidate was interviewed, so Kabir was interviewed. Follows (two steps in a chain).
II: Being interviewed was not said to be enough for an offer letter; some interviewed candidates may not have received one. Does not follow (the reversal trap).

**Q22. Answer: (d).**
Both steps are extreme. The problem is one city and a courier issue, so a measured fix would be to change the courier or reroute. Neither passes the proportionate test.

**Q23. Answer: (c).**
I: A walkthrough with an example addresses the confusion. Relevant and practicable.
II: Simplifying the instructions using staff's own questions also addresses it. Both should be followed.

**Q24. Answer: (c).**
I: The stated purpose is fewer errors. Flip: "System totals have as many errors as hand totals." Then the switch has no point. Implicit.
II: Flip: "Employees cannot use the online system at all." Then it cannot work. Implicit. Both.

**Q25. Answer: (a).**
I: "Most" means more than half. Follows.
II: "All" is stronger than "most". Does not follow.

**Q26. Answer: (b).**
I: More than 2 years of service is necessary to apply, not enough to get the transfer. Does not follow.
II: 1 year is not more than 2, so such an employee cannot apply. Follows.

**Q27. Answer: (a).**
Some trainees are interns. Those trainee-interns are trainees, and no manager is a trainee, so those interns are not managers. So some interns are not managers: I follows.
II: Interns who are not trainees might be managers; the statement says nothing about them. Does not follow.
Example check: managers {M1}, trainees {T1}, interns {T1, M1}. Both statements hold, I is true and II is false.

**Q28. Answer: (b).**
I: Removing the app permanently is extreme. Fails.
II: Peak-hour capacity directly addresses "Server busy" at peak hours. Should be followed.

**Q29. Answer: (d).**
People who prefer at least one drink = 200 - 30 = 170. Coffee plus tea = 120 + 90 = 210. The extra 210 - 170 = 40 are counted twice, so 40 employees prefer both.
I: Only coffee = 120 - 40 = 80, not more than 100. False.
II: 40 prefer both, so "no employee prefers both" is false. Neither follows.

**Q30. Answer: (c).**
Order of finishing: Dev, then Esha, then Farid.
I: Dev is before Esha and Esha before Farid, so Dev is before Farid. Follows.
II: Among the three, Farid came last. Follows. (The statement says nothing about anyone outside the three.)

**Q31. Answer: (a).**
I: The stated purpose is "to cut costs". Flip: "Free bags cost nothing noticeable." Then cutting costs by stopping them would make no sense. Implicit.
II: "All customers" is extreme; the aim is only to encourage it. Not implicit.

**Q32. Answer: (b).**
I: "Students will stop studying altogether" is extreme and not needed. Not implicit.
II: The reason is "students need rest". Flip: "Weekend homework does not take time that would be used for rest." Then the objection disappears. Implicit.

**Q33. Answer: (a).**
I: All three accidents are in one section, so reviewing that section and retraining are relevant, practicable and proportionate. Should be followed.
II: Fining workers before knowing the cause is unfair and does not fix any underlying hazard. Fails the "no new harm" check.

**Q34. Answer: (c).**
I: Finding the slowest steps and fixing them targets the delay. Relevant and practicable.
II: Reconciling during the month spreads out the work. Relevant and practicable. Both should be followed.

**Q35. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) No, (iii) Yes.**
(i) 24 of 30 late deliveries is 80 percent, which is more than half. Follows.
(ii) 6 late deliveries were not on bridge routes, and even for the 24 the statement does not prove the closure was the cause. Does not follow.
(iii) Relevant and practicable. Should be followed.

**Q36. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) Yes, (iii) No.**
(i) Flip: "Employees cannot file within 30 days." Then the rule would be unreasonable. Implicit.
(ii) 45 days is more than 30, so the claim is late and "will not be paid". Follows.
(iii) Paying late claims anyway contradicts the stated rule. No.

**Q37. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) No, (iii) No.**
(i) 35 + 20 = 55, and 55 - 50 = 5 counted twice, so 5 know both. Follows.
(ii) SQL-only applicants = 20 - 5 = 15, so not all SQL knowers know Excel. Does not follow.
(iii) The statement says every applicant knows at least one, so none knows neither. Does not follow.

**Q38. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) Yes, (iii) No.**
(i) Flip: "Replacements will not help." Then asking HR to hire them would be pointless. Implicit.
(ii) 6 / 2 = 3. Follows.
(iii) Customer feelings are not mentioned. Does not follow.

**Q39. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) No, (iii) No.**
(i) 6 years is at least 5, so Meera gets 30 days. Follows.
(ii) 4 years is under 5, so such an employee gets 24 days. Does not follow.
(iii) A new joiner has under 5 years of service, so gets 24 days. Does not follow.

**Q40. Answer: (i) Yes, (ii) No, (iii) Yes.**
(i) Flip: "A loyalty card cannot encourage anyone to return." Then starting one would be pointless. Implicit.
(ii) The statement says the fall came after the competitor opened, not that it was the only cause. Does not follow.
(iii) Asking customers is relevant, cheap and sensible. Should be followed.

## Chapter 22: Data Sufficiency

Choices: A = I alone; B = II alone; C = both together only; D = each alone; E = not enough even together.
Every answer below was re-checked in review by code (see the verification note at the end).

### Self-check answers

1. "Sufficient" means the facts leave exactly one possible answer (one value, or an always-yes or always-no for a yes/no question).
2. No. "Sometimes yes, sometimes no" is not a single answer, so it is not sufficient. (Only "always yes" or "always no" is.)
3. Yes. All packs are identical, so one pack holds 60 / 5 = 12 pens. One answer.

### Easy

**Q1: A.**
1. I alone: price of the box = 12 x 15 = Rs 180. One answer. Sufficient.
2. II alone: the box cost at most Rs 500, so a pen could cost Rs 9 (box 108) or Rs 12 (box 144), and so on. Not sufficient.
3. Only I works: A.

**Q2: C.**
1. I alone: opening 12, closing unknown. Days taken could be 0, 3 or 5. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: closing 9, opening unknown. Days taken could be 1 or 10. Not sufficient.
3. Together: 12 - 9 = 3 days. One answer. C.

**Q3: B.**
1. I alone: 6 seniors plus an unknown number of juniors. With 0 juniors the team is 6 (not larger than 10); with 8 juniors it is 14 (larger). Not sufficient.
2. II alone: 11 juniors already makes at least 11 people, which is more than 10. Always yes. Sufficient.
3. B.

**Q4: D.**
1. I alone: 6,000 / 4 = Rs 1,500.
2. II alone: 15,000 / 10 = Rs 1,500.
3. Each alone gives one answer: D. (Same answer in both, which is fine; each is separately sufficient.)

**Q5: E.**
1. Each alone gives one end of the age range only. Not sufficient.
2. Together: the youngest is 21 and the oldest is 24; the other two ages are free between them. Ages 21, 21, 21, 24 give an average of 21.75 (sum 87). Ages 21, 24, 24, 24 give an average of 23.25 (sum 93). Two different averages, so not sufficient.
3. E.

**Q6: A.**
1. I alone: Rs 11,200 is more than Rs 10,000. Always yes. Sufficient.
2. II alone: higher than Friday's, but Friday's is unknown. Saturday could be Rs 9,000 (no) or Rs 12,000 (yes). Not sufficient.
3. A.

**Q7: B.**
1. I alone: the deposit is two months of the total rent, but no amount is given, so the rent is unknown. The statement is always true whatever the rent is. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: 4 rooms with equal rent, total Rs 28,000, so one room is 28,000 / 4 = Rs 7,000. Sufficient.
3. B.

**Q8: D.**
1. I alone: 30 boxes in 20 minutes = 1.5 boxes per minute.
2. II alone: 90 boxes in 60 minutes = 1.5 boxes per minute.
3. D.

**Q9: E.**
1. I alone: 20 employees; women could be 3 or 9. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: more men than women says nothing about the count. Not sufficient.
3. Together: 20 employees with more men than women allows women to be any of 0 to 9. For example 9 women and 11 men, or 5 women and 15 men. Not sufficient.
4. E.

**Q10: C.**
1. I alone: 5 days, hours per day unknown. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: 7 hours on Tuesday, so 7 hours every working day, but the number of days is unknown. Not sufficient.
3. Together: 5 days x 7 hours = 35 hours. C.

### Medium

**Q11: A.**
1. I alone: Tomas earns Rs 5,000 more than Lakshmi, so Tomas earns more, whatever the actual salaries. Always yes. Sufficient.
2. II alone: Lakshmi's pay is known, Tomas's is not. Not sufficient.
3. A. (You never needed to know the actual salaries.)

**Q12: C.**
1. I alone: sold for Rs 1,200, discount unknown. The marked price could be Rs 1,500 or Rs 2,000. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: the discount is Rs 300, but the sale price is unknown. Not sufficient.
3. Together: marked price = sale price + discount = 1,200 + 300 = Rs 1,500. C.

**Q13: B.**
1. I alone: three sold Rs 50,000 each = Rs 1,50,000. The fourth could sell Rs 40,000 (total Rs 1,90,000, short of target) or Rs 60,000 (total Rs 2,10,000, target reached). Not sufficient.
2. II alone: nobody above Rs 45,000, so the most the four could sell is 4 x 45,000 = Rs 1,80,000, which is less than Rs 2,00,000. Always no. Sufficient.
3. B.

**Q14: E.**
1. I alone: Chennai relates to Delhi, whose revenue is unknown; Pune is not mentioned. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: Pune is Rs 5 lakh, but Chennai is unknown. Not sufficient.
3. Together: Chennai is 1.2 x Delhi, and Delhi is still unknown. If Delhi is Rs 3 lakh, Chennai is Rs 3.6 lakh (below Pune's 5 lakh). If Delhi is Rs 5 lakh, Chennai is Rs 6 lakh (above Pune's 5 lakh). Not sufficient.
4. E.

**Q15: D.**
1. I alone: Rs 11.5 lakh is over Rs 10 lakh. Always yes.
2. II alone: Rs 2 lakh more than the budget is Rs 12 lakh, which is over budget. Always yes.
3. D.

**Q16: A.**
1. I alone: 35 of 50 = 70%. Sufficient (the total of 50 is in the question).
2. II alone: 10 blank tells you nothing about how many said "satisfied". Not sufficient.
3. A.

**Q17: C.**
1. I alone: day : night = 2 : 3. Night agents could be 3 (day 2), 6 (day 4), 30 (day 20), and so on. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: night = day + 10. Many pairs fit (day 5, night 15; day 20, night 30). Not sufficient.
3. Together: let day = 2k and night = 3k. Then 3k - 2k = k = 10, so night = 30 (day = 20). One answer. C.

**Q18: D.**
1. I alone: average = 350 / 5 = 70. Sufficient.
2. II alone: the scores add up to 60 + 65 + 70 + 75 + 80 = 350, so the average is 350 / 5 = 70. Sufficient.
3. D.

**Q19: B.**
1. I alone: an average of 30 means a total of 150 minutes, which does not fix the longest and shortest. For example 28, 29, 30, 31, 32 has range 4; 20, 25, 30, 35, 40 has range 20. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: the five times are listed, so the longest is 40 and the shortest is 22: range = 40 - 22 = 18. Sufficient.
3. B. (II's times add up to 150, so they also agree with I's average of 30; the two statements do not conflict.)

**Q20: E.**
1. I alone: Kiran > Omar, Sana unknown. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: Sana > Omar, Kiran unknown. Not sufficient.
3. Together: both beat Omar, so Omar is not the top. But Kiran versus Sana is unknown, so the top could be Kiran or Sana. Not sufficient.
4. E.

**Q21: B.**
1. I alone: length + breadth = 50. Length could be 30 (breadth 20) or 40 (breadth 10). Not sufficient.
2. II alone: breadth is 20 and length x 20 = 600, so length = 30. Sufficient.
3. B.

**Q22: A.**
1. I alone: 6 days at an average of Rs 12,000 gives 6 x 12,000 = Rs 72,000. Sufficient. (The 6 days comes from the question.)
2. II alone: the number of items sold says nothing about rupees. Not sufficient.
3. A.

### Hard

**Q23: E.**
Let F = full-time staff and T = total staff.
1. I alone: contract = two thirds of F, so F : contract = 3 : 2. Whole-number sizes include F = 6 (contract 4, total 10) and F = 30 (contract 20, total 50). Not sufficient.
2. II alone: F = 60% of T, so T could be 10 (F = 6) or 50 (F = 30). Not sufficient.
3. Together: II says contract = 40% of T and F = 60% of T, so contract / F = 40 / 60 = two thirds. That is exactly what I says, so I adds nothing new. Both T = 10 and T = 50 still fit. Not sufficient.
4. E. (Trap: two statements look different but say the same thing, and neither gives an actual head count.)

**Q24: A.**
1. I alone: 3a + 5b = 22 with a, b whole numbers, at least 1. Try b = 1: 22 - 5 = 17, not a multiple of 3. b = 2: 22 - 10 = 12, so a = 4. Works. b = 3: 22 - 15 = 7, not a multiple of 3. b = 4: 22 - 20 = 2, not a multiple of 3. b = 5 would need negative 3 kg boxes. So the only possibility is 4 three-kg boxes and 2 five-kg boxes. Number of 5 kg boxes = 2. Sufficient.
2. II alone: a + b = 6 gives b = 1 to 5. Not sufficient.
3. A. (Whole numbers can make a single equation enough.)

**Q25: B.**
1. I alone: Q2 = Q3 - 5. Q1 is not mentioned. If Q1 is large, Q2 may be lower; if small, higher. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: Q1 = 0.8 x Q2. Because revenue is positive, 0.8 x Q2 is less than Q2, so Q2 is higher than Q1. Always yes. Sufficient.
3. B.

**Q26: C.**
1. I alone: 18 returns. If there were 100 orders, the rate is 18% (above 5%). If there were 1,000, it is 1.8% (below). Not sufficient.
2. II alone: 400 orders. If 10 returned the rate is 2.5% (no); if 30 returned it is 7.5% (yes). Not sufficient.
3. Together: 18 / 400 = 4.5%, which is below 5%. Always no. C.

**Q27: D.**
1. I alone: a fee of Rs 48,000 on Rs 6,00,000 is 48,000 / 6,00,000 = 8%. Since the percentage is the same for every hire, the fee is 8%. Sufficient.
2. II alone: Rs 72,000 on Rs 9,00,000 is 72,000 / 9,00,000 = 8%. Sufficient.
3. D.

**Q28: E.**
1. I alone: the total is 5 x 45,000 = Rs 2,25,000, but the middle value is not fixed. Not sufficient.
2. II alone: only the top is known. Not sufficient.
3. Together: five salaries adding to Rs 2,25,000 with a maximum of Rs 80,000. Example 1: 80,000; 80,000; 55,000; 5,000; 5,000. Sum = 2,25,000, maximum 80,000, median 55,000 (above 40,000). Example 2: 80,000; 80,000; 40,000; 20,000; 5,000. Sum = 2,25,000, maximum 80,000, median 40,000 (not above 40,000). Different answers, so not sufficient.
4. E.

**Q29: C.**
The question is yes/no: is 7,20,000 / hires greater than 30,000? This is true exactly when the number of hires is below 24.
- A (applicants 400): applicants do not fix the number of hires. Not sufficient.
- B (more than 20 hires): 21 hires gives about Rs 34,286 per hire (up); 25 hires gives Rs 28,800 (down). Not sufficient.
- C (exactly 24 hires): 7,20,000 / 24 = Rs 30,000, the same as last quarter. So it did not go up. Always no. Sufficient.
- D (interviewer hours up 15%): says nothing about the number of hires. Not sufficient.
- E (at most 30 hires): 23 hires gives about Rs 31,304 (up); 30 hires gives Rs 24,000 (down). Not sufficient.
Answer: **C**. (Only one option settles it.)

**Q30: D.**
After training: 30 staff averaging 72, so a total of 30 x 72 = 2,160. The question is whether the average before was lower than 72, which is the same as whether the total before was below 2,160.
- A (best score after is 95): the average after is already known; this tells nothing about before. Not sufficient.
- B (highest before was 90): the average before is at most 90, which could be 60 (rose) or 85 (fell). Not sufficient.
- C (20 of the 30 averaged 65): those 20 total 1,300. The other 10 can total anywhere from 0 to 1,000. The overall total is then between 1,300 and 2,300, which includes values below and above 2,160. Not sufficient.
- D (30 scores add to 2,100): the average before is 2,100 / 30 = 70, which is below 72. So the average rose. Always yes. Sufficient.
- E (average before below the pass mark of 75): before could be 70 (rose) or 74 (fell). Not sufficient.
Answer: **D**.

### Verification note

All 30 questions, and the four worked examples that rest on search (Examples 1 to 4), were checked by a brute-force program that listed every situation allowed by the question's background facts, then kept those that obeyed each statement (I alone, II alone, both) and counted the distinct answers. Q29 and Q30 were checked option by option, and in each exactly one option leaves a single answer. Example 5 and Scenario 1 and 2 numbers were recomputed separately (36,000; 78,000; 18%; 11.25%; 18.75%; 28 x 7 = 196). Search ranges are finite (for example, salaries up to Rs 1,00,000), and the "not sufficient" answers were each confirmed by two explicit situations shown above.

## Chapter 23 answer key: Logical Fallacies

### Self-check answers

1. A logical fallacy is a pattern of bad reasoning: the reasons offered look as if they support the conclusion but do not.
2. Ad hominem. The reason given (she is late for meetings) is about the person, not about the budget idea.
3. False. A fallacy only shows that this argument fails to support the conclusion. The conclusion may still be true for other reasons.

### Practice answers

Method for every question: find the conclusion, list the reasons, check whether the reasons connect to the conclusion.

#### Easy

**Q1 Ad hominem (1).** Conclusion: do not listen to the proposal. Reason: Mira talks fast and dresses oddly. Neither says anything about the leave tracker idea itself.

**Q2 Bandwagon (4).** Conclusion: you should switch. Reason: neighbours switched. The number of neighbours says nothing about whether the bulbs suit you.

**Q3 False cause (3).** Conclusion: green juice cures colds. Reason: it was followed by recovery. Colds usually pass in a few days anyway, so "after" is not "because".

**Q4 False dilemma (6).** Only two options are offered (raise for all, or half quit). Others exist: targeted raises, other benefits, discussions.

**Q5 Appeal to tradition (12).** Reason is only "we have always done it". There is no argument that Friday printing is useful.

**Q6 Appeal to ignorance (13).** "Not proved unsafe" is used as proof of safe. Absence of proof is not proof of absence; safety needs positive evidence.

**Q7 Straw man (2).** A asked for a quiet-hour trial. B replaced it with "ban all talking" and attacked that.

**Q8 Hasty generalisation (8).** Two cases are used to describe all riders. The sample is tiny and may be unrepresentative.

**Q9 Circular reasoning (9).** The reason ("treats everyone fairly") just restates the conclusion ("is fair").

**Q10 Appeal to emotion (10).** Pity for the puppy replaces any fact about the insurance (price, cover, claims).

#### Medium

**Q11 Loaded question (14).** "Have you stopped" assumes the person did pad claims. Any yes or no answer admits it.

**Q12 Red herring (11).** The safety award has nothing to do with the payroll delays; it changes the subject.

**Q13 Tu quoque (15).** The rule is dismissed because the speaker also broke it. The rule is still valid whoever says it. (Not ad hominem, because the specific attack is hypocrisy.)

**Q14 Slippery slope (7).** One person on Fridays leads, with no evidence, through "everyone" and "nobody" to "collapse". Each step is just asserted.

**Q15 Appeal to false authority (5).** A film star is not an expert on home loans. (Not bandwagon: it is one person's status, not the crowd.)

**Q16 Ad hominem (1).** The reason is the leader's pay, not the offer. (This is the circumstantial form: attacking motive.) The offer should be judged on its numbers.

**Q17 Straw man (2).** "Sandals in summer" became "swimwear". The extreme version was never proposed.

**Q18 False cause (3).** Conclusion: green paint raises sales. Reason: sales rose that week. Other causes (season, promotion) are ignored.

**Q19 Hasty generalisation (8).** Five friends are a tiny, similar group, not the city.

**Q20 False dilemma (6).** "With us or against the company" leaves out cautious support, concerns, or asking for changes.

#### Hard

**Q21 None.** The reasons are a record of 40 orders (39 on time) plus an independent check of 10 against separate warehouse records, and the conclusion is cautious ("usually", "expect"). The reasons connect to the conclusion, so it is not hasty generalisation.

**Q22 None.** The majority view here is backed by direct evidence: reports plus a documented own trial (6 of 8 cold mornings jammed). It is not bandwagon because the reason is evidence, not popularity.

**Q23 Circular reasoning (9).** The handbook's claim of being correct is used to prove it is correct. No outside evidence.

**Q24 Bandwagon (4) and appeal to false authority (5).** "Everyone is buying" is the crowd; a dentist is not an expert on heart health.

**Q25 Appeal to tradition (12) and appeal to emotion (10).** "Our grandparents trusted it" appeals to the past; "warm family dinners, smell of home" appeals to feelings. No fact about the product is given.

**Q26 Appeal to ignorance (13).** Lack of disproof is taken as a reason to act as if the ghost exists.

**Q27 (a) Straw man (2).** A said "review reporting lines"; B turned it into "reorganising was a mistake and leaders are incompetent". **(b)** Example: "I am not saying the reorganisation was wrong; I am asking us to check whether the new reporting lines relate to the attrition rise." (Any sentence that restates the real claim and asks for evidence.)

**Q28 Hasty generalisation (8).** The sample is only 40 and self-selected (brand followers), so it cannot represent all customers. Also a form of biased sampling.

**Q29 None.** The chain (skip backup, lose file, late report) is supported by recorded cases, and the conclusion is cautious ("may be late"). A slippery slope would claim a catastrophic chain with no evidence.

**Q30** Comment 1: ad hominem (1), the attack is on his seniority. Comment 2: bandwagon (4), competitors' actions are the only reason. Comment 3: false dilemma (6), adopting fully this month versus not caring leaves out pilots and other ways to show care.

## Chapter 24 answer key: Correlation vs Causation

### Self-check answers

1. Correlation: two things tend to move together. Causation: one thing actually makes the other happen. Correlation is only a pattern in the data.
2. Hot weather. It raises both ice-cream sales and swimming, so more people are at risk of drowning. (Another valid answer: both rise in holiday months.)
3. Any one of: a random experiment with a comparison group; comparing with a similar group that did not get the change; checking dose and response; checking a believable mechanism; repeating the pattern.

### Practice answers

Labels: P plausible cause, R reverse, T third factor, C coincidence.

#### Easy

**Q1 T.** The size of the fire drives both: bigger fires need more engines and cause more damage. Engines do not create damage.

**Q2 T.** Age: older adults have more grey hair and have had more years to save.

**Q3 T.** Town size drives both nests (more land and houses) and births (more people). Storks do not deliver babies.

**Q4 R.** Back pain leads people to visit the physiotherapist. Visits may help or not, but the comparison cannot show that visits cause pain.

**Q5 R.** Profit pays for the parties, so profit comes first. Parties may have small effects, but the stated mechanism points from profit to parties.

**Q6 C.** No known link, no mechanism, a few yearly points, and both can drift upward with city growth. Chance or shared trend.

**Q7 P.** Random assignment makes the groups alike, so the video is the likely cause of fewer early resignations in this group over these three months.

#### Medium

**Q8 T.** Opening the newsletter was the customer's own choice, so keen, loyal customers probably open it and also spend more. (A fair test would send it to a random half of customers.)

**Q9 R.** The budget is set from the number of existing employees, so staff numbers decide the budget. The data does not show whether a bigger recruitment budget raises staff numbers.

**Q10 T.** Case severity drives both: severe cases go to large hospitals and severe cases have higher death rates. Compare similar cases only.

**Q11 T.** Population: more people, more shops and more bicycles to steal. Compare per 1,000 people.

**Q12 R.** Output (last year) decided who could choose remote work, so high output came first.

**Q13 T.** Seasonality (year-end shopping) explains the rise: the same rise occurred last year without the offer. The offer may add nothing.

**Q14 C.** Three wins are too few; there is no mechanism by which socks affect bids. Chance.

#### Hard

**Q15 (a)** Users: (80 x 800 + 20 x 400) divided by 100 = (64,000 + 8,000) / 100 = 720. Non-users: (20 x 800 + 80 x 400) / 100 = (16,000 + 32,000) / 100 = 480. So users appear to spend 240 more. **(b)** Within each age group the averages are equal (800 vs 800, 400 vs 400): no difference. **(c) T (age).** Most users are under 30 and the under-30s spend more. Response: "The gap comes from the mix of ages; within the same age group the app shows no difference, so this table does not show the app causes more spending."

**Q16 (a)** Any two of: people chose the scheme themselves (self-selection, keen employees would rate higher anyway); high performers may be more likely to join (reverse); no comparison with their ratings before the scheme; no randomisation. **(b)** Example: "Among volunteers, randomly choose half to join the scheme this year and let the other half wait. Compare rating changes between the two groups."

**Q17 P.** Clues: before and after measures, steady dose and response (more hours, more loss), a controlled random lab result, and a known mechanism. Together these point strongly to cause.

**Q18 R.** The first reading assumed meetings lowered output; but low output came first and triggered the meetings. Time order and how the meetings were decided reverse the arrow.

**Q19 C (shared trend).** Both rise over time because of general growth or inflation, not because tea causes subscriptions. Before claiming a link, look for a mechanism, test whether the pattern holds in other places or after removing the time trend (for example by comparing changes year to year), and see whether other sources agree.

**Q20 (a) T.** The data team and their new projects likely raise ratings and also received upgrades first. **(b)** "Employees who received upgrades have higher ratings; this may be partly due to their role and projects, so we cannot yet say the upgrades raise performance." **(c)** Example: "Randomly pick half of eligible staff within the same role to receive upgrades first, and compare rating changes after a quarter."
