## Chapter 25 answer key: Cognitive Biases

### Self-check answers

1. **Confirmation bias.** Kavya reads only what agrees with her choice, so she never meets evidence that could change her mind.
2. **No.** The ₹5,000 is gone whether he continues or not (sunk cost). The only fair question is: from now on, is the course worth his time and any further cost? If not, stop.
3. **The shops that closed.** Survivorship bias: we see only the survivors, so we cannot tell whether closed shops did the same things as the open ones.

### Practice answers

**Q1.** Confirmation bias. Ravi keeps the evidence that fits (centuries) and explains away the rest (zeros). To test fairly: list all innings of the captain for a full season, compute the average, and compare with the averages of other top players using the same method. Decide in advance what result would make him change his view.

**Q2.** Sunk cost fallacy. The ₹300 is spent whether she stays or leaves. The sensible question: "From now on, would I rather spend the remaining time on this film or on something else?"

**Q3.** Anchoring: the seller's first number (₹50,000) made ₹40,000 feel like a good deal. Market price ₹30,000, so the buyer paid 40,000 minus 30,000 = ₹10,000 more. As a percentage of the market price: 10,000 divided by 30,000 = 0.333, about 33.3% more. Repair: research the market price before hearing the seller's number.

**Q4.** The missing group is the old buildings that were badly built and have already collapsed or been demolished; we only see the ones that lasted. This is survivorship bias. Today's buildings include the weak ones (not yet failed), so the comparison is unfair.

**Q5.** Halo effect. One good trait (appearance) spreads to unrelated traits (punctuality, accuracy, client skills). Fix: judge each trait from its own evidence.

**Q6.** Hindsight bias ("knew it all along"). A habit that prevents it: write predictions and their reasons before the event, with dates, then compare afterwards. Without a written record, memory rewrites itself.

**Q7.**
- (a) **Recency**: the latest weeks outweigh the full year.
- (b) **Overconfidence**: certainty far above the track record (about half right).
- (c) **Base-rate neglect**: ignores how rare real guilt is among all employees.
- (d) **Availability**: two vivid friends' stories make scams feel common.
- (e) **Framing**: 1 in 20 late = 5% late = 95% on time; same fact, different wording. 95% on time sounds better.

**Q8.**
(a) Total = 10 x 90 + 60 + 70 = 900 + 130 = 1,030. Average = 1,030 divided by 12 = 85.83 units.
(b) (60 + 70) divided by 2 = 65 units.
(c) Difference = 85.83 minus 65 = 20.83. 20.83 divided by 85.83 = 0.2427, so about **24.3%** below.
(d) Recency bias. The last two months are the weakest and they overshadow ten steady months. A fair review shows the full year and also notes the recent dip as a separate point to investigate.

**Q9.**
(a) Referral: 2 divided by 25 = 0.08 = 8%. Walk-in: 6 divided by 60 = 0.10 = 10%.
(b) Walk-ins have the higher leaving rate (10% against 8%).
(c) Availability bias: one vivid bad hire made the pattern feel common. (Also note the groups are small, so even the 2 percentage point gap is not strong proof either way.) Repair: compare rates, not stories.

**Q10.**
(a) X: 95% on time means 100 minus 95 = 5% late. Y: 1 in 20 = 1 divided by 20 = 0.05 = 5% late. Z: 4 in 100 = 4% late.
(b) Z is best (4% late). X and Y are equal (5% late).
(c) Framing effect (also the "1 in 20" form hides the size of the percentage). Repair: convert every offer to the same wording and unit before comparing.

**Q11.**
(a) Finish: benefit 4 minus future cost 5 = minus ₹1 lakh. Stop: 0 (no further cost, no further benefit). The ₹8 lakh already spent is the same in both. So **stop**; finishing loses another ₹1 lakh.
(b) Yes. Finish: 7 minus 5 = plus ₹2 lakh, which beats 0, so finish. The decision changed because of the future numbers, not the past spending.
(c) "We have already put in ₹8 lakh, we cannot waste that, so let us finish." This is sunk cost thinking: it looks backwards.

**Q12.**
(a) Anchoring.
(b) Group A saw 40% first, and its average guess (30%) is the higher one: pulled upwards. Group B saw 10% first, and its guess (15%) is the lower: pulled downwards. (The true value is unknown here; we only see the groups were pulled in opposite directions by different starting numbers.)
(c) Ask people to write their own estimate privately before anyone mentions a number; or collect estimates from several independent sources; or use the actual data.

**Q13.**
(a) Campaign weeks: 5 divided by 8 = 0.625 = 62.5%. Non-campaign weeks: 5 divided by 8 = 62.5%.
(b) Sales rose equally often with and without the campaign, so the data do not show a campaign effect.
(c) Confirmation bias: looking only at campaign weeks gives a number that looks supportive and hides the comparison that would disagree.

**Q14.**
(a) 20 divided by 5,000 = 0.004 = 0.4%.
(b) The 4,980 early leavers who did not become successful (and the people who stayed in university and succeeded, for a fair comparison).
(c) Survivorship bias. Out of every 1,000 early leavers only about 4 succeeded, so leaving early is not a good predictor of success.

**Q15.**
(a) Misusers = 5% of 2,000 = 100. Honest = 2,000 minus 100 = 1,900.
(b) Misusers flagged = 80% of 100 = 80. Honest flagged = 5% of 1,900 = 95.
(c) Total flagged = 80 + 95 = 175. Real misusers among flagged = 80 divided by 175 = 0.457, so **45.7%**.
(d) Base-rate neglect. The 80% is how often the detector catches misusers, not the chance that a flagged person is a misuser. Because honest employees are so many (1,900), even a 5% false alarm rate flags more honest people (95) than the detector catches misusers (80). So a flag means roughly a 46% chance, not 80%.

**Q16.** Three biases: (1) **sunk cost fallacy** ("already spent two years and a huge sum, cannot drop it"); (2) **survivorship bias** (one friend who succeeded stands in for all similar attempts; the failed ones are invisible); (3) **confirmation bias** (reads only praising comments and dismisses the negative ones). A fourth reading, overconfidence ("ours will too"), is also acceptable, but the three above are the clearest.

**Q17.**
(a) (20 + 30 + 50) divided by 3 = 100 divided by 3 = 33.3 days.
(b) (16 + 18 + 20) divided by 3 = 54 divided by 3 = 18 days.
(c) Drop = 33.33 minus 18 = 15.33 days. As a percentage: 15.33 divided by 33.33 = 0.46, so **46%**.
(d) Anchoring. The boss's number pulled everyone's estimate towards 15. Rule: collect written estimates privately first, discuss afterwards, and let the most senior person speak last.

**Q18.**
(a) 80% of 300 = 240.
(b) 20% of 100 = 20.
(c) (240 + 20) divided by 400 = 260 divided by 400 = 0.65 = **65%**.
(d) Survivorship bias. The 80% describes only people who stayed; the people most likely to be unhappy are the ones who left and were not asked. Note the 20% figure is hypothetical; the point is that the headline could be far too rosy, and an exit survey of leavers is needed.

**Q19.**
(a) A 70% chance of success means a 30% chance of failure. In 10 similar pilots about 10 x 0.3 = 3 would fail even with good decisions.
(b) Outcome bias (a close relative of hindsight bias): judging the decision by its result instead of by the information available at the time.
(c) Yes. Good decisions can fail through chance, and bad decisions can succeed through luck. Judge the quality of a decision by asking: was the information reasonable, were the options compared well, were the risks weighed? The result is one more data point, not the whole verdict.

**Q20.** Model answer (any sensible five steps with the right bias match are fine):
1. **Fix criteria and weights before seeing candidates.** Targets halo effect and recency; each trait gets its own score with agreed weights.
2. **Ask every candidate the same questions in the same order.** Reduces confirmation bias (no chasing questions that confirm a first impression).
3. **Score privately and in writing before any discussion.** Prevents anchoring on the first or most senior opinion.
4. **Actively look for disconfirming evidence:** each panel member lists one reason not to hire the favourite. Targets confirmation bias.
5. **Review hires later against the scores** (for example after six months) to learn which scores predicted success. Targets overconfidence and hindsight bias; also, include candidates who left or were rejected where possible so that you do not learn only from the survivors.

## Chapter 26 answer key: Evaluating Claims, Sources and Evidence

### Self-check answers

1. Any two of: urgency ("today"); "forward to 10 friends"; vague authority ("experts confirm") with no names; an offer that is too good ("free recharge for everyone"); no source or date.
2. The starting and ending numbers (a "50% fall" could be from 4 hairs to 2), and how it was measured: how many people, over how long, compared with what (people using no shampoo or another one). Also who ran the test.
3. The dated HR system report. It is the original record with an owner and a date; a memory is second-hand and may be wrong or out of date.

### Practice answers

**Q1.** Red flags: a too-good offer (free laptop for everyone); a request for bank details; a link to click; "forward to 15 groups"; no official source named. Action: do not click, do not enter details, do not forward. If curious, search for the scheme on the government's own official website (typed in yourself). Verdict: very likely a scam; at best "cannot tell" until an official source confirms it.

**Q2.** It fails WHO (which experts?) and WHAT evidence (no data, no test, no criteria for "best"). Questions: which experts, paid by whom, what was compared, on which criteria (battery, camera, price), and where can I see the test?

**Q3.** Any three good questions, for example: rated by how many customers; where and when (a website, last year, last week); on what scale and compared with which other restaurants; who collected the ratings (the restaurant itself?); what does "No. 1" mean (in this street, in this city)?

**Q4.** (b) HR export, most reliable: the original record, dated, owned by an accountable person. (c) Colleague's memory next: a named person who may be close to the facts, but memory is imperfect and unchecked. (a) Screenshot last: no author, no date, cannot be traced. (If the colleague works in HR and has just seen the report, they might move above memory, but still below the record itself.)

**Q5.** (a) Opinion. (b) Cause claim. (c) Prediction. (d) Fact claim (easy to check by counting floors).

**Q6.**
(a) 10 reviews with average 4.8: sum = 10 x 4.8 = 48.
(b) Five new reviews of 2 stars add 5 x 2 = 10. New sum = 48 + 10 = 58. Average = 58 divided by 15 = 3.8667, about **3.87**.
(c) The 4.8 came from 10 insiders; adding 5 honest ratings dropped it by almost a full point. Small samples move a lot, and ratings written by people connected to the seller are not independent evidence.

**Q7.**
(a) From 5 to 3 is a fall of 2; 2 divided by 5 = 0.4 = 40%. Correct.
(b) Before: 5 per 1,000 = 0.5%. After: 3 per 1,000 = 0.3%. Absolute change = 0.2 percentage points.
(c) 0.2% of 100,000 = 200 fewer failures (2 fewer per 1,000, times 100).
(d) The claim is honest (the number is right) but it sounds bigger than the absolute change. Still ask: how many payments were counted, over what period, whether the mix of customers or time of year was the same, and whether failures were counted the same way before and after.

**Q8.**
(a) 500 divided by 400 = 1.25, a 25% rise. Correct.
(b) A year earlier: 475 divided by 380 = 1.25, also a 25% rise.
(c) Seasonality: sales rise by the same 25% between these two quarters every year, so the logo cannot be credited. The comparison with the same period a year earlier removes the seasonal effect.

**Q9.**
(a) 36 recommend divided by 40 cards = 0.9 = 90%.
(b) Return rate = 40 divided by 800 = 0.05 = 5%.
(c) If none of the 760 non-returners would recommend: 36 divided by 800 = 4.5%. If all 760 would: (36 + 760) divided by 800 = 796 divided by 800 = 99.5%.
(d) The true share could be anywhere from 4.5% to 99.5%, so "90% of our customers recommend us" is not supported. It describes only the 5% who chose to return a card (probably the keenest or the angriest). Also ask how the people who returned cards differ from those who did not, and whether one Saturday represents the whole week.

**Q10.**
(a) None that are independent: the claim traces back to one unnamed "report" that nobody shows. Many repeats equal one source.
(b) The original report: its title, author, date, the sample, what "leave" and "bad experience" meant, and the method. Ideally a primary source you can open yourself.
(c) Verdict: cannot tell yet; not usable in your own report until traced to its origin.

**Q11.**
(a) Sum = 20+22+24+25+26+28+30+35+40+90 = 340. Mean = 340 divided by 10 = **34**. Sorted list is already in order; the middle two are the 5th and 6th values, 26 and 28; median = (26 + 28) divided by 2 = **27**.
(b) Report A quotes the mean (34), pulled up by one very slow delivery of 90 minutes. Report B quotes the typical middle value (27). Both are true; they answer slightly different questions. Without the definitions, they look like a disagreement.
(c) Examples: they may start the clock differently (from order placed versus from pick-up); they may cover different periods or areas; they may include or exclude cancelled or failed deliveries; they may measure different delivery services.

**Q12.**
(a) A: 9 incidents divided by 90,000 hours = 0.0001 per hour = 1.0 per 10,000 hours. B: 6 divided by 40,000 = 0.00015 per hour = 1.5 per 10,000 hours.
(b) Department B has the higher rate (1.5 against 1.0).
(c) The claim compared raw counts, but A worked more than twice the hours of B, so it had more exposure to risk. Question that prevents the mistake: "out of how many hours (or how many people)?" The rate shows B is the less safe department.

**Q13.**
(a) Fraudulent = 1% of 1,000 = 10. Genuine = 990.
(b) The "everything is genuine" tool is right on 990 claims: 990 divided by 1,000 = 99% accuracy, yet it catches no fraud at all.
(c) Conflict of interest: the speaker sells the tool and gains if you believe it. Accuracy alone is a weak figure when fraud is rare. Ask for: how many of the real frauds it caught (out of the 10), how many genuine claims it wrongly flagged, and how it was tested (whose data, which period), ideally by an independent party.

**Q14.** Model answer:
- **Data to ask for:** monthly or quarterly attrition for the firm for at least two years before and after remote work began; headcount per month (so rates, not counts); remote versus office status per employee; reasons for leaving; role, location, tenure.
- **Comparisons:** attrition of remote versus non-remote employees in the same period and similar roles; the firm's trend before the change (was attrition already falling?); external comparison, if available, with similar firms over the same period.
- **Other explanations to test:** job market changes, a different time period (seasonal), changes in who works remotely (perhaps more senior staff chose remote), a hiring freeze, a pay rise at the same time, people who left before the policy.
- **Evidence ladder position:** rung 4 (comparison of groups in existing data). It could show an association; for a stronger test, a pilot where teams are assigned remote or office by chance (rung 5) would be needed.
- **Verdict wording:** "Cannot tell yet; here is the data that would settle it."

**Q15.** Steps:
1. Do not forward and do not click any link inside the message.
2. Identify the claim: a named notice (2026/44) from a named authority (Ministry of Labour) with a stated right (3 holidays).
3. Go to the authority's official website by typing the address yourself (or using a known bookmark) and look for the notice number and date.
4. Check that more than one reliable outlet or your company's HR or legal team confirms it, and that the date is current.
5. Check the wording: does the notice apply to all employees, or to a particular sector or state?
6. Sufficient confirmation: the primary document on the official site (or confirmation by HR from that document), with a date and a number that match.
7. If you cannot confirm it: label it "unverified", tell the sender you could not find it, and do not share. Ask HR. The pressure phrase "share before it is withdrawn" is itself a warning sign.

## Chapter 27 answer key: Misleading Charts and Statistics

### Self-check answers

1. Much taller. Above the baseline of 94 the bars are 2 and 4 units, so the second looks twice as tall, though the real difference is 98 divided by 96 = about 2.1%.
2. The slices add up to 45 + 40 + 33 = 118%, but a pie must total 100%. Probably people could choose more than one answer; a bar chart would suit better.
3. One or a few very high salaries pull the mean up. Example: eight salaries around 30 to 42 thousand and one of 400 thousand give a mean far above the typical value; the median shows the typical person.

### Practice answers

**Q1.** (a) Concerning: a bar chart starting at 40 exaggerates differences because bar length is read as the amount. Fix: start at 0 or print values. (b) Acceptable: a line chart shows change; the axis is clearly labelled and the range suits body temperature, where all values lie near 36 to 38. (c) Fine: bars start at zero.

**Q2.** Ask: the unit of the vertical axis (₹, units, percent?); what exactly is measured (revenue, profit, orders); the dates and period covered; how "best year" is defined (best of how many years); the data source and who prepared it; whether a longer history would change the picture; whether the numbers are counts or rates.

**Q3.** The slices add up to 30 + 25 + 25 + 30 = 110%, which is more than 100%. Possible causes: a rounding or typing error, or a question that allowed people to give more than one reason, in which case a pie is the wrong chart. Ask the author for the raw numbers.

**Q4.** Ask for a longer period (several months or the same weeks last year) and for the counts and order volumes. Four weeks may be a lucky stretch or a seasonal dip; a title with a conclusion needs the window explained. Also ask whether the weeks differ in the number of orders (a rate may differ from a count).

**Q5.** Area ratio = 3 x 3 = 9, so the coin looks nine times as big, though sales only tripled. This impression exaggerates the growth. Fair version: draw three equal-sized coins against one.

**Q6.**
(a) Sales: (25 minus 20) divided by 20 = 0.25 = 25%. Visitors: (2,500 minus 2,000) divided by 2,000 = 0.25 = 25%.
(b) No. Lines can coincide because someone chose the scales to make them coincide; and moving together does not prove cause (Chapter 24). Visitors and sales may both rise with season, advertising or festival.
(c) The visitors line would span a short axis range (800 units): it would start at (2,000 minus 1,800) divided by 800 = 25% of the height and end at (2,500 minus 1,800) divided by 800 = 87.5%, so it would look steeper than the sales line. That shows dual axes let the drawer decide how the lines look. Better: two separate charts or an index with the first month set to 100.

**Q7.**
(a) Sum = 30+32+33+35+36+38+40+42+400 = 686. Mean = 686 divided by 9 = 76.2 (thousand). Median: nine values in order, the 5th is **36**.
(b) The median (36) is typical; the mean (76.2) is pulled up by the single 400.
(c) Add the median, a note of the highest value or a spread (range 30 to 400), and the number of employees. Or show a dot plot of all nine salaries.

**Q8.**
(a) From 2 to 8 is an increase of 6; 6 divided by 2 = 3 = 300%. The number is right.
(b) Before: 2 divided by 4,000 = 0.0005 = 0.05%. After: 8 divided by 4,000 = 0.002 = 0.2%.
(c) "Complaints rose from 2 to 8 out of 4,000 orders (0.05% to 0.2%)." The jump looks dramatic in percent because the base was tiny; the numbers themselves are still small.

**Q9.**
(a) 10, 10 and 40 minutes wide.
(b) 0 to 10: 20 divided by 10 = 2 orders per minute of band; 10 to 20: 40 divided by 10 = 4; 20 to 60: 40 divided by 40 = 1.
(c) The 10 to 20 minute band (density 4). The equal-width bars made the last bar look as tall as the middle one although those 40 orders are spread across a band four times as wide.

**Q10.**
(a) State A is shaded darker, since 5,000 is more than 3,000.
(b) A: 5,000 cases divided by 10 million people = 0.0005 per person = 500 per million. B: 3,000 divided by 2 million = 0.0015 = 1,500 per million.
(c) State B has three times the rate. A fair map would shade by cases per million (or per 100,000) people, and ideally show the totals in a tooltip or table.

**Q11.**
(a) Only A, the bottom segment, sits on the zero baseline in both bars (0 to 40 in Quarter 1, 0 to 45 in Quarter 2). B and C float on top of the segments below them.
(b) Quarter 1 total: 40 + 30 + 20 = 90. Quarter 2 total: 45 + 20 + 40 = 105.
(c) B: (20 minus 30) divided by 30 = minus 10 divided by 30 = about minus 33.3%. C: (40 minus 20) divided by 20 = plus 100%.
(d) B and C do not start from the same height in the two bars: B starts at 40 in Quarter 1 and at 45 in Quarter 2; C starts at 70 in Quarter 1 and at 65 in Quarter 2. So the eye cannot compare their lengths against a common line. Use a grouped (side by side) bar chart or a separate small chart for each product, all starting from zero.

**Q12.**
(a) 10 to 100: (100 minus 10) divided by 10 = 9 = 900%. 100 to 1,000: (1,000 minus 100) divided by 100 = 9 = 900%. Each year the value is multiplied by 10.
(b) No. On a normal scale the line would be almost flat for the first year or two and then shoot upward, because the increases are 90 and then 900 units.
(c) On a log scale, equal distances mean equal ratios, not equal amounts. A reader who assumes a normal scale would misread a "straight line" as steady additions rather than multiplying by 10 each year.

**Q13.**
(a) With 0 at the top, higher values are drawn lower. The values fall from 9 to 5, so the line climbs towards the top of the page: it looks like it is rising.
(b) (5 minus 9) divided by 9 = minus 4 divided by 9 = about minus 44.4%. Resolution time fell by about 44%, which is an improvement.
(c) A viewer who glances at the shape may think resolution time is rising (getting worse), since lines normally rise with higher values. The unusual axis must be read, labelled clearly, or avoided.

**Q14.**
(a) Training index: 200 divided by 200 x 100 = 100; 220 divided by 200 x 100 = 110; 250 divided by 200 x 100 = 125; 300 divided by 200 x 100 = 150. Coffee index: 40 divided by 40 x 100 = 100; 44 divided by 40 x 100 = 110; 50 divided by 40 x 100 = 125; 60 divided by 40 x 100 = 150.
(b) The two lines are identical, 100, 110, 125, 150.
(c) No. Two series rising together does not show cause. A likelier explanation: both rise as the company grows (more staff means more training hours and more coffee machines). Compare per employee to test it (Chapter 24).

**Q15.**
(a) Heights above 117: 120 gives 3, 118 gives 1, 121 gives 4, 119 gives 2, 122 gives 5, 120 gives 3.
(b) Tallest 5 divided by shortest 1 = 5 times taller.
(c) Actual ratio highest to lowest = 122 divided by 118 = 1.0339, so **1.034**, about 3.4% higher. Average revenue = (120+118+121+119+122+120) divided by 6 = 720 divided by 6 = ₹120 lakh.
(d) Problems: truncated axis (makes a 3.4% range look like a fivefold difference); 3D bars (distort height comparison); no title, units or source; no indication that revenue is basically flat. Fair redraw: a plain 2D line or bar chart with the axis from 0 (or if a line, clearly labelled 110 to 130), title "Product A quarterly revenue, flat around ₹120 lakh", units in the axis label, values printed on the points, and source and period stated.

## Chapter 28

### Self-check answers

1. Understand, Plan, Execute, Review.
2. Step 1 (Understand) should have caught it. It asks "what exactly is the unknown, and what are the units?" The meeting lasts 2 hours = 120 minutes, minus a 10-minute break gives 110 minutes of talking. The answer "2" was in hours and ignored the break.
3. Step 4 (Review): check fit, reasonableness, and a second route.

---

### Practice answers (each shown in the four steps)

#### Q1 🟢 Notebook price

- **Understand:** unknown = notebook price. Givens: 4 notebooks + 3 pens = 290; pen = 30.
- **Plan:** find the pens' cost, subtract from the total, divide by 4.
- **Execute:** 3 pens = 3 x 30 = 90. Notebooks cost 290 - 90 = 200. One notebook = 200 / 4 = 50.
- **Review:** 4 x 50 = 200, plus 90 = 290. Correct.

**Answer: 50 rupees.**

#### Q2 🟢 Canteen shifts

- **Understand:** unknown = end time of the last shift. 4 shifts of 35 minutes; gaps only between shifts, so 3 gaps of 5 minutes.
- **Plan:** total minutes, then add to 11:45.
- **Execute:** shifts 4 x 35 = 140; gaps 3 x 5 = 15; total 155 minutes = 2 h 35 min. 11:45 + 2 h = 13:45; + 35 min = 14:20.
- **Review:** list: 11:45 to 12:20, gap to 12:25, 12:25 to 13:00, gap to 13:05, 13:05 to 13:40, gap to 13:45, 13:45 to 14:20. Matches. (Using 4 gaps would give 14:25, wrong.)

**Answer: 14:20.**

#### Q3 🟢 Parcels

- **Understand:** unknown = second heaviest. Clues: P > Q, R < Q, S > P (heavier).
- **Plan:** chain the clues into one line.
- **Execute:** S > P (clue 3), P > Q (clue 1), Q > R (clue 2). Order from heaviest: S, P, Q, R.
- **Review:** each clue holds in the line. Second in line is P.

**Answer: P.**

#### Q4 🟡 Pens

- **Understand:** maximise pens with at most 100 rupees. Pack: 5 pens for 40; single: 1 pen for 9. Packs are cheaper per pen (8 versus 9).
- **Plan:** casework on the number of packs (0, 1, 2; three packs cost 120, too much), then spend the rest on singles.
- **Execute:**
  - 0 packs: 100 / 9 gives 11 singles (99 rupees), 11 pens.
  - 1 pack (40): 60 left, 60 / 9 gives 6 whole singles (54 rupees), so pens = 5 + 6 = 11.
  - 2 packs (80): 20 left, 2 singles (18), pens = 10 + 2 = 12, spending 98.
- **Review:** 12 beats 11. Could 13 be possible? 13 pens with 2 packs would need 3 singles: 80 + 27 = 107 > 100. With 1 pack, 8 singles: 40 + 72 = 112. With 0 packs, 13 singles: 117. No. So 12 is the greatest.

**Answer: 12 pens, using 2 packs and 2 single pens (98 rupees).**

#### Q5 🟡 Presenters

- **Understand:** match 3 people to 3 days, all different.
- **Plan:** table and eliminate.
- **Execute:** Ravi is not Monday, not Tuesday, so Ravi is Wednesday. Tom is not Monday, and Wednesday is taken, so Tom is Tuesday. Sana gets Monday (she is not Wednesday, so that is consistent).
- **Review:** Ravi Wed (not Mon/Tue, ok), Sana Mon (not Wed, ok), Tom Tue (not Mon, ok). All different.

**Answer: Sana Monday, Tom Tuesday, Ravi Wednesday.**

#### Q6 🟡 Treasurer and secretary

- **Understand:** ordered pairs (treasurer, secretary) of different people from 6, excluding the two pairs that put Ola and Pete together.
- **Plan:** count all, subtract forbidden.
- **Execute:** all pairs 6 x 5 = 30. Forbidden: (Ola, Pete) and (Pete, Ola) = 2. Allowed = 28.
- **Review:** second route by treasurer: Ola has 4 allowed secretaries (not herself, not Pete); Pete has 4; the other four members have 5 each: 4 + 4 + 4 x 5 = 28. Same.

**Answer: 28.**

#### Q7 🟡 Two workers, five jobs

- **Understand:** shortest finishing time; durations P3, Q2 (after P), R4, S2 (after R), T1 (after Q and S); two workers.
- **Plan:** find the longest chain of waiting jobs, then try to fit two workers around it.
- **Execute:**
  - Chain through P and Q: P (3) then Q (2) = 5 hours.
  - Chain through R and S: R (4) then S (2) = 6 hours.
  - T needs both chains finished, so T starts at hour 6 at the earliest and ends at hour 7.
  - Plan: worker 1 does P then Q (hours 0 to 5), then waits; worker 2 does R then S (0 to 6); T by either worker from 6 to 7.
- **Review:** the chain R, S, T is 4 + 2 + 1 = 7 hours and must run in sequence, so nothing shorter is possible. Brute force over all orders and worker assignments confirms 7.

**Answer: 7 hours.**

#### Q8 🔴 Tickets

- **Understand:** let a = 50-rupee tickets, b = 30-rupee, c = 20-rupee. a + b + c = 10; 50a + 30b + 20c = 320; a = b.
- **Plan:** use a = b to remove one unknown, then use the ticket count to remove another.
- **Execute:** with b = a: 2a + c = 10, so c = 10 - 2a. Money: 50a + 30a + 20(10 - 2a) = 320, so 80a + 200 - 40a = 320, so 40a = 120, so a = 3. Then b = 3 and c = 4.
- **Review:** 3 + 3 + 4 = 10 tickets; 150 + 90 + 80 = 320. Correct. (Brute force over all splits of 10 tickets: only 3, 3, 4 satisfies all conditions.)

**Answer: three 50-rupee, three 30-rupee, four 20-rupee tickets.**

#### Q9 🔴 Lock code

- **Understand:** digits H, T, U, all different, H + T + U = 12, H = 2U, T > H, code odd (so U is odd).
- **Plan:** list possible U (odd digits, since H = 2U must be a digit up to 9), then test.
- **Execute:** U must be odd and 2U at most 9, so U is 1, 3 (U = 5 would make H = 10, not a digit).
  - U = 1: H = 2, T = 12 - 2 - 1 = 9. T = 9 > 2 yes. Digits 2, 9, 1 are different. Code 291.
  - U = 3: H = 6, T = 12 - 6 - 3 = 3. T equals U, digits not different (and 3 is not above 6). Fails.
- **Review:** 2 + 9 + 1 = 12; 2 = 2 x 1; 9 > 2; 291 is odd. Without the "odd" clue, 462 would also fit (U = 2, H = 4 = 2 x 2, T = 6, and 4 + 6 + 2 = 12, 6 > 4), so the "odd" clue is what makes the code unique.

**Answer: 291.**

#### Q10 🔴 Floors and drinks

- **Understand:** 4 people, floors 1 to 4 (all different), drinks all different. Six clues.
- **Plan:** place the firm facts first (Jon floor 4, coffee on floor 1), then use the rest.
- **Execute:**
  1. Jon is on floor 4. The coffee drinker is on floor 1.
  2. Lea drinks neither tea nor coffee, and not juice, so Lea drinks water.
  3. Kai is below Lea, so Lea is not on floor 1 and Lea is not floor 4 (Jon). Lea is on floor 2 or 3; Kai is on a lower floor.
  4. Lea (water) is not the coffee drinker, so the coffee drinker is on floor 1 and is not Lea. If Lea were on floor 2, Kai would be on floor 1 (the only floor below). Then Kai drinks coffee, and the tea drinker is directly above Kai, on floor 2, which is Lea, but Lea drinks water. Contradiction. So Lea is on floor 3.
  5. Kai is below Lea: floor 1 or 2. Ira takes the remaining floor. Floors left for Ira and Kai: 1 and 2.
  6. The tea drinker is directly above Kai. If Kai is on floor 2, tea would be on floor 3 (Lea, water). Contradiction. So Kai is on floor 1 and Ira on floor 2. Kai is on floor 1, so Kai drinks coffee (the floor 1 drinker). The tea drinker is on floor 2: Ira.
  7. Remaining drink: juice goes to Jon.
- **Review:** tea (Ira, floor 2) is directly above Kai (floor 1). Jon is on 4. Lea drinks water (not tea, coffee or juice). Coffee drinker (Kai) is on floor 1. Kai (1) is below Lea (3). Brute force over all 576 assignments confirms this is the only solution.

**Answer: Kai floor 1 coffee, Ira floor 2 tea, Lea floor 3 water, Jon floor 4 juice.**

## Chapter 29

### Self-check answers

1. Work backward, simplify, find patterns, casework, elimination.
2. Work backward. You know the end result, so you undo each step in reverse order with its opposite.
3. Casework. Split by one question (for example how many 50 notes are used), and count each case separately.

---

### Practice answers

#### Q1 🟢 Leaving for the office (work backward)
Total journey: 10 + 7 + 28 + 5 = 50 minutes. Arrival 9:05 minus 50 minutes: 9:05 minus 5 is 9:00, minus 45 more is 8:15. Check forward: 8:15 + 10 = 8:25; +7 = 8:32; +28 = 9:00; +5 = 9:05. **Answer: 8:15.**

#### Q2 🟢 Think of a number (work backward)
Forward steps: add 7, times 3, minus 9, result 30. Undo in reverse: add 9 gives 39; divide by 3 gives 13; subtract 7 gives 6. Check: 6 + 7 = 13; x 3 = 39; minus 9 = 30. **Answer: 6.**

#### Q3 🟢 Ribbon cuts (simplify)
Simplify: a 10 m ribbon cut into 5 m pieces gives 2 pieces with 1 cut. So cuts = pieces - 1. Pieces for 60 m: 60 / 5 = 12. Cuts: 12 - 1 = 11. Check: 11 cuts make 12 pieces, 12 x 5 = 60. **Answer: 11 cuts.**

#### Q4 🟢 Seats in the 12th row (patterns)
Row 1 has 4, and each row adds 3. Row n has 4 + 3 x (n - 1). Check small: row 2 = 4 + 3 = 7, row 3 = 4 + 6 = 10, matches. Row 12 = 4 + 3 x 11 = 4 + 33 = 37. Why 11 and not 12: from row 1 to row 12 there are 11 steps. **Answer: 37 seats.**

#### Q5 🟢 Lunch combinations (casework by subtraction)
Count all, then remove the banned ones. All: 4 mains x 3 sides x 2 drinks = 24. Banned: fish with rice, with either drink: 1 x 1 x 2 = 2. Allowed: 24 - 2 = 22. Casework check: fish main has 2 allowed sides (salad, soup) x 2 drinks = 4; each of the other 3 mains has 3 sides x 2 drinks = 6, giving 18; 4 + 18 = 22. **Answer: 22.**

#### Q6 🟢 Even multiple of 9 (elimination)
Multiples of 9 in the list: 45 (5 x 9), 63 (7 x 9), 72 (8 x 9). Others: 21, 34, 52 are not multiples of 9. Of the three, only 72 is even. **Answer: 72.**

#### Q7 🟢 Petty cash (work backward)
End: 450. Wednesday one quarter was spent, so 450 is three quarters of the amount before: 450 / (3/4) = 600. Undo Tuesday: subtract 200 gives 400. Undo Monday: half was spent, so 400 is half: start = 800. Check: 800, half spent leaves 400; plus 200 is 600; a quarter spent (150) leaves 450. **Answer: 800.**

#### Q8 🟢 Lift (simplify)
Floor 1 to floor 4 is 3 floor-gaps (1 to 2, 2 to 3, 3 to 4), not 4. 12 seconds for 3 gaps means 4 seconds per gap. Floor 1 to floor 8 is 7 gaps: 7 x 4 = 28 seconds. **Answer: 28 seconds.**

#### Q9 🟡 Chairs in a triangle (patterns)
Totals: 1 row gives 1; 2 rows 3; 3 rows 6; 4 rows 10. Each new row adds its row number. For 12 rows: 1 + 2 + ... + 12. Pair from both ends: (1 + 12) = 13, (2 + 11) = 13, and so on, 6 pairs: 6 x 13 = 78. Check by adding on: 10 (4 rows), 15, 21, 28, 36, 45, 55, 66, 78. **Answer: 78 chairs.**

#### Q10 🟡 Odd numbers over 300 (casework)
Split by the hundreds digit (must be 3, 4 or 5 to exceed 300).
- Hundreds 3: remaining digits 1, 2, 4, 5. Units must be odd: 1 or 5 (2 choices). Tens: any of the 3 digits left. 2 x 3 = 6.
- Hundreds 4: remaining 1, 2, 3, 5. Odd units: 1, 3, 5 (3 choices); tens: 3 left. 3 x 3 = 9.
- Hundreds 5: remaining 1, 2, 3, 4. Odd units: 1, 3 (2 choices); tens: 3 left. 2 x 3 = 6.
Total: 6 + 9 + 6 = 21. A computer count over all numbers agrees. **Answer: 21.**

#### Q11 🟡 Pets (elimination)
- Ana: not cat, not dog, not bird, so Ana owns the fish.
- Ben: not fish, not bird, so cat or dog.
- Cara: not bird, not cat, so dog or fish; fish is Ana's, so Cara owns the dog.
- Ben then owns the cat (dog is taken).
- Dev gets the bird (not a dog, consistent).
Check all clues: every one holds. Brute force over all 24 assignments gives only this one. **Answer: Ana fish, Ben cat, Cara dog, Dev bird.**

#### Q12 🟡 Upload deadline (work backward)
Total time: drafting 135 minutes + editing 65 + lunch 45 + approval 50 + upload 10 = 305 minutes = 5 h 5 min. Work from 17:00: minus 10 (upload) = 16:50; minus 50 (approval) = 16:00; minus 45 (lunch) = 15:15; minus 65 (editing) = 14:10; minus 135 (drafting) = 11:55. Check: 17:00 minus 5 h 5 min = 11:55. **Answer: 11:55.**

#### Q13 🟡 Committee with at least one man (casework)
Cases by the number of men:
- 1 man and 2 women: 3 x 6 = 18 (there are 6 pairs of women among 4 women).
- 2 men and 1 woman: 3 pairs of men x 4 women = 12.
- 3 men: 1.
Total: 18 + 12 + 1 = 31. Check by subtraction: all committees of 3 from 7 people = 35; committees with no man (3 women from 4) = 4; 35 - 4 = 31. **Answer: 31.**

#### Q14 🟡 Matchstick staircase (patterns)
Small cases: 1 column: one square, 4 sticks. Add the second column (height 2): 3 horizontal lines (bottom, middle, top) = 3 sticks, plus 2 sticks on its right side; its left side already exists except for 1 more stick on the part above the first column. New = 3 + 2 + 1 = 6, total 10. In general the k-th column adds (k + 1) horizontals + k on its right + 1 new left stick = 2k + 2. Totals: 4, 10, 18, 28, 40, 54, ... which equals n x (n + 3) (check: 3 x 6 = 18, 4 x 7 = 28). For n = 10: 10 x 13 = 130. Also the sum of (2k + 2) for k = 1 to 10 is 2 x 55 + 20 = 130. A computer count of unique edges for n = 10 also gives 130. **Answer: 130 matchsticks.**

#### Q15 🟡 The machine (work backward, branching)
Rule: even gives half; odd gives plus 3. Undo each step. A result r came from either 2r (since 2r is even and halving gives r) or from r - 3 if r - 3 is odd and positive.
- After step 3 the number is 5. Before: 10 (even, halves to 5). Or 5 - 3 = 2, which is even, so adding 3 is not what the machine does to 2 (it would halve it). So only 10.
- Before reaching 10: from 20 (halves to 10) or from 7 (odd, 7 + 3 = 10). Both valid.
- Before reaching 20: from 40, or from 17 (odd, 17 + 3 = 20). Valid.
- Before reaching 7: from 14, or from 4 (even, so it would halve, not add 3). Only 14.
Starting numbers: 40, 17, 14. Check: 40 to 20 to 10 to 5. 17 to 20 to 10 to 5. 14 to 7 to 10 to 5. A computer search of 1 to 199 finds only these. **Answer: 14, 17 and 40.**

#### Q16 🟡 Offsite day (elimination)
Start with Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday. Not Monday: cross out Monday. Not Friday: cross out Friday. Board meeting is Tuesday, so not Tuesday: cross out Tuesday. Not the day after the board meeting, which is Wednesday: cross out Wednesday. Left: Thursday. **Answer: Thursday.**

#### Q17 🟡 Paying 60 (casework)
Split by the number of 20 notes (1, 2 or 3; at least one).
- One 20: 40 left using 5s and 10s. Tens: 0 to 4 (5 ways), and the rest in fives. 5 ways.
- Two 20s: 20 left. Tens: 0, 1 or 2. 3 ways.
- Three 20s: 0 left. 1 way.
Total: 5 + 3 + 1 = 9. A computer count agrees. **Answer: 9 combinations.**

#### Q18 🔴 Stamps 6 and 11 for 100 (casework with elimination)
Equation: 6a + 11b = 100, with a and b at least 1. Split by the number of 11-stamps b = 1 to 9.
- b = 1: 89 left, not a multiple of 6. b = 2: 78 = 6 x 13, yes (a = 13). b = 3: 67 no. b = 4: 56 no. b = 5: 45 no. b = 6: 34 no. b = 7: 23 no. b = 8: 12 = 6 x 2, yes (a = 2). b = 9: 1 no.
Two ways: 13 sixes and 2 elevens (15 stamps), or 2 sixes and 8 elevens (10 stamps). Check: 78 + 22 = 100 and 12 + 88 = 100. **Answer: 2 ways; the fewest stamps is 10 (two 6-stamps and eight 11-stamps).**

#### Q19 🔴 Timing 9 minutes with 7 and 4 (work backward and trial)
Goal 9 = 7 + 2, or 8 + 1, or 4 + 4 + 1. Plan: aim to reach minute 8 (when the 4 timer finishes twice) with exactly 1 minute of work left on the 7 timer.
- Minute 0: start both timers.
- Minute 4: the 4 timer ends. Flip it so it runs again.
- Minute 7: the 7 timer ends. Flip it at once (it restarts as a full 7 minutes). The 4 timer has been running for 3 minutes of its second run, with 1 minute left.
- Minute 8: the 4 timer ends. At this moment the 7 timer has run 1 minute since it was flipped at minute 7, so it has 6 minutes in the top and 1 in the bottom. Flip it now: the 1 minute of sand in the bottom is now on top.
- Minute 9: that 1 minute of sand runs out. Exactly 9 minutes have passed.
A search over all flip choices at the end of each timer shows that flips at minutes 4, 7 and 8 reach minute 9. **Answer: start both at 0; flip the 4 timer at minute 4; flip the 7 timer at minute 7 and again at minute 8; stop when it ends at minute 9.**

#### Q20 🔴 Interview order (casework / simplify)
Treat "E then D" as one block [ED], because D must come immediately after E. Now there are four units: A, B, C, [ED]. Orders of 4 units: 24.
- Keep only the orders where A is before B: half of 24 = 12.
- Remove those with C first: C first, then A, B, [ED] in the other three spots with A before B: 3 orders (3 spots for the block's position, A and B fixed in order). So 12 - 3 = 9.
- "A is not last" is already guaranteed (A must be before B), so it removes nothing.
List of the nine: ABCED, ABEDC, ACBED, ACEDB, AEDBC, AEDCB, EDABC, EDACB, EDCAB. Check a few against the clues: all have A before B, E immediately followed by D, and C not first. A computer count of all 120 orders gives 9. **Answer: 9.**

#### Q21 🔴 Passcode (elimination)
Digits sets from the guesses (the code has 4 different digits):
- Guess 1234 has 0 + 2 = 2 digits in the code. Guess 5678 has 1 + 1 = 2 digits in the code. That makes 4 digits, so the code has no 0 and no 9.
- Guess 2580: 1 + 2 = 3 digits in the code; 0 is not, so 2, 5 and 8 are all in the code.
- 2 is from the first set, so one more digit from {1, 3, 4}; 5 and 8 are the two from the second set (so 6 and 7 are not in the code).
- Guess 9071: only 1 digit is in the code; 9 and 0 are out, 7 is out, so 1 is in. The code uses the digits 1, 2, 5, 8.
Positions. Guess 2580 has exactly 1 hit: one of 2 first, 5 second, 8 third. Guess 5678 has exactly 1 hit: one of 5 first, 8 fourth. Guess 5120 has exactly 1 hit: one of 5 first, 1 second, 2 third. Guess 1234: 1 is not first, 2 is not second.
- Case 5 first: then 8 is not fourth. Guess 2580 needs a hit: 2 first (no, 5 is there), 5 second (no), so 8 third. Remaining 1 and 2 for second and fourth: 2 is not second, so 1 second, 2 fourth: 5182. Guess 5120 then has 5 first and 1 second, 2 hits, but it must be 1. Fails.
- Case 8 fourth (5 not first): guess 2580 needs one hit from 2 first or 5 second. If 5 second (and 2 not first): 1 and 2 go to first and third; 1 is not first, so 2 is first, a contradiction. If 2 first (and 5 not second): 5 must be third, 1 second: 2158. Check guess 5120: 1 second is a hit; 5 and 2 are in the wrong places (5 first? no. 2 third? no): 1 hit, 2 nears. Correct.
Check all five guesses against 2158: 1234 gives 0 hits, 2 nears (1 and 2 present, in wrong places). 5678 gives 1 hit (8 is fourth in both) and 1 near (5 is in the code but not first). 2580 gives 1 hit (2 is first in both) and 2 nears (5 and 8 are in the code in other places). 9071 gives 1 near. All match. A computer search of all 5,040 codes finds only 2158. **Answer: 2158.**

#### Q22 🔴 Six jobs, two people (work backward on the chain)
Durations: A2, B4, C3, D2 (after A), E3 (after B and C), F1 (after D and E).
- F is last and needs E; E needs B and C. The chain B (4), then E (3), then F (1) is 4 + 3 + 1 = 8 hours, and these must happen in order, so nothing can finish before hour 8.
- A schedule that achieves 8: Person 1 does B (hours 0 to 4), then E (4 to 7), then F (7 to 8). Person 2 does C (0 to 3), then A (3 to 5), then D (5 to 7). E starts at 4 (B ends at 4, C ended at 3). F starts at 7 (E ends at 7, D ends at 7).
- Check dependencies: D after A (A ends 5, D starts 5), E after B and C (4 and 3), F after D and E (7 and 7). All good. A computer search over all orders and worker assignments also finds 8 as the minimum.
**Answer: 8 hours (person 1: B, E, F; person 2: C, A, D).**

#### Q23 🔴 PIN codes (casework)
Split by the set of four different digits that add to 10. The possible sets are {0, 1, 2, 7}, {0, 1, 3, 6}, {0, 1, 4, 5}, {0, 2, 3, 5} and {1, 2, 3, 4}. (Check: 0 + 1 + 2 + 7 = 10, 0 + 1 + 3 + 6 = 10, 0 + 1 + 4 + 5 = 10, 0 + 2 + 3 + 5 = 10, 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10. No other set of four different digits works.)
For each set count the arrangements with the first digit not 0 and the last digit even:
- {0, 1, 2, 7}: 10 arrangements.
- {0, 1, 3, 6}: 10.
- {0, 1, 4, 5}: 10.
- {0, 2, 3, 5}: 10.
- {1, 2, 3, 4}: 12.
(Example of how to count one set: {1, 2, 3, 4} has no 0, last digit 2 or 4 (2 choices), the other three digits in any order (6 ways): 12. For {0, 1, 2, 7}: last digit 0 gives 6 orders of the others; last digit 2 gives 3 orders with 0 not first among {0, 1, 7}: 6 - 2 = 4; total 6 + 4 = 10.)
Total: 10 + 10 + 10 + 10 + 12 = 52. A computer count of all 4-digit codes agrees. **Answer: 52.**

#### Q24 🔴 Squares on a 7 by 5 floor (patterns)
Simplify: on a 3 by 2 grid, there are 6 squares of size 1 and 2 squares of size 2: 8 in all. Pattern: squares of size k can start in (length - k + 1) x (width - k + 1) positions.
For 7 by 5:
- 1 by 1: 7 x 5 = 35
- 2 by 2: 6 x 4 = 24
- 3 by 3: 5 x 3 = 15
- 4 by 4: 4 x 2 = 8
- 5 by 5: 3 x 1 = 3
- 6 by 6: does not fit (only 5 wide).
Total: 35 + 24 + 15 + 8 + 3 = 85. A computer count agrees. **Answer: 85 squares.**

#### Q25 🔴 Token game (work backward)
Work from the end. Call a pile "losing" if the player to move from it will lose with best play by the opponent.
- Pile 0 (no tokens): the player to move has lost (the other took the last token). Losing.
- Pile 1: take 1 and win. Winning. Pile 2: take 1 leaves 1, a winning pile for the opponent; the only move is 1 (4 is too many). So pile 2 is losing.
- Pile 3: take 1 leaves 2 (losing for the opponent). Winning. Pile 4: take 4 and win. Winning.
- Pile 5: take 1 leaves 4 (winning for the opponent); take 4 leaves 1 (winning for the opponent). Losing.
- Pile 6: take 1 leaves 5 (losing). Winning. Pile 7: take 1 leaves 6 (winning), take 4 leaves 3 (winning). Losing.
The losing piles are 0, 2, 5, 7, 10, 12, 15, 17, 20, 22, ... (the remainder is 0 or 2 when divided by 5). From 23, taking 1 leaves 22, a losing pile for your opponent. Taking 4 would leave 19, a winning pile for the opponent (they take 4 to leave 15). After you leave a losing pile, every move your opponent can make leaves a winning pile, and from that pile you can move to a losing pile again (the list above shows how). Example: from 22, if the opponent takes 1 leaving 21, you take 1 leaving 20; if they take 4 leaving 18, you take 1 leaving 17.
**Answer: take 1 token, leaving 22, a losing pile for your opponent.**

## Chapter 30: Proof Techniques

All results were re-checked by code (brute force over ranges of whole numbers for the true statements, and a direct search for the counterexamples).

### Self-check answers

1. **False.** The claim is a "for all" claim ("every employee"). One employee without a laptop is a counterexample, and one counterexample is enough to make it false.
2. **No.** Three examples cannot cover all numbers. The claim is in fact false: 4 is a number you could have picked and it is even.
3. Checking examples looks at some cases and can only suggest a pattern or find a counterexample. A proof is an argument that covers every case.

---

### Practice answers

**Q1** 🟢 **True (direct proof).**
1. Write the two odd numbers as 2a + 1 and 2b + 1. *Why:* that is the definition of odd.
2. Add: (2a + 1) + (2b + 1) = 2a + 2b + 2. *Why:* the two 1s combine into 2.
3. Pull out 2: 2(a + b + 1). *Why:* each part has a factor 2.
4. a + b + 1 is a whole number, so the result is 2 times a whole number, which is even. Proved.

**Q2** 🟢 **True (direct proof).**
1. A multiple of 4 is 4m for some whole number m.
2. 4m = 2 × (2m). *Why:* 4 is 2 times 2.
3. 2m is a whole number, so 4m is 2 times a whole number: a multiple of 2. Proved.

**Q3** 🟢 **False.** Counterexample: 2 is prime and even (its only divisors are 1 and 2). One case is enough to kill "every prime is odd".

**Q4** 🟢 **True, and yes, here checking is a proof.**
1. The statement is about a finite, complete list of five employees.
2. Check each row: 74, 88, 71, 92, 70 are all 70 or more.
3. Since every case on the list was checked, nothing is left uncovered. (This works only because the list is the *whole* group and is finite.)

**Q5** 🟢 **False.** Lata works the evening shift. She is a Support team member who does not work mornings, so she is a counterexample.

**Q6** 🟢 **False.** Counterexample: 2 and 3 are both prime and 2 + 3 = 5, which is odd. (Other examples: 2 + 5 = 7, 2 + 7 = 9.)

**Q7** 🟢 **False.** Counterexample: 3 is a multiple of 3 but not a multiple of 6 (3 is not 6 times a whole number). Another: 9.

**Q8** 🟡 **True (direct proof).**
1. Write the odd number as n = 2k + 1.
2. n² = (2k + 1)(2k + 1) = 4k² + 4k + 1. *Why:* expand the brackets: 2k times 2k is 4k², then 2k and 2k give 4k, then 1.
3. 4k² + 4k + 1 = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1. *Why:* pull a 2 out of the first two parts.
4. That is "2 times a whole number, plus 1": odd. Proved.

**Q9** 🟡 **True (direct proof, two cases).**
1. Split into cases because every whole number is even or odd.
2. **Case n even.** n² is even (even times even is even, since n × n = 2k × n = 2(kn)) and n is even; even + even is even (Example 1).
3. **Case n odd.** n² is odd (Q8) and n is odd; odd + odd is even (Q1).
4. Both cases give even. Proved. (A shorter route: n² + n = n(n + 1), and one of two consecutive numbers is always even, so the product is even.)

**Q10** 🟡 **False.** Counterexample: n = 1. Then n² = 1 and 2n = 2, and 1 is less than 2. (It works for n = 0, 2, 3, ... which is why the failing case is easy to miss.)

**Q11** 🟡 **True (direct proof by elimination).**
1. Two whole numbers are (even, even), (odd, odd) or (even, odd) in some order.
2. (even, even) gives an even sum (Example 1), so it cannot give an odd sum.
3. (odd, odd) gives an even sum (Q1), so it cannot either.
4. The only case left is one even and one odd. Proved.

**Q12** 🟡 **True (direct proof).**
1. Call the numbers n, n + 1, n + 2.
2. Sum = n + n + 1 + n + 2 = 3n + 3. *Why:* three n's make 3n, and 1 + 2 makes 3.
3. 3n + 3 = 3(n + 1), which is 3 times a whole number. Proved.

**Q13** 🟡 **False.** Counterexample: 12 is divisible by 4 (12 = 4 × 3) and by 6 (12 = 6 × 2), but not by 24. (36 also works.) The correct statement is that it must be divisible by 12.

**Q14** 🟡 **True (proof by contradiction).**
1. Assume no two of the 7 employees chose the same menu.
2. Then the 7 employees chose 7 different menus.
3. There are only 6 menus. Impossible.
4. So at least two chose the same menu. Proved.

**Q15** 🔴 **False.** Test n = 0, 1, 2, ...: the values 17, 19, 23, 29, 37, 47, 59, 73, 89, 107, 127, 149, 173, 199, 227, 257 are all prime, but n = 16 gives 256 + 16 + 17 = 289 = 17 × 17, not prime. Counterexample: n = 16. (Sixteen agreeing cases, still false.)

**Q16** 🔴 **True (proof by contradiction).**
1. Assume n² is a multiple of 3 but n is **not** a multiple of 3.
2. Then n leaves remainder 1 or 2 when divided by 3: n = 3k + 1 or n = 3k + 2.
3. If n = 3k + 1: n² = 9k² + 6k + 1 = 3(3k² + 2k) + 1. Remainder 1.
4. If n = 3k + 2: n² = 9k² + 12k + 4 = 3(3k² + 4k + 1) + 1. Remainder 1.
5. In both cases n² leaves remainder 1, so it is not a multiple of 3. That contradicts our assumption.
6. So n is a multiple of 3. Proved.

**Q17** 🔴 **True (proof by contradiction).**
1. Assume 4k + 2 equals n × n for some whole number n.
2. n is even or odd.
3. **If n is even**, n = 2m, so n² = 4m², a multiple of 4. But 4k + 2 leaves remainder 2 when divided by 4, so it is not a multiple of 4. Clash.
4. **If n is odd**, n² is odd (Q8). But 4k + 2 = 2(2k + 1) is even. Clash.
5. Both cases are impossible, so no number of the form 4k + 2 is a perfect square. Proved.

**Q18** 🔴 **True (proof by contradiction).**
1. Dividing by 4, the remainder is 0, 1, 2 or 3: four possible remainders.
2. Assume no two of the five numbers share a remainder. Then the five numbers have five different remainders.
3. Only four remainders exist. Impossible.
4. So two numbers have the same remainder r: a = 4p + r and b = 4q + r.
5. a minus b = 4p minus 4q = 4(p minus q), a multiple of 4. Proved.

**Q19** 🔴 **False.** Counterexample: 2 and 3. Their product is 6, which is even, but 3 is not even. (Also 1 and 4: product 4.)

**Q20** 🔴 **True (proof by contradiction).**
1. Assume that every batch has at most 2 trainees.
2. Then the total is at most 9 batches times 2 trainees = 18 trainees.
3. But there are 19 trainees. Impossible.
4. So some batch has at least 3 trainees. Proved.

## Chapter 31: Intro to Mathematical Induction

All formulas below were re-checked by code for n from 1 to 299, and the voucher results (Example 4, Q9) were found by exhaustive search of all combinations. The Example 5 "flawed" formula was confirmed to be exactly 1 more than the true sum for n = 1 to 4.

### Self-check answers

1. You need the passing-along rule: "whenever any domino falls, it knocks over the next one". Then domino 1 falls, which makes 2 fall, and so on.
2. **No.** Five matching cases are evidence, not a proof. Induction (or another full argument) is needed to be sure about n = 6 and beyond. (Example 5 shows a rule that fails at n = 1 even though its step works; Chapter 30 Example 3 shows a pattern that held for ten values and then failed.)
3. Because without the first fall, nothing ever starts. Dominoes that would pass the fall along are useless if nobody pushes the first one.

---

### Practice answers

**Q1** 🟢
1. Fact (A) "you can stand on step 1" is the **base case** (the first domino).
2. Fact (B) "from any step you can climb to the next" is the **inductive step** (each domino knocks over the next).
3. Together: you reach step 1, so step 2, so step 3, and so on up to step 40. **Conclusion:** you can reach step 40 (and any step on the staircase).

**Q2** 🟢 Nothing can be concluded about reaching any particular step. Fact (B) only says "if you are on a step, you can go to the next"; without (A) you never get onto the first step, so the chain never starts. (You might reach step 40 for some other reason, but this reasoning does not show it.)

**Q3** 🟢

| n | Sum added by hand | n(n + 1) / 2 |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 × 2 / 2 = 1 |
| 2 | 1 + 2 = 3 | 2 × 3 / 2 = 3 |
| 3 | 1 + 2 + 3 = 6 | 3 × 4 / 2 = 6 |
| 4 | 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 | 4 × 5 / 2 = 10 |
| 5 | 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15 | 5 × 6 / 2 = 15 |

All five rows agree. This is only a check of five cases; it does not show the formula works for n = 6 or 6,000. An induction proof also needs the step "if it works for k then it works for k + 1" for a general k.

**Q4** 🟡 **Claim:** 1 + 4 + 7 + ... + (3n minus 2) = n(3n minus 1) / 2.
1. **Base case (n = 1):** left side is 3 × 1 minus 2 = 1. Right side: 1 × (3 minus 1) / 2 = 1 × 2 / 2 = 1. Match.
2. **Assume** the claim for k: 1 + 4 + ... + (3k minus 2) = k(3k minus 1) / 2.
3. **Next term:** the term after (3k minus 2) is 3(k + 1) minus 2 = 3k + 1.
4. **Add it:** sum to k + 1 = k(3k minus 1) / 2 + (3k + 1) = (3k² minus k + 6k + 2) / 2 = (3k² + 5k + 2) / 2. *Why:* write 3k + 1 as (6k + 2) / 2 to share the denominator.
5. **Factor:** 3k² + 5k + 2 = (k + 1)(3k + 2). Check: (k + 1)(3k + 2) = 3k² + 2k + 3k + 2. Yes.
6. **Target for n = k + 1:** (k + 1)(3(k + 1) minus 1) / 2 = (k + 1)(3k + 2) / 2. It matches step 5.
7. By induction the claim is true for every n. (Quick check: n = 4 gives 1 + 4 + 7 + 10 = 22 and 4 × 11 / 2 = 22.)

**Q5** 🟡 **Claim:** handshakes among n people = n(n minus 1) / 2.
1. **Base case (n = 1):** one person has 0 handshakes; formula 1 × 0 / 2 = 0. Match. (n = 2 gives 1 handshake and 2 × 1 / 2 = 1, also fine.)
2. **Assume** k people have k(k minus 1) / 2 handshakes.
3. **New arrival:** the (k + 1)-th person shakes hands with each of the k people already there: k new handshakes. *Why:* every earlier handshake is still counted once; the newcomer only adds handshakes involving themselves.
4. **Total:** k(k minus 1) / 2 + k = (k² minus k + 2k) / 2 = (k² + k) / 2 = k(k + 1) / 2.
5. **Target for n = k + 1:** (k + 1)((k + 1) minus 1) / 2 = (k + 1) × k / 2. It matches.
6. Proved. Example: 10 people give 10 × 9 / 2 = 45 handshakes.

**Q6** 🟡 **Claim:** 5 + 10 + ... + 5n = 5n(n + 1) / 2.
1. **Base case (n = 1):** left is 5; right is 5 × 1 × 2 / 2 = 5. Match.
2. **Assume** 5 + 10 + ... + 5k = 5k(k + 1) / 2.
3. **Add the next week's saving,** 5(k + 1): total = 5k(k + 1) / 2 + 5(k + 1).
4. **Pull out 5(k + 1):** = 5(k + 1) × (k / 2 + 1) = 5(k + 1)(k + 2) / 2.
5. **Target for n = k + 1:** 5(k + 1)((k + 1) + 1) / 2 = 5(k + 1)(k + 2) / 2. Match.
6. Proved. **After 12 weeks:** 5 × 12 × 13 / 2 = 780 / 2 = **390 rupees**.

**Q7** 🟡 **Claim:** 2^n is bigger than n.
1. **Base case (n = 1):** 2^1 = 2, which is bigger than 1. True.
2. **Assume** 2^k is bigger than k.
3. **Step:** 2^(k+1) = 2 × 2^k. Since 2^k is bigger than k, 2 × 2^k is bigger than 2k.
4. For k at least 1, 2k is at least k + 1 (because k is at least 1, so 2k = k + k is at least k + 1).
5. Chain: 2^(k+1) is bigger than 2k, and 2k is at least k + 1, so 2^(k+1) is bigger than k + 1. That is the claim for k + 1.
6. Proved. (Values: 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 against 1, 2, 3, 4, 5.)

**Q8** 🔴 **Claim:** 4^n minus 1 is a multiple of 3.
1. **Check the hint's identity:** 4 × (4^k minus 1) + 3 = 4^(k+1) minus 4 + 3 = 4^(k+1) minus 1. Correct.
2. **Base case (n = 1):** 4 minus 1 = 3, a multiple of 3.
3. **Assume** 4^k minus 1 = 3m for some whole number m.
4. **Step:** 4^(k+1) minus 1 = 4 × (3m) + 3 = 12m + 3 = 3(4m + 1). *Why:* this is 3 times a whole number.
5. So 4^(k+1) minus 1 is a multiple of 3. Proved. (Values: 3, 15, 63, 255 are 3 × 1, 3 × 5, 3 × 21, 3 × 85.)

**Q9** 🔴
**(a)** Trying every combination of 4s and 7s, the amounts from 1 to 17 that **cannot** be paid are **1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 9, 10, 13, 17**. (For example, 17 would need 4a + 7b = 17: b = 0 gives 17 not a multiple of 4; b = 1 gives 10, no; b = 2 gives 3, no.) The payable ones are 4, 7, 8, 11, 12, 14, 15, 16.
**(b)** Base cases (four in a row, because the step reaches back 4):
- 18 = 4 + 7 + 7
- 19 = 4 + 4 + 4 + 7
- 20 = 4 × 5
- 21 = 7 × 3

Step: take any amount n of 22 or more and assume every amount from 18 up to n minus 1 can be paid. Then n minus 4 is at least 18 and below n, so it can be paid. Add one 4-rupee voucher and n is paid. So every amount from 18 on can be paid. (This is the same domino idea, except that each domino is knocked over by the one four places back, and we assume all earlier amounts, not only the one just before.) *Why four base cases:* the step uses the amount four earlier, so we must have started the chain at four different amounts.

**Q10** 🔴 The step fails when k = 1, going from groups of 1 to groups of 2.
1. With 2 employees, A and B: remove A, the remaining group is just {B}; remove B, the remaining group is just {A}.
2. The argument says "the two groups of k overlap, so A, B and everyone else share a size". For k = 1 the two groups {B} and {A} do **not** overlap at all, so there is nothing linking A's size to B's.
3. For k at least 2 the overlap argument is fine, but it needs "every group of 2 shares a size" as its starting point, and that was never proved. So the chain breaks at the first link: the base case n = 1 does not lead to n = 2. The step is valid only when the overlap exists (k at least 2), so it cannot be used to get from 1 to 2.
4. The conclusion is plainly false (two people can have different shoe sizes). **Lesson:** the step must work for *every* k from the base case on, including the first one.


---


## Chapter 32: Algorithmic Thinking

These are model solutions. Any correct, clear procedure is acceptable. Every sample result below was recomputed by running an equivalent program on the sample data (including the guess counts for Q18 and the totals for Q19).

### Self-check answers

1. **No, it is a goal.** An algorithm must say *how*: exact steps, in order, that anyone can follow (for example: take the first sale as the best so far, compare each remaining sale, keep the larger).
2. "Start with the largest number" is circular: you do not know the largest number yet. Fix: start with the **first number in the list** as the best guess so far, then compare the rest.
3. Decide in advance and write it: usually "show 'no data' and stop". Without a rule, "take the first value" has nothing to take.

---

### Practice answers

**Q1** 🟢 Smallest number.
Plain steps: (1) if the list is empty, show "no data" and stop; (2) call the first number the smallest so far; (3) look at each other number; if it is smaller, it becomes the smallest so far; (4) show the smallest so far.
```text
GIVEN a list called daily_sales
IF daily_sales is empty THEN
    SHOW "no data"
    STOP
END IF

SET smallest_so_far TO the first value in daily_sales
FOR EACH sale IN the rest of daily_sales
    IF sale < smallest_so_far THEN
        SET smallest_so_far TO sale
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW smallest_so_far
```
Trace for 320, 280, 415, 260, 390: start 320; 280 smaller (280); 415 no; 260 smaller (260); 390 no. **Answer: 260.**

**Q2** 🟢 Total.
Plain steps: start a total at 0; add each amount; show the total (an empty list gives 0, which is sensible).
```text
GIVEN a list called order_amounts
SET total_amount TO 0
FOR EACH amount IN order_amounts
    SET total_amount TO total_amount + amount
END FOR
SHOW total_amount
```
Trace: 0, 150, 350, 425, 550. **Answer: 550.**

**Q3** 🟢 Count more than 100.
```text
GIVEN a list called numbers
SET count_above_100 TO 0
FOR EACH number IN numbers
    IF number > 100 THEN
        SET count_above_100 TO count_above_100 + 1
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW count_above_100
```
Trace: 90 no, 120 yes (1), 100 no (not more than 100), 150 yes (2), 101 yes (3), 99 no. **Answer: 3.**

**Q4** 🟢 Attendance award.
Plain steps: take the days present; if it is 22 or more, the result is "award"; otherwise "no award".
```text
GIVEN days_present
IF days_present >= 22 THEN
    SHOW "award"
ELSE
    SHOW "no award"
END IF
```
Trace: 22 gives award (22 is "at least 22"); 21 gives no award; 26 gives award. (The total working days, 26, is only context here; the rule uses the 22.)

**Q5** 🟢 Multiples of 5 up to a limit.
Plain steps: start at 5; while the number is not above the limit, show it and add 5.
```text
GIVEN a number called limit
SET current_number TO 5
WHILE current_number <= limit
    SHOW current_number
    SET current_number TO current_number + 5
END WHILE
```
Trace for limit 23: 5 shown, 10 shown, 15 shown, 20 shown, next is 25 which is more than 23, so stop. **Answer: 5, 10, 15, 20.**

**Q6** 🟢 Tea for one (one possible answer).
Plain steps: (1) fill the kettle with enough water for one cup and boil it; (2) put one teaspoon of tea leaves or one teabag in a cup; (3) pour in the hot water and wait three minutes; (4) take out the teabag or strain the leaves; (5) if there is milk, add a splash; otherwise skip milk; (6) add sugar only if wanted; (7) serve.
```text
FILL kettle with water for one cup
BOIL the water
PUT one teabag IN the cup
POUR hot water INTO the cup
WAIT 3 minutes
REMOVE the teabag
IF milk is available THEN
    ADD a splash of milk
ELSE
    SHOW "serving without milk"
END IF
SERVE the cup
```
Points checked: steps are ordered (water cannot be poured before it boils), finite, and the missing-milk case is handled.

**Q7** 🟢 First employee older than 50.
```text
GIVEN a list of employees, each with name and age
FOR EACH employee IN the list
    IF employee.age > 50 THEN
        SHOW employee.name
        STOP
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW "nobody is older than 50"
```
Trace: Ravi 34 no, Meena 47 no, Imran 52 yes, so show Imran and stop. **Answer: Imran.** (Kavya, 61, is never examined because we stopped.) For a list with no one above 50 the loop finishes and the last line shows "nobody is older than 50".

**Q8** 🟡 Average ignoring blanks (same pattern as Example 5).
```text
GIVEN a list called overtime_hours
SET total_hours TO 0
SET number_of_entries TO 0
FOR EACH entry IN overtime_hours
    IF entry is NOT blank THEN
        SET total_hours TO total_hours + entry
        SET number_of_entries TO number_of_entries + 1
    END IF
END FOR
IF number_of_entries = 0 THEN
    SHOW "no data"
ELSE
    SHOW total_hours / number_of_entries
END IF
```
Trace: 12 (total 12, count 1), blank skip, 18 (30, 2), 15 (45, 3), blank skip, 15 (60, 4). 60 / 4 = **15**. If all entries are blank the count stays 0 and "no data" is shown (avoiding division by zero).

**Q9** 🟡 Largest and smallest in one pass.
```text
GIVEN a list called numbers (not empty)
SET largest_so_far TO the first value
SET smallest_so_far TO the first value
FOR EACH number IN the rest of numbers
    IF number > largest_so_far THEN
        SET largest_so_far TO number
    END IF
    IF number < smallest_so_far THEN
        SET smallest_so_far TO number
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW largest_so_far AND smallest_so_far
```
Trace for 34, 12, 56, 7, 45: start (34, 34); 12 gives smallest 12; 56 gives largest 56; 7 gives smallest 7; 45 changes nothing. **Answer: largest 56, smallest 7.** Both tests are separate IFs (not ELSE) because one number could in principle change either value; with the first value used for both, a list of one number works too.

**Q10** 🟡 Non-decreasing check.
Plain steps: compare each number with the one before it; as soon as a number is smaller than the previous one, the answer is "no"; if you reach the end without that, the answer is "yes".
```text
GIVEN a list called numbers
IF numbers has 0 or 1 items THEN
    SHOW "yes"
    STOP
END IF
SET previous_number TO the first value
FOR EACH number IN the rest of numbers
    IF number < previous_number THEN
        SHOW "no"
        STOP
    END IF
    SET previous_number TO number
END FOR
SHOW "yes"
```
Trace for 3, 5, 5, 9: 5 vs 3 fine; 5 vs 5 fine (equal is allowed); 9 vs 5 fine; end, **yes**. Trace for 2, 6, 4, 8: 6 vs 2 fine; 4 vs 6 is smaller, so **no**. (A list with 0 or 1 items is "yes" because there is nothing to break the rule; the first IF in the pseudocode handles that case.)

**Q11** 🟡 Missing from the bank.
```text
GIVEN a list called company_records
GIVEN a list called bank_statement
SET missing_at_bank TO an empty list
FOR EACH invoice IN company_records
    IF invoice is NOT in bank_statement THEN
        ADD invoice TO missing_at_bank
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW missing_at_bank
```
Trace: A12 not in bank (add), A15 in bank, A18 not in bank (add), A21 in bank, A30 not in bank (add). **Answer: A12, A18, A30.** (A40 is in the bank but not in the records; this question did not ask for that side.)

**Q12** 🟡 Tally per department.
Plain steps: keep a tally table (department, count). For each department in the list: if it is already in the table, add 1 to its count; otherwise add it with count 1.
```text
GIVEN a list called departments
SET tally TO an empty table of (department, count)
FOR EACH department IN departments
    IF department is already in tally THEN
        SET its count TO its count + 1
    ELSE
        ADD department TO tally with count 1
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW tally
```
Trace for HR, IT, HR, Sales, IT, HR, Ops: HR 1; IT 1; HR 2; Sales 1; IT 2; HR 3; Ops 1. **Answer: HR 3, IT 2, Sales 1, Ops 1** (total 7, matching the 7 items).

**Q13** 🟡 Second largest distinct value.
Plain steps (one way): find the largest; then find the largest among the values that are **not equal** to it.
```text
GIVEN a list called numbers
SET largest_value TO the largest value in numbers (use the Example 1 steps)
SET second_largest TO nothing yet
FOR EACH number IN numbers
    IF number is NOT equal to largest_value THEN
        IF second_largest is nothing yet OR number > second_largest THEN
            SET second_largest TO number
        END IF
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW second_largest
```
Trace for 40, 90, 90, 75, 60: largest is 90. 40 becomes second (40); 90 skipped; 90 skipped; 75 beats 40 (75); 60 does not beat 75. **Answer: 75.** The trap is the duplicate 90: a method that just takes "the second item in sorted order" would wrongly give 90.

**Q14** 🟡 Remove duplicates, keep first.
```text
GIVEN a list called employee_ids
SET unique_ids TO an empty list
FOR EACH id IN employee_ids
    IF id is NOT in unique_ids THEN
        ADD id TO the end of unique_ids
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW unique_ids
```
Trace for E4, E7, E4, E9, E7, E2: E4 add; E7 add; E4 already there; E9 add; E7 already there; E2 add. **Answer: E4, E7, E9, E2.**

**Q15** 🔴 Reverse a list.
Plain steps: make an empty new list; take items from the **end** of the original, one at a time, back to the start, adding each to the new list.
```text
GIVEN a list called words
SET reversed_words TO an empty list
SET position TO number of items in words
WHILE position >= 1
    ADD the item at position in words TO the end of reversed_words
    SET position TO position - 1
END WHILE
SHOW reversed_words
```
Trace for Mon, Tue, Wed, Thu: position 4 adds Thu; 3 adds Wed; 2 adds Tue; 1 adds Mon; position 0 stops. **Answer: Thu, Wed, Tue, Mon.**

**Q16** 🔴 Highest day and days above average.
```text
GIVEN a list called daily_sales (not empty)

NOTE: pass 1 finds the total, the average and the best day
SET total_sales TO 0
SET best_sales TO the first value
SET best_day TO 1
SET day_number TO 0
FOR EACH sale IN daily_sales
    SET day_number TO day_number + 1
    SET total_sales TO total_sales + sale
    IF sale > best_sales THEN
        SET best_sales TO sale
        SET best_day TO day_number
    END IF
END FOR
SET average_sales TO total_sales / number of items in daily_sales

NOTE: pass 2 counts the days above the average
SET days_above_average TO 0
FOR EACH sale IN daily_sales
    IF sale > average_sales THEN
        SET days_above_average TO days_above_average + 1
    END IF
END FOR

SHOW best_day AND days_above_average
```
Trace: total = 220 + 310 + 180 + 400 + 260 = 1,370; average = 1,370 / 5 = 274. Best is 400 on **day 4**. Days above 274: 310 and 400, so **2 days**. (The average must be known before counting, which is why two passes are needed.)

**Q17** 🔴 Merge two sorted lists.
Plain steps: keep a pointer at the front of each list. Repeatedly compare the two items at the pointers; move the smaller to the result and advance that pointer. When one list runs out, copy over the rest of the other.
```text
GIVEN sorted lists first_list and second_list
SET merged_list TO an empty list
SET first_position TO 1
SET second_position TO 1

WHILE first_position <= size of first_list AND second_position <= size of second_list
    IF item at first_position in first_list <= item at second_position in second_list THEN
        ADD that first_list item TO merged_list
        SET first_position TO first_position + 1
    ELSE
        ADD that second_list item TO merged_list
        SET second_position TO second_position + 1
    END IF
END WHILE

NOTE: one list is finished; the rest of the other is already in order
ADD all remaining items of first_list TO merged_list
ADD all remaining items of second_list TO merged_list
SHOW merged_list
```
Trace for (2, 6, 11) and (3, 4, 15, 20): 2 vs 3 gives 2; 6 vs 3 gives 3; 6 vs 4 gives 4; 6 vs 15 gives 6; 11 vs 15 gives 11; first list is finished, so add 15, 20. **Answer: 2, 3, 4, 6, 11, 15, 20.**

**Q18** 🔴 Halving search.
Plain steps: keep a low and a high position (start 1 and 9). Take the middle position (round down). If the middle item is the target, stop. If it is smaller than the target, the target can only be in the upper half, so move low to middle + 1; if larger, move high to middle minus 1. If low passes high, the target is not there. Count each look as one guess.
```text
GIVEN a sorted list called sorted_numbers and a number called target
SET low TO 1
SET high TO number of items in sorted_numbers
SET guesses TO 0
WHILE low <= high
    SET middle TO the whole-number part of (low + high) / 2
    SET guesses TO guesses + 1
    IF item at middle = target THEN
        SHOW middle AND guesses
        STOP
    ELSE IF item at middle < target THEN
        SET low TO middle + 1
    ELSE
        SET high TO middle - 1
    END IF
END WHILE
SHOW "not found" AND guesses
```
Trace for **35**: low 1, high 9, middle 5 (21 is smaller than 35, low becomes 6); middle 7 (35, found). **Found at position 7 in 2 guesses.**
Trace for **10**: middle 5 (21 is larger, high 4); middle 2 (8 is smaller, low 3); middle 3 (12 is larger, high 2); low 3 is past high 2, stop. **Not found, 3 guesses.**
Trace for **3**: middle 5 (21 larger, high 4); middle 2 (8 larger, high 1); middle 1 (3, found). **Found at position 1 in 3 guesses.**

**Q19** 🔴 Reconcile with date and amount.
Plain steps: for each company entry, look for a bank entry with the same date **and** the same amount; if none, put it in "unmatched records". Do the same the other way for bank entries. Add up each side. Report totals and the difference.
```text
GIVEN a list called company_entries of (date, amount)
GIVEN a list called bank_entries of (date, amount)
SET unmatched_records TO an empty list
SET unmatched_bank TO an empty list

FOR EACH entry IN company_entries
    IF no bank entry has the same date AND the same amount THEN
        ADD entry TO unmatched_records
    END IF
END FOR

FOR EACH entry IN bank_entries
    IF no company entry has the same date AND the same amount THEN
        ADD entry TO unmatched_bank
    END IF
END FOR

SET total_records TO the sum of all amounts in company_entries
SET total_bank TO the sum of all amounts in bank_entries
SHOW unmatched_records AND unmatched_bank
SHOW total_records minus total_bank
```
Trace: records (5 Mar, 1,200) matches; (6 Mar, 450) has no match (unmatched); (7 Mar, 980) has no match, because the bank has 990 on that date (unmatched). Bank (5 Mar, 1,200) matches; (7 Mar, 990) no match; (8 Mar, 300) no match.
**Unmatched records:** (6 Mar, 450), (7 Mar, 980). **Unmatched bank:** (7 Mar, 990), (8 Mar, 300).
Totals: records 1,200 + 450 + 980 = 2,630; bank 1,200 + 990 + 300 = 2,490. **Difference = 140.** Cross-check: unmatched records total 450 + 980 = 1,430; unmatched bank total 990 + 300 = 1,290; 1,430 minus 1,290 = 140. The 10-rupee gap on 7 March (980 versus 990) is likely a typing error, but the procedure only reports it; it does not decide. (Edge case not covered by this sample: two identical entries on one side, which would need a rule such as "each bank entry can be matched only once".)

**Q20** 🔴 Longest run above target.
Plain steps: keep a current run length and a best run length, both 0. For each day: if sales are above the target, add 1 to the current run; otherwise reset the current run to 0. After each day, if the current run is longer than the best, update the best.
```text
GIVEN a list called daily_sales and a number called target
SET current_run TO 0
SET longest_run TO 0
FOR EACH sale IN daily_sales
    IF sale > target THEN
        SET current_run TO current_run + 1
    ELSE
        SET current_run TO 0
    END IF
    IF current_run > longest_run THEN
        SET longest_run TO current_run
    END IF
END FOR
SHOW longest_run
```
Trace for target 100 and 120, 90, 110, 130, 105, 80, 140, 150: 120 (run 1, best 1); 90 (run 0); 110 (run 1); 130 (run 2, best 2); 105 (run 3, best 3); 80 (run 0); 140 (run 1); 150 (run 2). **Answer: 3** (days 3 to 5).

## Chapter 33 answer key: Estimation (Fermi Problems)

All inputs are illustrative assumptions given in the questions. Results were recomputed by code.

### Self-check answers

1. 300 x 220 = 66,000 teas a year. (People x teas per person per day x days.)
2. Fix what is counted (cups sold, not made at home; stalls only or cafes too) and the place and time window (one city, one day). Also state the population assumption.
3. The paths differ by 400,000 / 50,000 = 8 times, far more than ordinary rounding. At least one assumption is wrong or a unit is mixed up. Compare the factors in each path, find the one that seems least believable, fix it, and recompute.

### Practice answers

**Q1.** Pages: 600 x 8 x 220 = 1,056,000. Reams: 1,056,000 / 500 = 2,112. Sanity: about 1 million pages for 600 people is about 1,760 pages per person a year, or 8 a day, which matches the input. About 1.06 million pages, about 2,100 reams.

**Q2.** Hours: 7.5 x 365 x 70 = 191,625 hours. Years: 191,625 / 8,760 (hours in a year, 24 x 365) = 21.875, about 22 years. Share: 7.5 / 24 = 31.25%, nearly a third. Sanity: a third of 70 is about 23, consistent.

**Q3.** Loaves: 120 x 6 x 50 = 36,000. Flour: 36,000 x 0.4 = 14,400 kg. Sanity: 14,400 kg is 14.4 tonnes, plausible for a year of daily baking at this scale.

**Q4.** Leave days: 250 x 1.5 x 12 = 4,500. Working days: 250 x 230 = 57,500. Share: 4,500 / 57,500 = 7.83%, about 8%. Sanity: 1.5 days a month is 18 a year, 18/230 = 7.8%, same answer through a per-person route.

**Q5.** 9 x 180 x 26 = 42,120. Per day: 9 x 180 = 1,620; 1,620 x 26 = 42,120. About 42,000 a month (gross takings, before costs).

**Q6.** Visits: 300 x 365 x 10 = 1,095,000. Claim 5,000,000 / 1,095,000 = 4.57, about 4.6 times too high. Even counting every visit (repeat visits included), the claim is not believable unless the shop was much busier than assumed.

**Q7.** 80 x 2 x 12 x 22 = 42,240 minutes. 42,240 / 60 = 704 hours. Sanity: 704 hours / 80 people = 8.8 hours each a month, about 24 minutes a day, matches 2 x 12.

**Q8.** Overall conversion: 0.20 x 0.25 x 0.80 = 0.04. Applications needed: 90 / 0.04 = 2,250. Check forward: 2,250 x 0.20 = 450 shortlisted; x 0.25 = 112.5 offers; x 0.80 = 90 hires. So about 2,250 applications.

**Q9.** Users in the rush: 600 / 2 = 300. One lift in 30 minutes does 30 / 2 = 15 round trips x 8 = 120 people. Lifts: 300 / 120 = 2.5, round up to 3 (you cannot have half a lift). Sanity: 3 lifts move 360, which is more than 300.

**Q10.** Cells: 3,000,000 x 15 = 45,000,000. Bytes: 45,000,000 x 10 = 450,000,000, which is 450 MB. Files: 3,000,000 / 1,048,576 = 2.86, round up to 3 files. Sanity: 3 files x 1,048,576 = 3,145,728, which covers 3,000,000.

**Q11.** Ordinary: 60 / 4 = 15 per hour x 8 = 120. Peak: 60 / 2 = 30 per hour x 2 = 60. Total 180 customers. Revenue 180 x 340 = 61,200. Sanity: 61,200 / 10 hours = 6,120 an hour.

**Q12.** Path A: 2,000,000 x 0.30 x 2 = 1,200,000. Path B: 2,000,000 / 500 = 4,000 stalls; 4,000 x 250 = 1,000,000. Ratio 1.2, well inside a factor of 2. Report about 1.0 to 1.2 million, best single number about 1.1 million.

**Q13.** Total checking: 1,200 x 3 = 3,600 minutes = 60 hours. Capacity: 2 x 6 = 12 hours a day. Days: 60 / 12 = 5 working days.

**Q14.** Low: 60 x 365 = 21,900. High: 100 x 365 = 36,500. Middle (80 a day): 80 x 365 = 29,200. Ratio high/low = 36,500 / 21,900 = 1.67. A range of 22,000 to 36,500 is a reasonable statement.

**Q15.** Central: 1,000 x 0.3 x 4 = 1,200. Lowest: 800 x 0.1 x 3 = 240. Highest: 1,200 x 0.5 x 5 = 3,000. Spread of each factor (high / low): A 1,200/800 = 1.5; B 0.5/0.1 = 5; C 5/3 = 1.67. B has the widest spread, so it drives the uncertainty; the overall high/low ratio is 3,000 / 240 = 12.5 (and 1.5 x 5 x 1.67 = 12.5). Improve B first (better data or a second path).

**Q16.** Active households: 1,000,000 x 0.20 = 200,000. Orders a day: 200,000 / 5 = 40,000. Peak orders: 40,000 x 0.55 = 22,000 over 4 hours = 5,500 an hour. Riders at peak: 5,500 / 2.5 = 2,200. Sanity: 2,200 riders x 2.5 = 5,500 an hour, matches. Note the hourly peak (5,500) is about twice the average over a 14-hour day (40,000 / 14 = 2,857, an extra assumption), which is why peak, not average, sets staffing.

**Q17.** Bytes per hour: 40,000 x 250 = 10,000,000 = 10 MB. Per day: 10 x 24 = 240 MB. Days: 50,000 / 240 = 208.3, about 208 days (roughly 7 months). Sanity: 208 days x 240 MB = 49,920 MB.

**Q18.** Leavers: 2,400 x 0.14 = 336. Cost: 336 x 300,000 = 100,800,000. One point lower is 1% of 2,400 = 24 fewer leavers; saving 24 x 300,000 = 7,200,000. The 300,000 per leaver is an exercise assumption, not a fact.

**Q19.** Maximum from registered users: 30,000 x 5 = 150,000. Remainder: 2,000,000 - 150,000 = 1,850,000, which is 1,850,000 / 2,000,000 = 92.5% of visits. So at least 92.5% must come from non-registered visitors (or automated traffic), or the report is wrong.

**Q20.** Meeting time: 10 x 1 x 800 x 48 = 384,000. Prep: 10 x 0.5 x 800 x 48 = 192,000. Total 576,000. New: meeting 8 x 0.75 x 800 x 48 = 230,400; prep 8 x 0.5 x 800 x 48 = 153,600; total 384,000. Saving 576,000 - 384,000 = 192,000 a year.
